---
description: "OMPdesk rules: the conversion of OMPdesk from Electron/TypeScript to Rust: target architecture, what stays TypeScript, the unit order, the native workspace and its build, staging, packaging and CI, the ledger of what has moved. Read before changing these files or porting anything to Rust."
globs:
  - "native/ompdesk/**"
  - "scripts/build-native.ts"
  - "scripts/cargo.ts"
---

## Rust conversion (`native/ompdesk/`)

- **The owner's order (11 Nov 2026)**: convert every part of OMPdesk that can be converted to Rust, for a lighter and faster app. It is a conversion order, not a study: a part is left in TypeScript only for a reason this file states. **A Rust unit alone never cuts a release** (the owner's rule, 12 Nov 2026, his choice among three: release now, wait for the whole conversion, or this): units land on `main` one by one, each leaving a working app, with no version bump of their own. Changes that are not Rust work release by the usual cadence (AGENTS.md › Change workflow), and such a release carries whatever Rust is on `main` by then. `gh workflow run release.yml --ref main -f dry_run=true` rehearses packaging for a unit that changes it.
- **What stays TypeScript, and why** (the owner chose both on 12 Nov 2026: the React page stays and only the main process and the shell move; omp stays the one installed on the machine, never bundled)
  - `resources/gui-bridge/**`: an omp extension, loaded by stock omp's Bun process with `-e`. omp is never forked, so its extension language is omp's.
  - The page (`src/renderer/**`, `src/shared/**` as the page uses it): the paired phone and another computer open the same UI in a browser over the web server, which only a web page can serve. The desktop window shows that same page; a second, native UI for the desktop would be a copy of 190 000 lines that the ui-parity rule forbids.
  - Tests and build scripts (`scripts/**`, `e2e/**`): tooling, not the app.
- **Target architecture**: a Rust process owns everything `src/main` does today (sidecars, bridge client, session index, control socket, web server and link, git, files, updater, memory service, terminals, preview) and a thin shell hosts the page. The page already reaches main only through `createOmpApi(transport)` (`src/shared/omp-api.ts`), so the transport is the seam: the same channel names and payloads, served by Rust.
- **Order** (a strangler on `main`, never a long-lived branch: other sessions keep changing `src/main`, and a branch would rot):
  1. The `ompdesk` CLI as a native binary. It carries the whole path once: cargo workspace, build script, staging, signing, packaging check, CI, notices. **Done** (ledger below).
  2. The core process `ompdesk-core` (crate `crates/core`: a host-agnostic library over any `AsyncRead`/`AsyncWrite`, plus its binary), spawned by Electron main on its first request with an open stdin pipe (it exits when the pipe closes, as every child here does). Main's subsystems move into it one at a time; for each one the logic is deleted from TypeScript in the same change (clean cutover) and what stays is its typed client: the exported functions other files call, now a request to the core, plus whatever needs Electron or must stay synchronous. **The subsystem's vitest file stays and runs against the real process**: it is the parity suite, so its cases are not rewritten for the port. Pure parts get Rust unit tests, with tables captured from the TypeScript before it is deleted. Order by independence from Electron: workspace files (done), git's read side (done), git's write side (done), worktrees and the rest of `local/git`'s reads (done), gh with the PR Center and the GitHub center (done), worktree care (branch standing, merge, orphan worktrees; done), disk care (sizes, the regenerable rules, saving and removing worktrees; done), the memory sampler (done), the session index's file work (done; list share stays TypeScript), the control socket server and router (done), worktree seeding (done; a worktree hook's script still runs in main and moves with setup's supervisor), the git that autopilot and the other local handlers still run through `exec.ts`, sidecar pool and RPC client, bridge client, web server and remote link, updater, setup.
     - **Wire** (`crates/core/src/wire.rs`, `src/main/core/process.ts`): one JSON object per line over stdio. Request `{id, method, params}`; response `{id, ok: true, result}` or `{id, ok: false, error: {code, message}}`; event `{event, data}`; the first frame is the event `ready` `{version, protocol: 1}`. A frame with `"bin": n` is followed by exactly n raw bytes that belong to it (file contents either way, never base64). Requests run concurrently and answer in completion order; one writer task, so frames never interleave. stdout carries frames only, stderr diagnostics. A number is read as its digits say, as `JSON.parse` reads it (serde_json's `float_roundtrip`, for the whole core crate): its default parse is best effort and returned one time in ten with sub-millisecond digits one bit off, and the core compares times with `==` (2g › Exact numbers). Base: tokio (later units bring sockets, child processes, timers and the HTTP server into this process).
     - **Client** (`CoreProcess`, `core()`): each request has a 30 s timeout (per-call override; a late answer is dropped); a process that exits rejects what is pending and starts again on the next request, at most three failed starts before a 30 s rest; `stop()` ends stdin and kills after 5 s, called in the quit teardown. `src/main/native-bin.ts` is the one place that knows where a native binary is (packaged `<resources>/ompdesk-bin/`, otherwise `resources/ompdesk-bin/<platform>-<arch>/`). `scripts/vitest-native.ts` (vitest `globalSetup`, node project) runs `ensureNative()` once per run, so every test that reaches the core finds the binary. `core.echo` is the plumbing tests' method and ships in the binary.
  3. The shell. Electron stays until a Rust shell passes the list below; the core process makes the swap small, because by then main is only windows, menus, tray, dialogs, the preview's views and the IPC relay.
  4. What the shell swap leaves in `src/preload` and `src/main` is deleted with it.
- **Batches** (the owner's call, 12 Nov 2026: "조금씩 작업하고 검사하면 하루종일하잖아"). The remaining units move in batches, not one unit per landing:
  - A batch is several units of one area (the batches of `docs/rust-remaining-units.md` §3.2) taken by one session on one branch (`omp/agent/rust-batch-<name>`), each unit still a clean cutover in its own commits. While building, run only what the unit at hand needs to compile and its own Rust tests; no per-unit full parity run, no mutation run, no formal restamp, no CI watch.
  - **The gate is before landing, once per batch**: push the branch and run `bun scripts/ci-run.ts ci --ref <branch> -f batch=true -f mac=true` (every job a push to main runs: static, check, build, `native` on all three OSes, `win-platform`, `mac-smoke`, `mac-native`, `memory`; the branch must be in `MAC_REFS`, which Main sets, and its macOS jobs take the one dispatch lane, e2e-ci-windows › CI › The check queue; pinned by `packaging-config.test.ts` and `ci-queue.test.ts`; runs before it, while the batch is fixed, leave out `-f mac=true`, AGENTS.md › Build and the agent loop). Every parity suite of the batch's units is in that run (each unit adds its parity files to `.github/actions/native-parity/action.yml` in the batch). Mutation checks and the formal restamp of every model a batch touches run once, on the branch, after the batch run is green. Only then does the batch land on `main` (rebase, push), and the branch, its worktree and local branch are deleted at once.
  - The session does not wait on the batch run: it starts the next batch on a new branch from its branch head and goes back to land the first when its run reports (`bun scripts/watch-run.ts <id>` as an async job). A red run is fixed on its branch and run again; nothing red lands.
  - The cost the owner accepted: a failure is found inside a larger change, so finding its cause takes longer.
- **The shell is chosen by measurement among three candidates** (the owner's call, 12 Nov 2026; facts read 11 Nov 2026, `tauri-runtime-cef` 3.0.0-alpha.7). The Preview panel needs Chromium (CDP from the host, input injection, capture, PDF, offscreen pages), which the system webview on macOS (WKWebView) does not give. The candidates:
  - (a) **CEF** for the whole app: Tauri 3's CEF runtime or the `cef` crate directly. Tauri 3 is alpha ("Keep production apps on 2.x") with open defects on paths OMPdesk uses: custom-scheme range requests (tauri#16479), a crash on title-bar drag (tauri#14936), no Windows title-bar overlay (tauri#9458), no Windows sandbox, transparent windows black (tauri#15718). A CEF shell is about Electron's size on disk, so its gain is the removed Node main process, not the download.
  - (b) **The system webview alone** (Tauri 2, stable): expected to fail on the Preview's needs above; it is dropped once the prototype shows which of them it cannot meet, with that evidence written here.
  - (c) **A system webview window plus a separate Chromium process for the Preview only**: the UI already runs in WebKit (a paired phone opens the same page in Safari, `docs/mobile-web.md`; e2e drives it in WebKit, `e2e/web-no-reload.e2e.ts`), and a phone already gets the Preview as a picture stream of an offscreen page (`web/preview-stream.ts`, `web/preview-input.ts`). Known costs: Chromium still ships or must be found on the device, so the download barely shrinks; the desktop e2e attaches Playwright over the window's debugging port, which a system webview has no equivalent of, so the desktop tests change; a streamed Preview may answer input more slowly (inferred, not measured).
  - **Chosen: (a), CEF through the `cef` crate** (batch P, 12 Nov 2026; measured on Windows, macOS by `native/shell-prototype/RUNSHEET-macos.md` on the prototype's branch; record: Ledger › P). On Windows every candidate passed the whole list and the update move; (b) is dropped because WKWebView gives no CDP, no Playwright attach and no trusted input on macOS; (c) loses the desktop Playwright attach on macOS, is the heaviest with the Preview open and the largest; (a) and (b) tie on memory. If the macOS run fails an item of (a), the fork goes to the owner. macOS (13 Nov 2026) confirms (a): every item passed but a physical title-bar drag, not tried; (c) loses the desktop attach, the page's drag regions and the traffic-light position there.
- The order (the owner's call of 12 Nov 2026 19:4x, "너무 느리다", to shorten the chain): the shell prototype runs beside the leaf batches B1 and B2 (it shares no file with them), and the first hub batch H1 is built on its branch beside the prototype. H1 landed on 12 Nov 2026 once the prototype had passed on Windows only, ahead of this rule's "once the prototype passed" (the main session's call, told to the owner: 9a-9c are the same core under every candidate). From here every hub batch still lands only after P has passed on both platforms, and S only after P's choice is final. The prototype runs (a) and (c), and (b) unless dropped, against one pass list on both platforms: the page loads from the custom scheme with video seek, a second webview in the window or its equivalent, CDP attach to the Preview, `capturePage`/input/PDF equivalents, the hidden title bar with drag regions, the tray, deep links, Playwright attached (over the debugging port or the candidate's replacement), plus resident memory and size on disk. The candidate that passes the list with the best measured memory and size is taken without asking the owner again; a fork only the owner can decide goes to him as a question.
- **People on the Electron app move by the ordinary automatic update** (the owner's rule, 12 Nov 2026): nobody downloads or installs again. It is on the prototype's pass list for every candidate, on both platforms, measured from the released Electron app of that day: its updater (`app-updater` rule › Install modes) finds the new version, installs it and relaunches into the new shell with nothing the person does. What the move must keep, each one checked in that run:
  - The update path itself: the same release asset names (`ompdesk-mac-arm64.dmg`, `ompdesk-windows-setup.exe`) and the `<!-- ompdesk-update … -->` metadata the Electron updater reads; macOS's relaunch swap (`stageBundleFromDmg`'s `codesign --verify`, bundle id and version checks, `SWAP_SCRIPT` with its rollback); Windows' NSIS install over the existing one. If the new shell cannot ship in that form, the last Electron release (the handoff release) learns the new form first, and only then does the new shell ship.
  - Identity: macOS the same bundle id `com.seuput.ompdesk`, signed with the same certificate (AGENTS.md › Signing: the designated requirement, so privacy grants survive) and replacing `/Applications/OMPdesk.app` in place; Windows the same app id, install folder and uninstall entry, so no second copy appears.
  - Data: the same userData folder (`ompdesk`), read as it is (prefs, tab layouts, sessions' snapshots, `deleted-sessions.json`, disk care and update state), and what Electron's `safeStorage` encrypted (the "ompdesk Safe Storage" keychain item on macOS, DPAPI on Windows: the GitHub update token, mail account passwords and the like) decrypted by the new shell, or each such secret asked for once again with the reason shown; never silently lost.
  - Failure: a move that fails leaves the Electron app installed and starting as before (the swap's rollback, the next launch's `swap result` log line).
  - Nothing asks the person: no keychain or credential dialog during or after the move (the owner, 12 Nov 2026: "이거 없이 작동해야"). macOS's "ompdesk Safe Storage" item trusts the app's designated requirement, so the new shell keeps bundle id and certificate and reads it silently; a dialog counts as a failed move. Tests never put such a dialog on a person's screen: their keychains are temporary, unlocked, never in the user's search list, and a step that could prompt runs on a CI runner.
- **Workspace** (`native/ompdesk/`): one cargo workspace, `crates/*`, toolchain pinned in `rust-toolchain.toml` (1.95, as `native/memory`), `Cargo.lock` committed, edition 2024. `unsafe_code = "deny"` for the workspace; a crate's one `sys.rs` may allow it for OS calls, each with a `// SAFETY:` line. `clippy::all` denied. Release profile: fat LTO, one codegen unit, stripped, `opt-level = "s"`, unwinding kept. Dependencies stay few and are named in the crate's `Cargo.toml` with the reason when not obvious; `native/memory` (the vendored ai-memory) is a separate workspace and is never mixed in.
- **Build and staging** (`scripts/build-native.ts`, `bun run build:native`): `cargo build --locked --release --workspace`, then each binary of `BINARIES` into the gitignored `resources/ompdesk-bin/<platform>-<arch>/` with `ompdesk.build.json` (each binary's SHA-256, the SHA-256 of the workspace's sources, rustc, when). `--check` holds the staged bytes to the record and the record to the sources; `--ensure` (and `ensureNative()` for a test) builds only when that check fails, so a run with a fresh staging never calls cargo. `bun run build` stays TypeScript-only (`out/` is one artifact for every OS); `bun run dev` builds native first. Rust is a development requirement (`scripts/cargo.ts` finds `cargo` on PATH or in `~/.cargo/bin`).
- **Packaging**: `extraResources` `resources/ompdesk-bin/<key>` → `ompdesk-bin` in both builder configs; `scripts/after-pack.cjs` `checkNative` fails the package unless every binary of `NATIVE_BINARIES` is there, executable and the bytes the record names. macOS signs it with the app (`scripts/mac-sign.cjs` signs every Mach-O in the bundle); Windows ships it unsigned like the app. Pinned by "native binaries" in `packaging-config.test.ts`.
- **CI**: `.github/actions/install` restores `resources/ompdesk-bin` from a cache keyed by the sources (`native/ompdesk/**`, `scripts/build-native.ts`, `scripts/cargo.ts`) per OS and architecture for every job that takes Electron; a miss installs the pinned toolchain, builds and saves; a hit only runs `--check`. Both `ci.yml` and `release.yml` run `native` on Linux, Windows and macOS arm64: `cargo fmt --check` on Linux once, `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` and `cargo test --workspace` on every OS, using `rust-toolchain.toml` and the native cargo cache. `mac-native` runs the real-binary vitest parity suites for the process, lock fence, session index, memory sampler, git changes/scm/worktree/gh, every `disk/*.test.ts` and the agent-control suites (`src/main/agent-control/*.test.ts`, against the real core; batch H3). Pull requests keep the Linux-only policy. There is no per-job path filter, so these jobs run on every eligible workflow run; docs-only and release-push skips stay unchanged.
  - The macOS parity list also runs `session-processes.test.ts` (3d: `KERN_PROCARGS2` and the kill on real processes), `control-socket/{server,router,events,cli}.test.ts`, `checkout-peers*.test.ts`, `git-fingerprints*.test.ts`, `ai-memory/freshness*.test.ts` and `routines/*.test.ts`, covering the control listener and the remaining git readers as their ports land.
- **Licenses**: the crates a binary links are listed in `LICENSES/ompdesk-native-dependencies.txt` (`bun scripts/gen-crate-notices.ts`, needs cargo and the crate sources), embedded in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.txt`; `bun run licenses:check` fails when `native/ompdesk/Cargo.lock` changed without regenerating.
- **A port keeps behaviour**: what a person, a script or the page can observe (output text, exit codes, wire frames, file formats and modes) is the same after a unit unless its ledger line names the difference. The TypeScript tests of the moved code are the specification; they become Rust tests of the same cases.

### Ledger

- **1. `ompdesk` CLI** (`crates/cli`, binary `ompdesk`; was `resources/cli/ompdesk.ts` run by Bun): same commands, flags, output and exit codes; it no longer needs Bun on the machine, so the launchers (`cli-install.ts`) run the binary by its absolute path. The wire contract moved to `src/shared/control-protocol.ts` (main's side) and `crates/cli/src/protocol.rs` (the CLI's); `CONTROL_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is spelled in both and `cli.test.ts` runs the built binary against main's own server.
  - Measured (11 Nov 2026): 743 invocations run through both programs against one fake server (every command and flag, stdin forms, number forms, error codes, events, the owner checks); 690 gave the same request bytes, stdout, stderr and exit code.
  - Differences kept on purpose: an interrupt during a command other than `events` ends the process at once (the TypeScript swallowed the first one); `events` stops when nobody reads its output (`ompdesk events | head`), where the TypeScript ran on; an ended `events` prints no second `OMPdesk closed the connection` line; the operating system's wording inside `OMPdesk is not running (…)`; a command named like a JavaScript object member (`toString`) is an unknown command.
  - Differences that only a fake server or hand-written JSON shows: numbers in `--json` output and in JSON a person passes (`call`, `app`, `--answers`, stdin) keep the spelling they came with (`1.0`, `1e3`, a 20-digit integer) where JavaScript rewrote them, and keys keep their order where JavaScript moved integer-like keys first; half a surrogate pair reads as U+FFFD (`json.rs`); JSON nested deeper than 128 levels is refused as input and, as an answer, ends the wait with `unavailable`.
  - **Checks** (`crates/checks`, a library linked into `ompdesk`; new, not a port): the CLI's local commands `run --heavy`, `heavy` and `check`, answered before the control commands and without a running OMPdesk. Rules: control-tools › Heavy slots and `ompdesk check`. This repository's `scripts/lock-slots.ts` takes its machine-wide `heavy` semaphore from the same pool (`ompdesk heavy hold`), one copy of the rule.
- **2a. Workspace files** (`crates/core/src/fs/`; was the logic of `src/main/workspace-fs.ts`): the Files panel's listing, text, binary, range and image reads, the plan file, the folder picker's level, stat and the panel's writes are `fs.*` methods of the core. Main keeps argument checks and path resolution with node's `path` (`resolveWithin`, `resolveReadPath`, both synchronous for their callers), MIME sniffing (`sniffMime`, one copy; the core sends the head bytes) and the move to the trash (Electron's; the core validates and answers what to trash). `workspace-fs.test.ts` runs its 43 cases unchanged through the process.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: listing order (`Intl.Collator(undefined, {numeric: true})` is ICU4X `icu_collator` with the locale main resolves; names ICU ranks equal fall back to byte order, as libuv's scandir gives them; `workspace-fs-order.test.ts` compares 50 awkward names in seven locales), the gitignore subset (a matcher over UTF-16 units; 76 pattern/path pairs captured from the TypeScript), node's error texts (`CODE: description, syscall 'path'`), text decoding at the byte cap (16 captured sequences), `mtimeMs` as node's double.
  - Differences: a call that takes over 30 s fails where the TypeScript waited; a crashed core is a failed call with a message and no code; JSON carries no infinite number, so `maxBytes: -Infinity` reads the whole file (or is `file_too_large`) and a write's `expectedMtimeMs: ±Infinity` is `invalid_path`; a lone surrogate in a path reads as U+FFFD; `readWorkspaceImage` reads a non-image under 25 MB whole before refusing it; a folder whose name is not UTF-8 is listed on POSIX; a save in a workspace that is a volume's root (`/`, `C:\`) works, where the TypeScript refused every one as escaping (`mutate.rs` `within` compares whole path components).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, this repository's root, five alternating runs, load 4.45 on 10 cores): the default listing (2 000 files) 5.6 ms → 5.4 ms, no measurable difference; the full listing (5 103 files) 21.8 ms → 19.6 ms. Both walk one folder at a time, which bounds them; the first request also starts the process (26 ms).
  - CI coverage: `win-platform` runs `workspace-fs.test.ts` against the real Windows binary, including the Win32 error table, reparse points, `\\?\` in messages, mtime and `same_file`; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes.
- **2b. Git's read side** (`crates/core/src/git/`; was `src/main/local/git/changes.ts` and the parsers only it used): the checkout root, `get_git_status`, `get_git_changes`, `get_git_fingerprint`, `get_git_diff` and the source-control head are `git.*` methods of the core. Main keeps how git runs: `gitRunner` (`exec.ts`) hands every request the binary (`resolveBin`/`developerGit`, cached there), the argument prefix, the whole environment (selectors dropped, login-shell PATH, `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`), the 60 s timeout and the 8 MiB cap, the rules main's `gitRaw` follows for the write side; the core only spawns and supervises (SIGTERM, SIGKILL 2 s later; `crates/core/src/sys.rs` is the crate's one unsafe file). Main also keeps the diff path's check (`isSafeRelativePath`), the repository-lock fence around each request (`currentStepSignal`, `trackEffect`) and the path guards its write side uses (`isInside`, the rest of `parse.ts`; `parseGitStatus` moved in 2h): the core reads porcelain v2 and lists it in v1's order. `changes.test.ts`, `git-env.test.ts`, `scm*.test.ts`, `git-fingerprints.test.ts` and `repo-lock-fence.test.ts` run through the process; the cases of `parseStatusV2`, `newFileDiff` and `parseBranchHeaders` are Rust tests.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: answers captured from the TypeScript on 12 folders (renames, copies, conflicts among changes, intent-to-add, type change, symlinks in and out, a non-UTF-8 index path, CRLF, binary, over 4 MiB, unborn, detached, rebase, upstream and stash, no repository, a missing folder) are equal but for the differences below; the timeout text (`-c timed out after 60000ms`), node's `spawn <bin> ENOENT`, git's stderr as the message.
  - Differences: a diff cut at 200 000 UTF-16 units through a surrogate pair ends in U+FFFD (the TypeScript kept the lone half); the fingerprint is a 16-hex SipHash, compared only with this app's own answers; containment is by whole components, so a changed file at the root named `..x` shows its diff; a read fails with `ompdesk-core …` when the core cannot answer and gets `rounds × 60 s` plus slack in all.
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, this repository, 24 changed files, five alternating runs, load about 5 on 10 cores): `getGitChanges` 41.0 → 40.8 ms median, `getGitStatus` 19.4 → 19.4 ms, no measurable difference. git bounds both: `status` alone 19.1 ms; the changes read's second wave runs seven gits at once, `diff --numstat` alone 24.6 ms; `gitRunner` and the core round trip with its 4 KB environment cost 0.1 ms each.
  - CI coverage: `win-platform` exercises `git.exe` spawn, `CREATE_NO_WINDOW`, case-insensitive environment names and `\\?\` real paths in the trusted-root check. `mac-native` runs `changes.test.ts` against the macOS core; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes.
- **2c. Git's write side** (`crates/core/src/git/scm.rs`; was the logic of `src/main/local/git/scm.ts` and `parseDecorations`, `remoteWebBase`): stage, unstage, discard, commit, undo, push/pull/fetch, stash, continue/abort, branches and branch actions, the log, a commit's files and diff, the history actions and the commit message's staged check are `git.*` methods, each one request that runs its whole sequence of gits in the core. Main keeps the checkout root and the repository lock around each mutation (`requireRoot`, `mutate`, `withRepoLock`), the checks of what the page sent (`checkPaths`/`isSafeRelativePath`, `checkRef`/`isValidRefName`, `checkSha`, an empty message, a reset mode) and the trim of typed text, discard's removal of the untracked paths the core answers (`fenced-fs`), the commit message's omp call with `parseGeneratedMessage`, and the timeouts (`slowMs` names a request's one slow git). Every core git request, reads included, goes through `coreGit` (`exec.ts`): a stopped lock step sends `{"cancel": id}` (`process.ts` → `server.rs`), the core ends the git it runs (SIGTERM, SIGKILL 2 s later) and starts no other, and answers only once every git of the request exited (each git's supervisor holds the request's `Scope`); main keeps that answer (`onEnded`, also after its own timeout, which now cancels too) as the step's tracked effect. The gits die with the core: on POSIX main spawns it detached (a process group and session of its own, never `unref`ed, still ending at stdin's end), and when it ends main SIGKILLs the group and settles what waited only once `kill(-pid, 0)` says the group is gone (`GROUP_GONE_MS` 3 s; past it, an error that says git processes of the dead core may still run, logged); on Windows the core joins a `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` job at start (`sys.rs`, which now also holds the crate's hand-declared kernel32 calls `CreateJobObjectW`, `SetInformationJobObject`, `AssignProcessToJobObject` and `GetCurrentProcess`; a failure is logged and the core runs on); `stop()`'s 5 s kill takes the group or the job. The core reads porcelain v2 for the conflict count and the discard (`parseGitStatus`'s last caller moved in 2h and the TypeScript parser was deleted); `git.head` is no longer a method. `scm.test.ts`, `scm-history.test.ts` and `repo-lock-fence.test.ts` run through the process; the cases of `parseDecorations` and `remoteWebBase` are Rust tests, with 32 URLs captured from the TypeScript. TLA+ model `formal/repo-lock-core` (`.omp/skills/repo-lock-core-verify`).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 139 steps captured from the TypeScript on deterministic repositories (every read and write, each refusal and its code, conflicts, a rejected push, a conflicting and a refused pull, a fetch from a gone remote, stash pop conflicts, history moves) give the same answers, messages, codes and repository states; a stopped holder's git is ended and the next holder starts after it exited (`repo-lock-fence.test.ts` "scm commit in the core…", which fails with the cancel removed); a core killed mid-request, or by `stop()`'s kill, takes its git with it before what waited settles and before the lock's next holder starts (`process.test.ts` "a core that ends while a request runs its git", a node stand-in for git so it runs on Windows too; it failed before the process group); the remote's web base by the WHATWG URL standard (`url` crate), as node's `URL`. Probed (`formal/repo-lock-core/evidence/session-probe.log`): in the core's own session a credential helper, `GIT_ASKPASS` and `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` answer as before.

- **2g. Disk care** (`crates/core/src/disk/{sizes,regenerable,facts,saved,tree}.rs`; was the walking, sizing, classifying, git and disk logic of `src/main/disk/tree-sizes.ts`, `regenerable.ts` (deleted), `worktree-facts.ts` and `saved-worktrees.ts`): the size walk and the reading kept in `<userData>/disk-care/sizes.json` (`disk.sizesOpen`, `disk.sizesBytes`, `disk.sizesSave`; the reading lives in the core between requests, keyed by an id `TreeSizes` holds, one per file), a worktree's files with the regenerable rule table (`disk.worktreeFiles`: status, untracked list, `ls-files`, `check-ignore -v`, the rules' reads, 64 MiB caps), its half-done operation (`disk.inProgress`), HEAD (`disk.head`), its last activity (`disk.lastActivity`, the 20 000-entry walk budget), the process folders (`disk.processFolders`: `lsof -d cwd` on macOS, `/proc` on Linux, none on Windows), dropping regenerable entries (`disk.dropArtifacts`: measured, then removed with node's three retries), a save's checks (`disk.saveCheck`: HEAD, the untracked commit size cap, `rewritesBytes`, the staged files with content; `disk.stagedBlob`: one staged file's content sent back in `bin` for main's secret check), its commits, branch and archive in one request (`disk.saveWork`: staged commit from a copy of the index, the working tree's commit from a scratch `GIT_INDEX_FILE`, `ompdesk/saved/<folder>-<day>` by `update-ref` against the zero sha, the private archive cloned where the volume can, 0700/0600, verified by a walk; the branch and archive taken back when the archive fails), its rollback (`disk.saveRollback`), the forced removal (`disk.forceRemove`), the landed archive and its settle (`disk.archive`, `disk.settleArchive`), a restore's checkout and files (`disk.restoreCheckout`, `disk.restoreFiles`) and the archive's removal (`disk.forgetArchive`). Each is one request: a git step through `coreGit`/`coreGitAnswer`, a disk step through the new `coreHeld` (`exec.ts`, `heldRequest` without a runner), so every one is held to the repository-lock step it is made in.
  - Added since (12 Nov 2026, machine-setup-updates › Disk care): `disk.clearCache` (`cache.rs`: the entries of a tool's cache that are the tool's own, Bun's `.pile` files or cacache's folders of a `_cacache`, the rest of the folder untouched; an entry that cannot go is counted and left). The table of caches, their bounds and the running-process rule are main's (`caches.ts`). `git.orphanCandidates` without a `root` answers every linked worktree of the repository, wherever it is, for disk care's build-output step. `disk.scratchFacts` and `disk.scratchSweep` (`scratch.rs`): when anything in a session's scratch folder last changed, and its removal but for the files its sessions' replies name (the session files are read in the core); which folder may go, and when, is main's (`disk/scratch.ts`).
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `DiskCare` (`care.ts`: the schedule, the run's order, the tally, `state.json`, the HTTP cache through Electron's `session.clearCache`, `bun pm cache rm` and its `ps` check, the IPC), `SavedWorktreeStore` (`saved.json` written by `writeFileAtomic` with `commitIf: () => !stepStalled()`, the record order, what the page lists), the secret check (`secretIn`/`looksSecret`: the central redactor is `resources/gui-bridge/redactor.ts`, one copy, read by memory and diagnostics too; the core sends each staged blob's bytes back instead), `stillSame` (main's process list and the save's reread), `processInside` (`realFolder` of main's open folders, the same form the removal claim compares), the removal claim, the open folders, `prepareWorktree` between a restore's checkout and its files (`worktree-seed.ts`, outside this unit), where things are (`diskParts`, `bunCacheDir`, stock's session folder of a cwd), and every pref the run reads. `TreeSizes`, `readWorktreeFiles`, `lastActivity`, `inProgressOperation`, `processWorkingFolders`, `dropArtifacts`, `saveAndRemoveWorktree`, `archiveBeforeRemove`, `restoreSavedWorktree` keep their signatures; `gitDirOf`, `classifyIgnored`, `readEvidence`, `RACY_MS`, `writeArchive`, `archiveEntries`, `archiveIgnoredData`, `settleArchive`, `SAVE_COMMIT_MAX_BYTES` and `ARCHIVE_MAX_BYTES` had no caller outside `src/main/disk/` and are gone from TypeScript; `worktreeHead` is new (care.ts's two `git rev-parse HEAD`). No caller outside `src/main/disk/` changed.
  - The lock's promise gained a clause, and its model with it. Under `withRepoLock` disk care's save made its scratch indexes, archive copies, rollback and forced removal on main's fenced files (`fenced-fs`: refused before a stopped step's call, a dispatched call waited for). They are now the core request's: `crates/core/src/disk/tree.rs` `live` refuses each file system call once the request is cancelled, every call runs on a worker that holds a clone of the request's scope, and the core answers only after it returned. `formal/repo-lock-core` models them (`DiskStarts`, `DiskEnds`, `CoreAnswers` waits for them) with two new frozen properties, `NoDiskAfterStop` (the next holder never runs while a file system call of the stopped request is in flight) and `NoDiskAfterCancel` (a cancelled request starts none), and the mutations `answer-before-disk-call-returns` and `disk-call-ignores-cancel`; `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core`: 7 of 7 runs as expected (code runs 6 088 and 9 923 states), 13 of 13 mutations detected, every `@code` tag resolves, DRIFT on the nine changed or new covered items (to be stamped after the commit). `saved.json`'s write stays in main with both its properties (atomic replace with the Windows rename retry, never after a stop); the core's own atomic write (`tree.rs` `write_atomic`, `sizes.json`) has the same two (the `atomic-file.ts` backoff, the cancel asked right before every rename; Rust test `replaces_a_file_whole_and_never_after_a_cancel`). `repo-lock-fence.test.ts` passes unchanged (19 cases).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: `classifyIgnored` over 273 scenarios captured from the TypeScript (every build tool and its scripts, configs literal, computed, missing and untracked, out flags, manifests that are not objects, JavaScript's key order, monorepo ownership, lockfiles, `__pycache__`, `CACHEDIR.TAG`, 23 `.gitignore` sections; `regenerable-captured.json`), git's status, `check-ignore -v` and the TypeScript's reading of them on three worktrees (`status-captured.json`), the walk on built trees (blocks as `du -sk` counts on APFS, a hard link once, a sparse file's blocks, a link to a folder and a file outside the tree counted as the link alone, a missing target 0, a vanished entry 0, a non-UTF-8 name where the file system holds one, a cancelled walk that reports nothing, a reading reused, kept and overdue, a `sizes.json` the TypeScript wrote), removal without following a link and of a read-only file, the archive written privately, verified, settled and restored over a hook's default, and a cancelled archive that writes nothing. On deterministic folders the exported reads answer as before (33 steps: files of a project with renames, staged, unicode, tab and space names, every rule, a linked worktree, a rebase, an unborn repository, a plain folder, a missing one; operations; nine activity stamps and the over-budget walk; sizes of a tree, a file, a link, a sparse file; a drop and what it left): 29 equal, 4 differ only in the error's class. The six `disk/disk-care*.test.ts`, `tree-sizes.test.ts`, `repo-lock-fence.test.ts`, `user-folder-fs.test.ts`, `worktree-orphans*.test.ts`, `branches-worktrees.test.ts` and `host-services.test.ts` run through the process unchanged.
  - Differences: a git failure or a file system failure of a moved read or step is a `CoreError` with the TypeScript's message (code `git_exit`, `spawn_failed` where node's error carried `ENOENT`, `io` with node's text) where it was a `GitCommandError`, node's error or a plain `Error`; a refusal to save is the fault `keep_worktree`, turned back into a `KeepWorktree` (`saveStep`); `path_exists` is still the `LocalCommandError` the page reads. A name that is not UTF-8 on POSIX is measured, walked, archived and timed by its bytes where node's lossy name failed its `lstat` (counted 0, its folder unread); a folder holding such a child is never reused from `sizes.json` (`x`), and the archive walk still fails on one, as node's did. `sizes.json` keeps a 64-bit inode exactly (the TypeScript rounded one past 2^53) and is written by the core. A reading the core lost (the core started again between `load()` and `save()`) fails the usage step with `unknown_reading` where the TypeScript had nothing to lose. The walk keeps at most 4 file system calls in flight across every walk of the process (`WALK_THREADS`), where the TypeScript allowed 16 per `TreeSizes` (on libuv's pool of 4 threads, so 4 ran at once then too). Deadlines where the TypeScript had none: a walk 60 min, a drop 30 min, an archive copy 10 min, the readings 60 s (each plus the core slack). `lsof` past 30 s, past 64 MiB or exiting non-zero with no output reads as "cannot tell" (every caller keeps the worktree) where node handed on the partial stdout of a timeout or a full buffer; a stopped step's `lsof` is asked to end (SIGTERM) and killed 2 s later where main sent SIGKILL at once. Stock's session files of a worktree are read by their bytes where node read lossy names. A copy is `std::fs::copy` (clonefile on APFS, `copy_file_range` on Linux, `CopyFileExW` on Windows) where node asked `COPYFILE_FICLONE`. A removal that fails on a busy, refused or not-empty path (`EBUSY`, `EPERM`, `ENOTEMPTY`, `EMFILE`, `ENFILE`) starts the whole tree again, three times after 100, 200 and 300 ms (`tree.rs` `remove`), where node's `rm` retried each failing path; a folder that vanishes between its `lstat` and its listing fails the removal (`scandir … ENOENT`) where node passed it as gone. A `CACHEDIR.TAG` that is a folder reads as no tag (the entry is judged by the next rules), where the TypeScript's read of it threw and the worktree was skipped for the run. A drop stopped by its 30-minute limit or a cancel leaves the entries it had not reached, and that one in part, with one `failed` line for the worktree. Every relative entry a request carries (a drop's, an archive's and its overwrites, a save's excluded and untracked files, the activity's stamps and ignored entries, and an archive's manifest on restore and settle) must be a path inside its folder: an empty, absolute or `..` entry, `.` alone, a NUL, and on Windows a drive, UNC or stream prefix or `\`-separated `..`, refuses the whole request as `invalid_entry` before anything is touched (Rust test `refuses_a_drop_or_an_archive_that_leaves_the_worktree_and_takes_nothing`); the TypeScript joined them unchecked (main never sent such an entry; the check holds the process boundary). A save's staged files are read one request each (`disk.stagedBlob`), stopping at the first that holds a secret, as the TypeScript read them; a save whose archive or archive worker fails after the saved branch exists takes the branch and the archive back on every such path (Rust test `a_save_whose_archive_fails_takes_its_branch_back`). A run whose core cannot list the processes keeps every worktree ("process check unavailable"), one whose core cannot open the kept measurement measures by full walks, and the daily run comes due again after a run that failed (`disk-care.test.ts`, three cases that fail without it).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, this Mac: 10 cores, 16 GB; warm cache, `du -sk` once before each target; seven alternating runs per side, timed around the walk only: `TreeSizes.bytes` in plain node 24 against `disk.sizesBytes` from its request line to its answer; same bytes and the same lstat/readdir counts on both sides every run): this repository's `node_modules` (41 867 lstat, 3 326 readdir, 1 028 853 760 bytes, on the external volume `/Volumes/BACKUP`) 535.5 ms (range 434.9–9 700.7) → 140.0 ms (106.5–193.8); `~/.omp/wt` (587 lstat, 6 readdir, 17 199 104 bytes, internal disk) 10.4 ms (6.9–116.8) → 2.5 ms (1.8–4.9). Load 5.5 before and 32.2 after, from work outside this unit that started during the runs (not identified); the outliers fall in it. The bound: the core's walk at 1, 2, 4 and 8 threads over `node_modules`, seven alternating rounds at load 4.8 → 3.9: 202.5, 130.1, 79.9 and 74.0 ms median (8 threads within 4's spread of 52.4–138.4), so 4 is where it stops getting faster and it is the size of libuv's pool main walked on. The TypeScript is bound by its per-call promise work on main's thread, the core by the file system calls themselves. The end-to-end run on this machine is dominated by git, not the walk (`~/.omp/wt` holds 17 MB here; the user's 924 219 files were measured 20 Oct 2026 on another Mac and are not re-measured).
  - Windows verification (12 Nov 2026, temp folders only): junctions and directory symlinks are removed as links without touching their targets; read-only files and paths longer than 260 characters are removed; directory/file links and the read-only bit round-trip through the archive and restore; drive, UNC, stream and backslash-parent entries refuse the whole drop/archive before its good entry is touched. Windows `Metadata::len` reported 0 for a link where node counted its target text: `sys.rs` `file_link_size` now reads `FSCTL_GET_REPARSE_POINT` from the entry's own handle (`FILE_FLAG_OPEN_REPARSE_POINT | FILE_FLAG_BACKUP_SEMANTICS`) and counts UTF-8 bytes as libuv does, stripping only the implicit NT prefix, not an explicit Win32 namespace. The Windows Rust test compares six links with this machine's node: junction, directory/file symlinks, a missing Unicode target, verbatim drive and UNC targets; no link is descended. A failed identity, metadata or reparse read still counts 0 and never fails the size walk. Only the HTTP cache's 256 MB and Bun cache's 2 GB thresholds use the walked sizes to decide a removal (worktree measurements are tally/display); the save's 256 MB and archive's 512 MB limits read each file's size separately. The shared entry-size helper keeps their link sizes, archive manifests and settle checks consistent too. A real-core smoke with a handle denying `FILE_SHARE_DELETE` kept the old sizes file whole, then completed its atomic replace after the handle closed. The reuse test's POSIX-only directory open failed AccessDenied on Windows; its setup now opens with `FILE_WRITE_ATTRIBUTES` and `FILE_FLAG_BACKUP_SEMANTICS`. `measures_names_that_are_not_utf8_and_reads_their_folder_again` still runs only where the filesystem holds such names (Linux; APFS refuses them). `e2e/disk-care-asar.e2e.ts` passed on Windows: with the real Electron app still running, both landed worktrees holding `.asar` files disappeared and other measured folders' archives remained deletable. It now guards the whole IPC-to-core path and main's remaining file calls, rather than a walk inside Electron.
  - Exact numbers (found by CI's Linux `native` job after landing: the reuse test failed with all three folders read again): a directory's time written to `sizes.json` came back from serde_json's default float parse one bit off for one time in ten with sub-millisecond digits (102 of 1 001 tried), so that directory was read again on every run where the TypeScript's `JSON.parse` kept it; the test's three folders share one time, so it failed whole about one run in ten, on any OS. The core crate now builds serde_json with `float_roundtrip`. Rust tests, 1 001 times each: `keeps_a_directory_time_through_the_kept_file_to_the_bit` (through `sizes.json`; failed before in every build) and `reads_the_time_main_sends_to_the_bit` (2a's `expectedMtimeMs` through the request's params; failed before with the core crate built alone, 102 of 1 001, and passed in the workspace build only because the CLI's `arbitrary_precision` there keeps a `Value`'s digits, so the staged binary's editor write was not affected). No new crate: `Cargo.lock` and the notices are unchanged.
- **2h. The remaining independent git readers** (`crates/core/src/git/{checkout,freshness,triggers}.rs`): shared-checkout path presence and submodule reads (`git.checkoutStatus`, was `checkout-peers.ts`), the folders a fingerprint watches (`git.checkoutDirs`, was `git-fingerprints.ts`), memory freshness's checkout/ref/root discovery (`git.freshnessCheckout`) and each page's path/symbol verdict (`git.freshnessVerdict`, was `ai-memory/freshness.ts`), a routine's branch head and commit range (`git.routineHead`, `git.routineCommits`, was `routines/ports.ts`), and the local Autopilot branch comparison (`git.autopilotBranchStatus`, was `local/autopilot.ts`) run their whole git sequences through `coreGit`. The porcelain-v1 parser, path containment and NFC comparisons used for checkout presence moved together; `parseGitStatus` and `GitStatusEntry` were deleted from TypeScript. The core answers only the requested paths still held, not the potentially large porcelain text. `icu_normalizer` is a direct dependency at its already locked version for the same NFC operation as `String.prototype.normalize("NFC")`.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: the checkout coordinator's live session and claim state (`judgeClaim`, fresh bridge answers, edit stamps, committed claims and retire messages); the fingerprint's shared watcher/cache lifetime; the routine's timer and baseline; local Autopilot's command validation and bridge forwarding. Memory keeps its client calls, page-by-page limit and retirement/feedback actions, and `namedItems` (Markdown extraction, not git output; it moves with memory management). The freshness cache and pickaxe budget are an opaque serialized state main passes from one verdict to the next; only Rust creates, reads and updates it. No reader handle or core-side lifetime was added.
  - Preserved limits and exit meanings: checkout status is literal pathspecs in chunks of 6 000 UTF-16 units, each git 5 s and 16 MiB, a failed or cut status is unknown and retains claims; `-z` paths keep embedded newlines, invalid UTF-8 is decoded as node did. The shared runner keeps stderr to 64 KiB and the environment from `gitRunner`, including `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0` and no ambient repository selectors. Freshness keeps its 20 s gits and 8 MiB default stdout cap, `cat-file -e` existence, `grep -q` exit 1 as absence, other grep exits as unknown, and at most eight `log -S` runs per check. A routine's quiet `rev-parse` exit 1 is a missing branch, not an error. Main's deadlines are rounds × 60 s plus 7 s (checkout/dirs/head: one round; range: two), branch comparison 30 s plus 7 s; freshness uses (unique folders + 6) × 20 s for checkout discovery and four 20 s rounds for a page with paths, two for symbols only, plus 7 s.
  - Differences: checkout and routine git failures are `CoreError` faults instead of ordinary `Error`/`GitCommandError`, with the same git stderr; their consumers already handle failure. A failed core verdict request ends that check with one page error: its consumed pickaxe budget is unknown, so no later page can spend it again; TypeScript swallowed individual git failures. Freshness's eight pickaxes are assigned to absent symbols in page order, where simultaneous greps previously spent the budget in completion order; the cache, page order and `maxChanges` early stop remain. The serialized freshness state crosses the pipe per page instead of living only in main. Simultaneous checkout chunks are all joined before failure is answered, instead of `Promise.all` rejecting at the first failure. Hand-written non-git count output uses Rust's floating-point parser rather than JavaScript `Number` (git prints decimal integers). No speed measurement.
  - Verified on Windows: the captured checkout and freshness parser/verdict tables and their real-git Rust tests; `cargo fmt --check`, `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` for the host, `aarch64-apple-darwin` and `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, and `cargo test --workspace` (181 passed on the tree rebased over 2g, 3a and 4a). The staged release core serves `checkout-peers.test.ts`, `checkout-claims.test.ts`, `git-fingerprints.test.ts`, `ai-memory/{freshness,care}.test.ts`, `routines/git.test.ts`, `local/autopilot.test.ts`, `local/git/{parse,git-env}.test.ts` and `repo-lock-fence.test.ts`; the routine suite now waits on the real read promises rather than a one-second polling deadline. Eight captured routine/head/branch-range answers match the core on the same repository. `licenses:check` passes. macOS and Linux runtime paths have not run here, only their clippy builds (CI's `native` job runs the Rust tests on all three OSes); the existing POSIX-only fence tests still skip on Windows.
- **2i. Worktree seeding** (`crates/core/src/git/seed.rs`, a file's clone and Windows' junction in `sys.rs`; was all of `src/main/local/git/worktree-seed.ts` but the run of a hook's script): the seed of a fresh worktree from the main checkout's `.worktreeinclude` is one request (`git.worktreeSeed`) through `coreGit`: the list's lines (`includePattern`), the glob (`globRegex`), what the patterns name (a plain path that exists; for globs `ls-files -z --others --ignored --exclude-standard --directory`), `check-ignore` 100 paths at a time, at the top and again for every folder's entries, the walk in JavaScript's sort order, the copy that clones where the volume can, links recreated or copied, links written with an absolute path pointed at the worktree's own copy, the file and byte budgets and every skip note. A hook's lookup is a request too (`git.worktreeHook`: the worktree's own script before the main checkout's, the log in the worktree's git dir). `worktree-seed.ts` is the client: `seedWorktree`, `runWorktreeHook`, `prepareWorktree`, `describePreparation`, the script names and the budgets other files import. 2d and 2f name the seed among what main keeps; from here only the hook's run is.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: **the run of a hook's script** (`runWorktreeHook`: `spawnSupervised`, the timeout, the output's tail, the log's write), decided from the code. A hook is a shell whose children must end with it at its timeout (`bun install` under `/bin/sh`; a `.cmd` shim's `bun` on Windows), and it must never outlive the app. The core's runner (`run.rs`) ends the one process it started (SIGTERM to its pid, `TerminateProcess` on Windows). On Windows the core can end a whole tree since 4b (`sys::ProcessTree`, a job per process). On POSIX it cannot do both at once: its children end with a dead core only because they stay in the core's process group, which main kills (`process.ts` `groupGone`, the repository lock's model), and ending a hook's whole tree at a timeout takes a group of the hook's own, which leaves that one; 4b's ssh accepts that what ssh started ends later, a hook's install must not run on. The setup supervisor has both through a process of its own beside each command, which reads its stdin and kills the command's group when the pipe closes. It is `src/main/setup/supervisor.ts`, the setup unit's; the hook's run moves with it, on both systems at once (done in 6a: the hook runs under `ompdesk-core --supervise`). A hook in the core would also end with every restart of the core, two minutes into an install. The three properties hold as before, by the same code: a hook never outlives the app (the supervisor's stdin pipe), its whole tree is killed at the timeout, its log holds stdout and stderr in arrival order. `describePreparation` stays too (a sentence for the agent's notes and Autopilot's log).
  - The seed writes only in the new worktree and only reads the main checkout. In `seed.rs` every call that creates, links or removes takes a `Place`, and only `Worktree::place` makes one, after it checked the path: 2g's entry check (`disk/tree.rs` `relative_entries`: no `..`, no absolute path, on Windows no `:`), on Windows no part ending in `.` or a space, its nearest existing folder a real folder whose real path is the worktree or inside it. The file calls are 2g's too, not copies (`tree::make_dirs`, `copy`, `symlink`, `read_link`, `remove`, `entry_size`, `under`, and `live` before each; `tree` is `pub(crate)` for it); only the junction is new (`sys.rs` `junction`: `tree::symlink` makes a symlink on Windows, which needs a privilege the seed must not). Rust test `writes_only_in_the_worktree_and_leaves_everything_else_as_it_was`: patterns with `..` (both separators), an absolute path, a link in the main checkout to a folder outside it (a junction on Windows) as the match, as a match's parent and inside a copied folder, a file link out (POSIX), and a worktree folder that is itself a link out, at one and at two levels; after the seed everything outside the worktree (the main checkout but its `.git`, the folder outside) is byte for byte and time for time what it was. It fails with the real-path check or the link check removed (both tried).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 97 list lines, 3 496 glob/path pairs (46 globs over 76 paths: `**` at every place, `?` over a surrogate pair, line ends the expression's `.` skips, regular-expression characters taken literally) and 4 sort lists captured from the TypeScript (`git/seed-captured.json`); 15 seeds on deterministic repositories on Windows gave the same report and the same worktree (every entry, each link's text, every file's bytes) before and after: the list with a BOM, CRLF and every refusal, no list, a list that is a folder, one over 64 KiB, 230 patterns (200 read), globs with Korean and spaces, force-added files in an ignored folder and a nested `.git`, files the worktree already has, junctions in, out, to the checkout itself and looping, a destination behind a link out, the byte budget at its edge and a first file over it, odd names, a partly tracked folder, file links. `worktree-seed.test.ts` runs through the process; its `includePattern` case moved to Rust with the function; its three volume stand-ins are `pretendClones` (a count: that many files clone, then the volume refuses) where they were a JavaScript `copyFile`; the file-link case asserts the real platform's behaviour where it faked `process.platform`; one case is new (a `.cmd` hook on Windows, in a folder with a space).
  - Differences: a seed may start new entries for 60 s (`SEED_BUDGET_MS`, sent with the request) and skips the rest as `budget`; the TypeScript ran on, past `worktree_create`'s answer. macOS clones with `clonefile` itself (was a `/bin/cp -c` process per file after a `df -T apfs` probe and a same-device check), so a file the volume cannot clone always falls to the plain copy and its byte budget. On Windows a path with a `:` part or a part ending in `.` or a space is skipped as `destination_outside`: std's path calls read `secret.` as `secret` and `a::$DATA` as `a`, where node's `\\?\` paths took such a name literally and found nothing. A name with half a surrogate pair reads U+FFFD and is not found. The hook's lookup needs git even where no script exists (it ran git only after finding one). A request that fails as a whole reports the core's text in the seed's note (`ompdesk-core did not answer git.worktreeSeed in time`) where the TypeScript could not fail that way.
  - The lock's promise is unchanged: `prepareWorktree` has no caller under `withRepoLock` (`createWorktree` calls it after the lock's step, a restore and Autopilot's land check hold none), so the seed is never a held step's request; made in one, `coreGit` would hold it like any other (the walk asks before each git and each file call and answers `cancelled`; Rust test `a_cancelled_seed_makes_no_further_file`). `sys.rs` is a covered file of `formal/repo-lock-core` and gained `clone_file` and `junction`, nothing the model reads; `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core` passes its runs and its mutations.
  - Not measured: speed (git bounds a seed: one `check-ignore` per folder).
  - CI coverage: `check` runs `worktree-seed.test.ts` on Linux with every hook case; `win-platform` runs its seed cases, the file-link copy and the `.cmd` hook. `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes: the junction and the Windows path rules on Windows, `clonefile` on macOS (`clones_past_the_byte_budget_where_the_volume_clones` requires a clone there), `FICLONE`'s refusal on Linux's ext4. `mac-native`'s list does not name `worktree-seed.test.ts` yet; until it does, the file's macOS run is nobody's.
- **3b. Memory sampler** (`crates/core/src/memory/`, Windows' OS calls in `sys.rs`; was the reads and rules of `src/main/memory-sampler.ts` and the stock helper's `resources/gui-bridge/stock-helper/process-memory.ts`): the OS's reading (`memory.reading`) and every process with its parent and resident bytes (`memory.processes`) are core methods. Windows calls the OS directly: `GlobalMemoryStatusEx` (was Electron's `process.getSystemMemoryInfo()`, which reads the same call; `app.getAppMetrics` was never used here) and a Toolhelp32 snapshot with `OpenProcess` and `K32GetProcessMemoryInfo` per process (was the same calls through bun's FFI in the stock helper's terminals worker; `processes.memory` and its file are deleted). macOS keeps the two programs the TypeScript ran, `/usr/sbin/sysctl -n kern.memorystatus_vm_pressure_level kern.memorystatus_level hw.memsize` and `/bin/ps -axo pid=,ppid=,rss=`, through the core's shared runner (`run.rs`) with main's environment and folder. The port originally kept subprocesses because macOS had no Rust or vitest CI; `mac-native` now exercises both real reads. Linux (the CI's vitest) reads `/proc/meminfo` `MemTotal`/`MemFree` (what Electron answered there) and runs `ps`.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: the watch (`memory-watch.ts`: the 5 s timer, one sample at a time, the release and its log line) and idle release (`idle-release.ts`) are rules over main's session state; `treeBytes`, which idle release calls synchronously over a sample's rows; `testMemoryReading`, a test launch's file read; `SAMPLE_TIMEOUT_MS`, which main sends with each request (how long `sysctl` or `ps` may run) and applies to the request itself. `memory-sampler.ts` is the client: a failed, refused or late answer is `null`, as `within` made it.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 91 rows captured from the TypeScript (`memory/captured.json`: 33 `sysctl` outputs with levels 1/2/4 and unknown, a percentage below 0 and above 100, the `Math.round`, missing and extra values, CRLF; 27 `ps` listings with blank and short lines, tabs, leading zeros, a 32-bit pid; 31 total/available pairs around the 5 % and 10 % shares) give the same readings and rows. `memory-sampler.test.ts` reads this computer through the real core: the RAM node reports, this process under its parent with its image name on Windows, a child it starts, resident bytes within a factor of node's own; its `parseMacSysctl`, `windowsReading` and `parsePs` cases moved to Rust with their functions and `treeBytes`'s stay.
  - Differences: a value that is not decimal digits (`1e3`, `0x10`, `1.5`, `+5`; 21 captured rows) is no reading and no row, where `Number()` read it; neither program prints one. A pid past 2^32 or a size past 2^64 bytes is no row. Windows lists processes whether or not omp and Bun are installed (the stock helper refused while omp was missing, so nothing was released then), and the watch's first sample starts the core instead of the terminals worker. An image name with half a surrogate pair reads U+FFFD. `ps` and `sysctl` get no environment variable whose name or value is not UTF-8. A `ps` or `sysctl` that ignores the SIGTERM of its 4 s timeout is killed 2 s later (`execFile` asked once).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, 16 cores at about 49 % load from other sessions' builds, 464 processes, 7 alternating rounds of 100 samples after 20 warm ones, no failed sample; node's `os.totalmem()`/`os.freemem()` stood in for Electron's reading, the same OS call): one sample 13.8 ms before (13.2 to 14.7) and 13.8 ms after (12.7 to 14.2), no measurable difference; the serving process's CPU per sample 13.6 ms (bun) and 15.4 ms (core), ranges overlapping, three rounds of 500. Windows bounds both: the Toolhelp32 snapshot alone takes about 8 ms of it and the 280 processes this user may open about 5 ms (the same calls timed through bun's FFI), so at one sample per 5 s the watch costs about 0.3 % of a core either way. What changed is which process serves it: the watch no longer starts the terminals worker (a Bun process, 56 MB working set after the run) at launch; the core held 10 MB. macOS was not measured: it still starts `sysctl` and `ps` per sample, now from the core.
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `memory-sampler.test.ts` against the real macOS core, asserting the `sysctl` reading and `ps` rows. `check` exercises Linux's `/proc/meminfo` and `ps`; `win-platform` exercises the Windows OS calls. `native` runs the parsers and captured rows as Rust tests on all three OSes.
- **3c. The session index's file work** (`crates/core/src/sessions/`; was the watch, the settle, the header read and the row rules of `src/main/session-index.ts`): the one recursive watch of the sessions tree with its 300 ms settle, its wait for a root that is not there yet (looked for every second) and its change reports (`sessions.watch`; events `sessions.watching` `{index, watching, missed}` and `sessions.changed` `{index, names}`, `names: null` when anything may have changed), the legacy chat flag read from a session file's first 4 096 bytes once per session id (`sessions.listed`, kept per index; `sessions.kind` reads one file now), and which rows a list keeps, the first of each session id (`sessions.listed`). Main names each index and drops it with `sessions.close`. It is the first unit whose work outlives its request: a handler is given `Events` (`server.rs`), which holds the output only weakly, so a watch left running never keeps the core from ending with its input (Rust test `a_watch_reports_as_events_and_ends_with_the_connection`).
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: the list itself (stock's `listAllSessions` through the stock helper, never re-derived); the tombstones (`deleted-sessions.ts`, another unit's); the list's order and the numbered shared copy with its diff (`SessionListShare`: `compareSessionRows` and `diffSessionList` are `src/shared` code the page runs too, and a Rust copy would be a second definition, so the core is handed the rows already in order and numbers nothing); a window's folder filter (`path.resolve` over the shared copy, per ask, no disk); the GUI's own chat labels (`SessionKindStore`, a pref); the 2 s warm-up re-read (it acts on the shared copy); content search with its text cache (`session-snippet.ts`, `session-cache.ts`) and the journal's pace (`session-pace.ts`), which are other files' logic and move with them. `sessionHasContent` is the page's (`src/renderer/lib/session-title.ts`) and was never main's.
  - The watch, by system (`sessions/source.rs`): Windows reads `ReadDirectoryChangesW` itself (`sys.rs` `DirChanges`: one overlapped read on the root, a 64 KiB buffer, a stop event; `open` makes the first read, and the system keeps what changes between two reads). macOS uses the `notify` crate's FSEvents backend (CC0-1.0; one `FSEventStream` for the one path, file events, no latency, `MustScanSubDirs` reported as a rescan; read in notify 8.2.2's `fsevent.rs`), because FSEvents is CoreFoundation calls, a run loop thread and a C callback that no Windows machine can run. notify is not used on Windows: its backend there reports nothing of a buffer overflow and silently stops watching a deleted root (8.2.2's `windows.rs`). Linux, where the core is built only for the tests CI runs there, reads the three levels the index looks at every 300 ms and compares.
  - No change is lost, and what shows it. A change is named in a report, or it is covered by a report that anything may have changed, after which whoever lists reads again. Named: every change after the watch began (Windows: after `DirChanges::open` returned; Rust test `windows_names_every_change_after_open_or_says_it_dropped_them`, with a 1 KiB buffer so the burst overflows). Covered: what the system dropped (Windows' 0-byte read or `ERROR_NOTIFY_ENUM_DIR`, FSEvents' rescan); what was written before a watch that had to wait for its root or that started again (`missed`); what had not settled when a watch ended by itself (main reports the end as a change); what was written while no core ran (`SessionIndex` asks the next core for the watch at its `ready`, or at the next ask for the list, and reports a change once it is watched). Cases, each in `session-index.test.ts` against the real core and as a Rust test in `watch.rs`: a session file renamed in place (both names), a project folder renamed with its sessions, a deleted project folder (it and the sessions in it), a burst of 1 000 writes, a root deleted and remade, a root made later; in `session-index.test.ts` only: the core killed while it held the watch.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: which names are looked at (41 names captured from the TypeScript), the legacy chat flag of a head (42 heads: title slots, a header past 4 096 bytes, a cut character, a key written twice, non-objects, escaped keys, half a surrogate pair in a value, invalid UTF-8, numbers JavaScript reads as infinite), the rows kept (7 lists), all in `sessions/captured.json`; the settle rule (300 ms after a path's last change; a path whose time and size are what was last reported reports nothing; beside session files only folders count; a path that is gone always reports); the flag read once per session id at a path and again for another id or after the file went. `session-index.test.ts`'s 11 cases pass unchanged through the process, with 5 added.
  - Differences. Coalescing: the TypeScript reported each settled path by itself (one `onChange` and one re-read request per path); the core sends every path that is due at the same moment in one event, main calls `onChange` for each and asks for one re-read per event. Ordering: within an event the paths are in the order of their last change (it was the order their `lstat` finished). A watch that ends by itself (the root deleted: Windows' access-denied read; elsewhere a path under it that settles as gone while the root is no folder) is reported as a change and tried again every second, and is a change again once it watches; the TypeScript logged it and stayed unwatched until relaunch on Windows, and never noticed on macOS. A root the system refuses for another reason than "not there" is tried every second too (logged once), where the TypeScript gave up. Windows: last-access, attribute and security changes are not asked for (libuv asks for them; they move neither time nor size, so they only ever reported a path that had not been reported yet). A second `start()` is nothing (it made a second watcher). New ways to fail: the list read fails with the core's error when the core cannot answer `sessions.listed`; after a core ended, reports resume when the next core starts (any request starts it). The flag is read for the rows the list keeps, the GUI-labelled ones included (it was read for every row but those). A header line with half a surrogate pair in a key reads as no header; a lone surrogate in a path reads as U+FFFD.
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, this machine): `ompdesk-core.exe` 3 989 504 → 4 129 280 bytes with this unit alone, before 3b's code was in the binary (notify is not linked there); 1 000 files written one after another into ten folders (the Rust test): all 1 000 named, none dropped. Not measured: speed (a list read adds one round trip carrying each row's path and id; the stock scan bounds it), and the macOS binary's size with notify.
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `session-index.test.ts` against the real macOS binary, including FSEvents delivery, `/private/var/…` real paths, root creation/deletion and restart recovery. `native` runs the session Rust tests on Linux, Windows and macOS; Linux's scan also runs in `check`, and Windows' watch runs in `win-platform`. An actual OS-generated FSEvents overflow/rescan is not forced by these suites. notify panics on an FSEvents flag it does not know, which would end the core (the next core watches again).
- **3a. Control socket server** (`crates/core/src/control/`; was `src/main/control-socket/{server,endpoint,events,router}.ts`): the Unix socket / Windows named-pipe listener, stale-socket probe, owner checks and modes, token and endpoint files, hello/auth, line framing and connection/request/output caps, event ring/cursors/`events_lost`, and the request/wait/send-and-wait rules now run in the core. The CLI and `src/shared/control-protocol.ts` are unchanged. `router.ts` and `events.ts` are deleted; main's `server.ts` only relays the agent-control service.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `ControlBackend` and its calls into `AgentControlService` (this unit does not move the service); synchronous endpoint naming and environment installation (`endpoint.ts`, `index.ts`, `env.ts`, needed before the first await); CLI installation (`cli-install.ts`, shell launch plumbing). Main sends visible-session snapshots at the same instant it observes activity, answers a service call or polls, rather than letting the core read a later state. Core-to-main asks use the existing `Events` (`control`); main answers with ordinary, numbered `control.push` requests, ordered in the core even though its handlers run concurrently. No second wire or reply-frame kind.
  - Parity: the existing server/router/events vitest cases run through the real core, and `cli.test.ts` still drives the unchanged CLI against that server. Rust tests replay 1 613 rows captured from the TypeScript before deletion: 120 connection scripts, 24 callers, 22 absolute paths, 37 number spellings, 487 waits, 907 send-and-waits and 16 event-hub scripts. Instantaneous state changes are queued with their snapshots; a send's edge cursor starts when main actually invokes the service, not when the core asked it to resolve the session.
  - Lifetime: stdin EOF closes listeners and connections and removes this listener's endpoint and Unix socket before the core exits, including a bind still pending at EOF. The token stays and is reused across starts. Killing the core leaves `endpoint.json` and, on POSIX, the socket node; the next core's refused-connection probe removes the stale socket, binds and replaces the endpoint. Windows drops the named pipe when its process dies. Real `CoreProcess` tests cover EOF cleanup and kill/restart with the same token and address, a new core pid, and old callers released.
  - Security: a new token is still 32 OS-random bytes encoded as 64 hex characters; an entropy read failure refuses startup. Unix files are 0600 and folders 0700, with the same owner/link refusal and rotation of an exposed token. Windows inherits the profile directory's ACL and the pipe's default DACL, as Node did; the pipe also rejects remote clients and requires its first instance. No token is logged. No new direct dependency: tokio's `net` feature brings socket2; `test-util` is enabled only for tests.
  - Differences: a lost core closes outside callers and main rebinds the listener through the existing `CoreProcess` restart path; connection ids have a numeric incarnation prefix so a restarted core cannot revive a previous caller's ownership. Unix binds inside the protected 0700 folder and then chmods the socket to 0600, without changing the multithreaded process's umask. Cleanup checks the socket's device/inode so it does not unlink a replacement. JSON nested deeper than 128 levels is refused; isolated surrogate halves in strings the core reads become U+FFFD, as in the native CLI. Backpressure remains a 16 KiB application queue, but the OS pipe/socket buffer can accept a different number of frames before a paused reader blocks; `events_lost` is still exactly oldest retained sequence minus that reader's cursor (pinned by captured Rust rows).
  - Order: what a request does before its first wait (its ask of main) happens in the order its connection's lines came, as the TypeScript's synchronous call did: `conn.rs` polls each request once before the scheduler takes it. Rust test `requests_sent_together_reach_main_in_line_order` (fails without that poll) and the cap case of `server.test.ts`, which the first macOS run failed with answers 3 before 2.
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs the four control-socket vitest files against the real macOS core and Unix socket, `check` runs them on Linux's Unix socket and `win-platform` on the named pipe; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes. Developed on Windows with the Unix implementation also checked under WSL Linux; no Darwin target is installed on that computer, so macOS paths are CI's checks. No performance claim.
- **4a. Tailscale** (`crates/core/src/tailscale/`; was the logic of `src/main/tailscale/{cli,locate,status,whois,serve}.ts` and the CLI sequences of `service.ts`): finding the CLI (`tailscale.locate`: the override, the pref, the Mac's fixed places with the app behind a binary, a link or a wrapper script and its bundle id through `plutil`, the two Program Files folders, the PATH), running it and reading what it prints (`tailscale.status` with the document and why HTTPS cannot work, `tailscale.whois`, `tailscale.up` with the sign-in link of a `--json` run that is cut short, `tailscale.controlHost`, `tailscale.version`), the serve sequences with their rules (`tailscale.publish`: read the configuration, pick our own earlier port or the first free one, refuse an exposed port, run `serve --bg`; `tailscale.confirm`: the handler is in place and private; `tailscale.unpublish`: only a handler the record still names exactly, then the check that the user's other handlers are as they were) and the Mac's "start the app" with a test's stand-in (`tailscale.startApp`).
  - Main hands over how the CLI runs with every request, as for git and gh: the binary a lookup found, main's whole environment at that moment with the located CLI's own variables over it, the 10 s ceiling (4 s for whois, 15 s for setup's version read) and the 4 MiB cap; for a lookup the platform whose rules apply, the override, the pref, `%ProgramFiles%`, `%ProgramFiles(x86)%` and the login shell's PATH. A run is reported as `execFile` reported one (`Ran` in `mod.rs`): a CLI that fails, cannot start (`spawn <bin> ENOENT`) or is cut short (no exit code, what it printed kept) is an answer, never a fault, because Mobile web's phases are read from exactly that. `run.rs` gained `Exec::run_kept` for it (the timeout as an answer with the output kept; the kill, the wait for the exit and the scope rules are `run`'s, which is now `run_kept` plus its fault). Nothing logs a request, an address, a login or the serve secret.
  - A request made in one of Mobile web's steps is held to that step (`tailscaleRequest` in `cli.ts`: `currentStepSignal`, `trackEffect`): it never starts in a stopped step, a step stopped while it runs has its CLI ended by the core with no other started, and the chain's next step waits until the core is done with it.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: Mobile web's state machine (`service.ts`: the phases, the poll and its timers, the switch) is state over main's own ports, the prefs, the page's server (`WebHost`), machine setup, Electron's `shell` and the push to the windows, and moves with the web server's unit; between `tailscale.publish` and `tailscale.confirm` it saves the record, so the two stay two requests. Computer sharing's caches (`tailnet.ts`: the CLI it found, a status for 3 s, a whois for 60 s, one read shared by callers that ask together) are kept by main's clock, which its tests set, and belong to `src/main/remote`, which is still TypeScript; no caller reads them synchronously, they move with that unit. `isTailnetAddress`, which the gate of `remote/devices.ts` reads synchronously for every connection. `loginUrlAllowed`, main's own check of what it hands `shell.openExternal`, read while a status is applied. `cleanLogins` and `parseMobileWebRequest`, the checks of what the page and the prefs sent. The version floor (`compareVersions` is `src/shared` code the page runs too). Windows' tray app (`tailscale-ipn.exe`) is started by main, detached: it has to outlive OMPdesk, and every process the core starts ends with it (its job object). `status.ts` and `whois.ts` are the answers' types.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 472 rows captured from the TypeScript (`tailscale/captured.json`: 179 status documents with what `httpsProblem` said of each, every key absent, `null`, of another type and in its edge spellings, keys JavaScript orders as numbers, a key written twice; 35 whois outputs; 35 serve configurations; 19 pref values as records; 80 rules over them, ownership, a taken port, an exposed one, each plan, a turn-off's check; 10 outputs for the first line, 21 for the login link, 16 `up --json` outputs, 23 `debug prefs` outputs; 49 machines a lookup reads and 5 bundle ids) give the same answers, every one. `service.test.ts` runs its 57 cases through the real process: its fake CLI is now an executable the core runs (`fakeTailscaleCli`), which asks the test's process for each answer; three cases wait for a real run where they assumed an immediate one (the two stopped-step cases until the first step reached its server, the poll case for each poll), and the source check reads the core's module too. `tailnet.test.ts`'s 4 cases pass unchanged. The cases of `parseStatus`, `parseWhois`, `serve.ts`, the CLI text helpers and the made-up machines of `locate.test.ts` are Rust tests; `locate.test.ts` now looks over real files (the override, a wrapper, a link into an app, the execute bit, Windows' order) and `cli.test.ts` is new (node standing in for the CLI: exit codes, a missing CLI, the environment, an `up --json` cut short, the app's stand-in).
  - Differences. A CLI past its ceiling is asked to end, killed 2 s later and waited for before its call answers (`execFile` asked once and answered at once). A process a signal ended that was not ours reads exit code 1 (node read no code and, when it printed nothing, showed its own `Command failed` line as the detail). stderr is kept to 64 KiB (was 4 MiB, then an error) and the stdout cap counts bytes (was UTF-16 units). A `.cmd` stand-in runs as Rust's std runs one (`cmd.exe /e:ON /v:OFF /d /c`, an argument quoted only where needed, one holding a line break refused), where node quoted every argument. A Mobile web step stopped past its bound has its CLI ended at once and the next step starts after that; the TypeScript let it run to its own ceiling beside the next step. `published` is cleared when a publish answers, no longer just before the command runs (the same unless the step throws in between). A core that cannot answer reads as a CLI that could not run, with the core's message as the detail; a lookup then fails, which Mobile web shows as `error`, turning the switch off survives (the handler and its record stay for the next launch) and machine setup's detection reads Tailscale as not found.
