A user id past 2^53 keeps every digit, so its login is found where JavaScript's rounding missed its `User` entry (`userId` itself still rounds once main reads it). Half a surrogate pair reads as U+FFFD, also where a whois name is cut through a pair at its 80 units; JSON nested deeper than 128 levels is no document; a number no double holds reads as absent. A PATH folder spelled with `..`, a doubled or a forward slash is joined as spelled (node's `join` normalized it; the file is the same), and the login shell's PATH is asked for before every lookup (it was only read when the fixed places had none; main caches it). Two serve configurations with the same handlers are equal whatever order ICU would sort tied keys in. `tailscale.whois` refuses an argument that starts with `-` itself, beside main's check that it is an address.
  - Not measured: speed (a request replaces one or more CLI runs main started itself; the CLI bounds each) and the binary's growth.
  - CI coverage: `check` and `win-platform` run the four vitest files against the Linux and the Windows core (on Windows the fake CLI is a `.cmd`, so cmd.exe, the job object and `CREATE_NO_WINDOW` are exercised); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes, the `/bin/sh` stand-ins (`run_kept`, the reported runs, the cut-short `up`) on Linux and macOS only. The macOS parity list does not name `src/main/tailscale/*.test.ts` yet. A real Tailscale is never run by any suite; `e2e/mobile-web.e2e.ts` and the remote specs drive the fake CLI through the app.
- **4b. SSH tools** (`crates/core/src/ssh/`, Windows' per-process job in `sys.rs`; was `src/main/ssh/options.ts`, `askpass.ts` and every process `service.ts` started): what a saved host may be and the arguments it becomes, the config every run reads with `-F`, the listening ports in a server's listing, what an askpass question asks for, why an ssh ended and the names its host keys are saved under (`rules.rs`); the questions ssh asks through `SSH_ASKPASS` (`askpass.rs`); the port forwards and port watches that stay (`live.rs`); and the short runs of `ssh -G`, `ssh-keygen` and `ssh-add` through the shared runner (`run.rs` `Exec`). Methods: `ssh.plan` (checks what the page sent and names a free local port), `ssh.forward` and `ssh.watch` (answer once the ssh started; the run then reports by events and ends by `ssh.stop`, by its service's `ssh.close`, or when the core's input ends: `close_all` beside 3a's in `serve`, so a main that died leaves no ssh and no socket file), `ssh.answer`, `ssh.agent`, `ssh.createKey`, `ssh.loadKeys`, `ssh.forgetHostKey`. Events: `ssh.up` and `ssh.ports` (a listing only when it differs from the one before), `ssh.exit` `{failure: {kind, message}}`, `ssh.prompts` (the waiting questions after every change). `src/main/ssh/client.ts` is the typed client; `options.ts` and `askpass.ts` are deleted.
  - Main hands how to run with every request, as for git and gh (`SshCore.#how`): the three programs (names for the PATH, or beside `OMP_GUI_SSH`, the tests' seam, whose siblings now keep its extension: `ssh.cmd` → `ssh-add.cmd`), the whole environment, the home folder, `<userData>/ssh`, 30 s for a short run, and with a run the prompt timeout and the watch's poll seconds (`src/shared/ssh-tools.ts`, the page's constants).
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `SshTools` (`service.ts`), what the person wants running: which forwards exist and each one's standing (connecting, up, retrying by the delay table, stopped, host key changed), one connect per generation, the grace clock, the watch set, the launch restore and the saved list `ssh-forwards.json` (through `atomic-file.ts`, the one writer of a file replaced in one step; the core has none yet). They are rules over main's own state that its commands change within one tick: `service.test.ts`'s re-entrancy cases pin that a stop, a remove or a dispose issued right after a forward's answer never starts an ssh, which only the process that takes the commands can promise (2c names the same gap for git: a stop in main reaches the core a moment later). It moves when the commands themselves are taken in the core. Also main's: the shape check of a request and the grace range (zod), the broadcast to windows (`ipc.ts`, Electron).
  - The question dialog stays the page's; the core asks main through `ssh.prompts` and main sends the person's answer in `ssh.answer`. The listener and the helper are the core's because they belong to the ssh it runs and need nothing of Electron: the helper ssh runs is `ompdesk-core` itself (`main.rs` looks first for the two variables only `Askpass::grant` sets), so ssh needs no Node, no Electron and no helper file. The listener is a Unix socket only this user may open or a Windows named pipe that rejects remote clients and requires its first instance, as 3a's control socket (tokio's `net`); token, question id and socket name are the system's random bytes (`sys::random`). The answer has to cross from the page to the helper either way; it crosses in one request frame the core never logs.
  - Secrets: a password, passphrase or code is in `ssh.answer`'s params, in the listener's memory until the helper's connection took it, and on the helper's stdout, nowhere else. A prompt names ssh's question and never an answer. The core logs no request; every refusal of an `ssh.*` request is a fixed text (`Invalid SSH tools request`, `Invalid host`, …) that repeats nothing of it, where serde's own message would quote a value (the params hold the environment and, for `ssh.answer`, the answer). What an ssh was answered (every answer but a host key's, 4 characters or more) is cut out of that ssh's stderr before anything reads it (`rules::scrub`, `***`), so a status or a watch's error never carries it. Proved by `service.test.ts` "reports nothing of a password or a passphrase…" (a stand-in ssh that prints what it was answered and exits as a refused login, a forward and a watch, a refused answer here and in the core: every broadcast to the windows, every error and every line the core process writes to its log is free of both), and in Rust by `keeps_what_a_process_was_answered_only_for_its_own_stderr`, `replaces_every_answer_a_process_was_given` and `refuses_a_request_without_repeating_anything_of_it`.
  - No option injection. The guards, each kept from the TypeScript and listed at the top of `rules.rs`: a host of 1 to 255 UTF-16 units that never starts with `-`; a user of at most 100 and a key path of at most 1 000 units that never start with `-`; every port a whole number from 1 to 65535; user, port and key path each the value of its own flag (`-l`, `-p`, `-i`); the host last, after `--`; no shell (an argument list). `Target` is what the page sent and only `Destination`, what passed them, builds arguments. Added: a folder with `"` or a line break in its name is refused before it is written into the config (it would end its quoted value), and the watch's sleep is a number by its type. They are checked in the core alone (the zod copies in `service.ts` are gone), at `ssh.plan` before every connect, at a watch's start and at a forgotten key. Rust test `hostile_targets_never_become_options` (21 refused targets, 10 odd ones that pass and stay one argument behind their flag or `--` in all four argument lists); `starts_no_ssh_for_a_hostile_host_and_reports_one_that_cannot_start` over the wire (no folder, no config, no process for a refused request).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: rows captured from the TypeScript on Windows before it was deleted (`ssh/captured.json`): 50 listings (`ss`, `netstat` of Linux and macOS, `lsof`, headings, brackets, zone ids, ports out of range, Unicode spaces), 32 questions, 32 stderr texts, 54 `ssh -G` outputs with their saved names (`Number()` read as JavaScript reads it: hex, exponent, empty), 52 targets and 10 ports through the zod schema, 3 configs. `service.test.ts`'s 13 `SshTools` cases pass through the process, and for the first time on Windows (the stand-in is a `.cmd` shim there), unchanged but for one count: the stand-in now records a port listing before it writes it and the watch case waits for the fourth record, because the core reads ssh's pipe without the supervisor's hop and main heard a listing before the stand-in had counted it (Linux CI, `ENOENT … listings`). Its 4 `ssh options` cases and 2 `askpass` cases moved to Rust with their functions (`rules.rs`, `askpass.rs`: a forged token, a release that cancels only its own process's questions), where 3 more cover a question nobody answers, a helper that left and a closed listener, and two POSIX ones run a watch and a forward with a shell stand-in through the wire (listings, a stop answered by the exit of a process that is gone, a refused login, a close).
  - Differences. Stopping: on Windows an ssh's whole tree ends at once through a job of its own (`sys::ProcessTree`; the supervisor swept the tree with PowerShell), on POSIX the ssh gets SIGTERM and is killed 2 s later (the supervisor signalled ssh's process group): what ssh started (a ProxyJump hop, a ProxyCommand) ends when its pipes close, or with the core, whose process group main kills. The ssh processes are the core's children, so a core that ends takes every forward and watch with it; main reports them dropped (`ompdesk-core exited`) when the next core starts or a stop finds none, and a forward then reconnects as after any drop. Short runs: `ssh -G`, `ssh-keygen` and `ssh-add` are ended after 30 s and then read as not started (the TypeScript waited without bound); the request itself gets 40 s (`ssh.agent`, `ssh.createKey`), 2 min (`ssh.forgetHostKey`) or 5 min (`ssh.loadKeys`); an `ssh-add -l` a signal ended reads as an agent without keys (code 1 where node gave `null`: error). Checks: a refused host, user, key path or port fails with the core's words (`Invalid host`) where it was a ZodError's list; a saved list that holds one loads, and that forward stops with the refusal, where the whole list was set aside as corrupt; an answer past 4 000 units is refused by the core; turning a watch off needs only the host's name. Askpass: no `askpass.js`, `askpass.sh` or `askpass.cmd` is written to `<userData>/ssh` (those of an earlier version stay there unused), and on Windows no cmd.exe reads ssh's question on the way to the helper; the helper reads its answer as one line and leaves, and the listener keeps the connection until then (2 s at most); a helper line that is JSON `null` is cancelled (it threw in main); a question cut at 4 000 units through a surrogate pair ends in U+FFFD; after a core restart the windows are told no question waits. The config is written whenever it does not read as it should (once per launch before) and never while it does. stderr's kept end is 8 000 bytes (was UTF-16 units); a listing past 1 MiB without its end mark is dropped; a starting forward's port is asked every 400 ms with a 400 ms limit per try. An ssh's environment is main's plus five askpass variables (seven before: the two that named the Node helper are gone).
  - One observation, not a benchmark (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, this machine, the stand-in ssh): a forward is up 440 ms after its start (the 400 ms probe bounds it) and a stopped one is gone 12 ms after the stop. Not measured: speed against the TypeScript (ssh and the network bound every run), the binary's size.
  - The repository lock's model (`formal/repo-lock-core`) covers `run.rs`, `sys.rs`, `main.rs` and `server.rs` whole: this unit changed none of the modelled steps (`Runner::environment` became crate-visible, the job object code moved into `sys.rs`'s `job` module beside the per-process job, `main.rs` looks for the helper's variables first, `server.rs` registers the module and ends its services at the end of input). `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core`: 7 of 7 runs as expected, 13 of 13 mutations detected, `@code` tags resolve, DRIFT on those four files, not stamped here (`formal/` is not this unit's; its `server.rs` line tags sit one line lower since the module's registration).
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run `ssh/service.test.ts` against the real core with the stand-in ssh (the Unix socket and SIGTERM there, the named pipe, the job and the `.cmd` shim on Windows); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes (the askpass listener's over a real socket or pipe on each). `mac-native` should add `src/main/ssh/service.test.ts`. Developed on Windows; the Unix code was also run under WSL Linux (Rust tests), macOS paths are CI's. No test connects to a real host or reads the person's `~/.ssh`.
- **3d. Session processes** (`crates/core/src/procs/`, the OS calls in `sys.rs`; new, nothing moved from TypeScript): every process with the tag in its environment (`procs.list {env, timeoutMs}` → pid, parent, resident bytes, start, image name, tag) and the end of what carries given tags (`procs.end {env, tags, keepArg, graceMs, timeoutMs}` → `ended` with `forced`, `left`, `kept`, `own`). The rules of which tags end and when are main's (`src/main/session-processes.ts`, control-tools › Session processes).
  - By system: Windows extends the memory sampler's Toolhelp32 snapshot (`sys.rs` `process_list`; `memory.processes` opens with the limited right as before and reads no start) with `GetProcessTimes` and a handle opened to read and end (`PROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_VM_READ | PROCESS_TERMINATE | SYNCHRONIZE`), reads the PEB's `RTL_USER_PROCESS_PARAMETERS` (`NtQueryInformationProcess`, `ReadProcessMemory`: `CommandLine` at 0x70, `Environment` at 0x80, `EnvironmentSize` at 0x3F0; 64-bit only, a compile error otherwise), asks through `EnumWindows`/`PostMessageW(WM_CLOSE)` (user32 is linked now) and ends with `TerminateProcess` through the same handle. macOS and Linux list with `/bin/ps -axo pid=,ppid=,rss=,etime=,comm=` through `memory::program` (shared, not copied); macOS reads `sysctl {CTL_KERN, KERN_PROCARGS2, pid}` into one `KERN_ARGMAX` buffer (`parse_procargs`, pure, tested on every OS), Linux `/proc/<pid>/environ` and `cmdline`; both signal with `kill` (never pid 0 or a negative one).
  - What it cannot read: another user's process, an elevated or protected one on Windows (406 of 624 were readable in the measurement), a zombie; none of them is ever a target. macOS and Linux see the environment a process was started with, Windows the one it holds now. Parameters are checked before anything is ended (`env` a variable name, each tag 32 lowercase hex digits, `keepArg` not empty).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, 608 processes, the machine at 99 % CPU from other sessions' work, an optimized build without LTO): one `procs.list` 90 ms (median of 20, 65 to 190): 55 ms the snapshot and handles (what `memory.processes` costs under that load), 33 ms the environments (3.4 MB read), 1 ms the tags. The binary grew 4 605 952 → 4 706 816 bytes. Not measured: macOS (one `sysctl` per process), and what linking user32 adds to the core's start.
  - CI coverage: `native` runs the Rust tests on Linux, Windows and macOS: a tagged tree with a re-parented grandchild found and ended while another tag's and an untagged process stay, `keepArg`, `own`, the parsers; on Linux and macOS also SIGTERM before SIGKILL (a shell that traps it is not forced, one that ignores it is). `mac-native` and `win-platform` run `session-processes.test.ts` against the real core. Developed on Windows, with the Unix code run under WSL Linux and compile-checked for `aarch64-apple-darwin`; macOS's `KERN_PROCARGS2` read ran first in CI. The `WM_CLOSE` step has no committed test (a runner may show no window): checked once by hand (a WinForms window closed unforced, a console `ping` forced).
- **4c. Extension update rules** (`crates/core/src/ext/`; was `src/main/ext-update/mcp.ts`, the rules of `classify.ts` and `running.ts`, the file reads of `mcp-owner.ts`, the parsers of `mcp-plans.ts`, and `src/shared/version.ts` `isExactVersion`, which only `classify.ts` called): what an MCP server's configured launch is (`ext.mcpClasses`: the built-in REA by its source folder, a remote URL, a runner's package with its pin, a direct command, an interpreter), the row seed of every marketplace, plugin, skill group and MCP server with its key, method, skip reason, target and order (`ext.seeds`), what the file a server's command resolves to says about itself (`ext.binaryFacts`: script or native by its first bytes, the python its shebang names, a pipx or `uv tool` venv by its path and the version that venv's `dist-info` records), the running rule (`ext.defersWhileRunning`, and `ext.serverRunning` over the `ps` lines main hands in) and the newest version in a registry lookup's output (`ext.pipLatest`, `ext.registryLatest`). `src/main/ext-update/{mcp,classify,running}.ts` are the contracts and clients (`core.ts` `extRule`, fenced to the updater's step as its other outside calls are). A refresh asks the core twice (the servers' classes, then the seeds) and once per direct MCP command.
  - Main hands in what it or the page also defines, so nothing is spelled twice: the REA version (`REA_VERSION`), the kinds' display order (`EXTENSION_KINDS`), the locale its `localeCompare` resolves, each project folder's key (`projectHash`, node's SHA-1, so every row key is what it was) and the binaries it resolved; the client joins a binary target's `binary` and `owner` back from main's own map.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why. `ExtensionUpdater` (schedule, back-off, row state, the stock helper's inventory, checks and upgrades, `SetupService.runToolUpdate`, the apply plan) is main's: the scheduler, the helper and setup are other units. Finding a command and telling who owns it (`mcp-owner.ts`: the PATH lookup, realpath, the `.cmd` shim, `classifyInstall`, the writable check, `packageVersion`, a keg's version, the pip probe) and the check and update commands (`mcp-plans.ts`) stay whole, because they are built on rules `CliUpdater`, machine setup and the omp lookup share: `spawn.ts` (`findOnPath`, `launcherNames`, `needsShell`, `spawnCommand`, `spawnArgs`), the cli-tools rules (`classifyInstall`, `cmdShimTarget`, `packageVersion`, `writableTarget`, `ownerCheckCommand`, `ownerUpdateCommand`, `parseLatest`, `bunView`, `probeOutput`, `runCheckProcess`) and `parseOmpVersion`. Those answer synchronously where no core can be counted on: `resolveOmp` before any session, machine setup's first-launch detection, the setup supervisor (its own process, with no core client) and `scripts/build-lock.ts`, which runs the command that builds the core itself. Moving them would strand a broken install behind the core it has to repair, and a second definition beside the TypeScript one is forbidden (AGENTS.md › Where a new feature goes). **PATH lookup and install ownership (`spawn.ts`, the cli-tools rules, `parseOmpVersion`) can move only once the core is not needed to repair itself, which is the shell swap's question, not a unit's.** `scansProcesses` (which platform's main has a `ps` listing to hand in) and `projectHash` stay too.
  - Secrets: a server's `args` and `url` and the processes' command lines can carry a token. They cross the pipe as the stock helper and `ps` gave them; the core logs no request, and an `ext.*` request of another shape answers where the mismatch is (`… error at column n`), never serde's words, which quote the value (Rust test `a_refused_shape_tells_nothing_of_what_it_held`).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 786 rows captured from the TypeScript (`ext/captured.json`: 24 command names, 22 built-in checks, 331 servers over every runner, flag table and spec form, 32 version specs, 39 inventories with their seeds, 42 names ordered under 8 locales, 36 running-rule rows, 62 `ps` matches, 54 venv paths on both platforms, 33 file heads, 50 shebangs read through `detectMcpBinary`, 39 venv layouts on disk, 34 pip and 22 registry outputs) give the same answers. The regular expressions are hand-written matchers (the crate links no regex engine) with JavaScript's `\s`, `.`, `^` under `m`, `i` over ASCII and the backtracking the patterns allow (`===?`, the last `-` of a `dist-info` name, the last venv marker of a path). `mcp.test.ts`, `classify.test.ts`, `running.test.ts`, `mcp-plans.test.ts` and `updater.test.ts` keep their cases, awaited, through the real core; `updater.test.ts`'s three fake-clock cases wait for the core's answers (real I/O no fake clock moves) before they move the clock. Three cases are added for a core that cannot answer. The cases of `commandName`, `fileKind`, `shebangPython`, `distInfoVersion` and `isExactVersion` moved to Rust with their functions (their rows are in the capture), and `mcp-owner.test.ts` reads a shebang, a file's kind and a venv's version through `detectMcpBinary` instead, on Windows too.
  - Differences: a rule that threw now rejects (`parsePipIndex`, `parseRegistryVersion`, `parseMcpOwnerLatest`; the updater awaits them); a lookup's output that names no version fails as a `CoreError` (`no_version`) with the same message, which is all a row shows. New ways to fail, since the rules are requests now. While the rows are read again (the servers' classes, a binary's file, the seeds), a core that cannot answer fails the pass and the rows stay as they were; an owned server is never listed as an unknown install because its file's read got no answer (`noBinary`). During a row's check (a lookup's answer, the running rule, the binary read again after its update) it is that row's failed check, with the core's message and the row's back-off, and the pass goes on: no update runs without the running rule, and an update whose result cannot be read is neither "updated" nor "not updated" (`updater.test.ts` "a core that cannot answer"). Half a surrogate pair in a name, a command, an output or a `ps` line reads U+FFFD. An inventory of another shape than the helper's contract is `invalid_params` where the TypeScript threw a `TypeError` or built an odd row: a count that is no number is no grouped row (JavaScript coerced `"3"`), a plugin channel the contract does not name has method null (was undefined), a Homebrew owner without a name matches no keg (was `/Cellar/undefined/`). Names sort by ICU4X's collation for the locale main resolves: equal to `localeCompare` for the captured names, and it may differ for other strings where the two ICU data versions do. A file's head is read once (256 bytes) for its kind and its shebang, also for a binary a package manager owns, where the TypeScript opened it twice and read the shebang only of an unowned script.
  - Not measured: speed (the stock helper's inventory read and the registry lookups bound a pass).
  - CI coverage: `check` and `win-platform` run the ext-update vitest files against the real core (Windows' file opens and `\` paths in the venv rule); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes, the venv layouts on each one's file system. `mac-native`'s list does not name `src/main/ext-update/*.test.ts` yet.
- **4d. GitHub sign-in's gh and git runs** (`crates/core/src/gh/auth.rs`; was the logic of `src/main/github-auth/cli.ts` and the sequences `service.ts` ran over it): the check (`gh.authCheck`: gh's version, `gh auth status` with its fallback for gh < 2.40, the active login in both of gh's spellings, the other signed-in accounts, git's global identity), the commit identity filled from the account (`gh.authIdentity`: `gh api user`, the noreply address, each key set only while empty, the identity after), the sign-out with its `--user` retry (`gh.authLogout`), the switch (`gh.authSwitch`) and the star, set or read with gh's 404 (`gh.star`) are core methods, each one request. Every run in them is 2e's `Gh::text` (30 s, every exit code answered). Main hands over gh's and git's runners (`ghRunner`, exported for it; git runs with gh's runner, as `ghText(home, git, …)` ran it), `null` for a tool not found, and the core answers the service's own words for it (`GitHub CLI (gh) is not installed`, `git is not installed`). `cli.ts` is the typed client (`coreHeld`, the request's runs in a row × 30 s: check 3, fill 5, sign-out 2, switch and star 1).
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: the sign-in itself (`service.ts`), decided from the code. `startLogin`, `openLogin` and `cancelLogin` change the state and answer the page in the same tick (each IPC handler returns the state the call returns), and `openLogin` decides from the transcript main holds whether gh waits on its "Press Enter to open" and releases it at once; a transcript in the core would make every PTY chunk and that decision a round trip, and a cancel could cross an answer about a sign-in already ended (4b's reasoning for `SshTools`). So the state, its pushes to the windows, `gh auth login` in stock's PTY through the stock helper (`pty.ts`), the transcript's rules (escapes stripped, the device code and URL, the Git-credentials and Enter answers, the 20 000-character tail, the exit's last three lines), the 10-minute timeout, gh's install through setup and the check's reuse (`refresh`: 10 s, one check at a time, a forced one after the last) stay; so does the switch's check that the login is one gh listed, a rule over that state. Finding gh and git is main's as for every gh request (2e: `resolveBin`, and `usableGit`'s `xcode-select -p` on macOS so Apple's install dialog never opens).
  - Secrets: every fault of these requests has the values of gh's token variables in either runner's environment read `***`, stdout included. The TypeScript built a failure's message as `stderr.trim() || stdout.trim()` with only stderr scrubbed (by `gh.text`), so a token gh printed on stdout reached the page as the error; it no longer does. The core logs nothing of a request (2e). Proved by Rust `a_fault_carries_no_token_of_either_environment` and `service.test.ts` "reports nothing of a token a failing gh prints" (the fake gh quotes `GH_TOKEN` on stdout and stderr for every call but the version and status: each message reads `HTTP 401: Bad credentials ***`, no state pushed to the page holds the token).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 150 rows captured from the TypeScript at `b0c90dd3` (`gh/auth-captured.json`): 34 outputs for the active login (both spellings, `account` before `as` wherever each stands, the leftmost match, JavaScript's `\s` with NBSP, U+2028, U+3000 and U+FEFF but not U+0085, U+200B or U+180E, `\s+` across a line break), 23 for the account list (CRLF and a lone CR, `/i` over ASCII only, a Kelvin sign that does not fold, failed accounts and duplicates), and 93 of the service's sequences over a scripted gh and git with every argv in order: the check (the fallback in three spellings, signed out, no login line, the status on stderr, a run that fails, gh or git missing), the fill (every `id` spelling JSON allows against `Number.isSafeInteger`, name and email trimmed with Unicode spaces, each answer that is no account, git config failing with and without words), the sign-out with and without `--user`, the switch, the star with each 404 spelling. They give the same answers and calls but for the differences below. `service.test.ts` runs its 13 cases through the real core with the fake gh (now a `.cmd` shim on Windows, the script itself elsewhere) and adds the token case; its parser case moved to the Rust rows.
  - Differences: a `gh api user` answer that is no JSON fails with serde's words (`gh_json`) where JavaScript wrote its own (the rows were captured under Bun, `JSON Parse error: …`; Electron's V8 says `Unexpected token …`). An `id` past a double (`1e400`) is read as `JSON.parse` read it (Infinity, left out of the noreply address) only because the shipped core is built with the workspace, whose CLI brings serde_json's `arbitrary_precision`; the core crate built alone fails the fill as no JSON (the Rust row accepts either). A failure is a `CoreError` with a code (`gh_exit`, `git_exit`, `gh_json`, `gh_missing`, `git_missing`, `spawn_failed`, `gh_timeout`) where it was an `Error`; the page reads only the message. The check answers once every run in it ended, and fails with the first failure in the order identity, version, status, where `Promise.all` rejected at the first failure in time and left the other runs going. A check is one request bounded at 3 × 30 s plus slack, where each run had its own 30 s.
  - The lock's model: no request here is made under `withRepoLock`. `exec.ts#coreHeld`, a covered item of `formal/repo-lock-core`, changed its doc comment only (it names these requests now); `run.rs`, `server.rs` and `sys.rs` are unchanged.
  - Not measured: speed (gh's network calls bound every request; one request replaces up to seven runs).
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run `service.test.ts` against the real core with the fake gh (the `.cmd` shim, cmd.exe and the job on Windows); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes (a scripted runner, no process). `mac-native`'s list does not name `src/main/github-auth/service.test.ts` yet. `e2e/github-sign-in.e2e.ts` and `github-cli-install.e2e.ts` drive the app's sign-in through the existing selections.
- **4e. Routine triggers' reads and rules** (`crates/core/src/routines/`; was the reading, ids, lines and mark of `src/main/routines/github.ts`, `ports.ts`' `ghApi` and `scanPath`, `files.ts`' `changedNames` and `parseMark`, and `source.ts`' `describeCommits`): `routines.github` is one read of one kind of a GitHub trigger (`gh api` through `coreGh`, the endpoint, the rows each kind keeps, new ids against the seen ones, at most 200 kept and 10 fired per read, oldest first, the default branch's commits and every line); it answers `{state, mark, lines, error}`, where `state` is the core's, opaque to main and handed back with the next read (so an id that was absent stays apart from `null`, as the TypeScript's `Set` kept it), and `mark` is what `routine-marks.json` keeps, restored by the core on a launch's first read as `#restore` did. `routines.filesScan` is a files trigger's listing (name order in UTF-16 units, the ignored folders and the cap sent by main as `IGNORED_DIRS` and `FILES_LISTING_MAX`, a link listed and never followed, its size node's on Windows through 2g's `entry_size`); `routines.filesChanged` reads the last launch's listing from the mark and answers the names that changed (`name in entries` also finding `Object.prototype`'s members, a file named `__proto__` never kept, as the TypeScript's objects did). `git.routineCommits` (2h) takes the branch and answers the trigger's line (`text.rs` `describe_commits`), or `null` when there are no commits.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `RoutineTriggers` (`triggers.ts`: the schedule service's state, a source per enabled trigger, the quiet window after a run, marks dropped) and every source's timers, the order of its reads and telling a failure once (`failureReporter`); the git source's state machine over main's run state; the files watcher (`watchPath`, `original-fs`'s recursive watch, whose events drive the burst's 2 s/10 s timers, the minute's retry and the quiet check); the session trigger (`sessions.ts`: the tab pool's activity and `openerOfSessions`, which `src/shared/sidebar-rows.ts` keeps for the page); `FileMarks` (`marks.ts`: read synchronously as a source starts, written synchronously at quit through `atomic-file.ts`); `usableRepo` (over `GITHUB_REPO`, which the page uses: a malformed repository never reaches the core). **The email trigger and its IMAP client stay** (`email.ts`, `imap.ts`): TLS needs rustls with a crypto provider (ring or aws-lc-rs), none of which is in `Cargo.lock`, and both build C for the target, which this repository's cross-target clippy from Windows cannot; the snippet and the RFC 2047 words decode with `TextDecoder` in whatever charset a mail names (`euc-kr`, `iso-2022-jp`, `windows-1252`, …), which needs `encoding_rs`, not in `Cargo.lock` either. `clip` stays for the session, files and email lines; the core has a copy for the lines it writes, and both are held to the same 150 captured rows (`text.rs`, `source.test.ts`).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: rows captured from the TypeScript at `b0c90dd3` (`routines/captured.json`): 150 `clip` rows, 11 `describeCommits` rows, 19 GitHub scenarios replayed as the source ran them (each kind, stored marks of every shape and `Object.prototype` names in one, the seen cap, 17 odd rows, answers that are no list, `null` rows, gh failures told once, a renamed default branch, branch names through `encodeURIComponent`, every kind in one source), 6 trees listed (past the 2 000 cap, ignored folders, a file named `.git`, `__proto__`, Korean, emoji and fullwidth names, a time in 1970, a missing path, a path under a file) and 350 mark/listing comparisons. `github.test.ts` runs its 10 cases through the real core with a stand-in gh (node behind a `.cmd` on Windows, a shell script elsewhere), `git.test.ts` checks the core's lines on real repositories, `files.test.ts` runs its relaunch cases through `routines.filesChanged` and lists a real folder.
  - Differences: a field GitHub gives as text that comes as another type (a number as a title, a commit message that is an object) fails the read with `GitHub's answer has a field of another type`, where V8's `TypeError` named the bundle's minified variable; `null` read through keeps V8's `Cannot read properties of null (reading '…')`. An answer that is no JSON fails in serde's words (as 2e). A read the core cannot answer fails like any failed read (told once; the trigger stays where its last answer left it). The files trigger compares in a request after the listing, so its quiet check comes after that answer, and a comparison that fails is told as the listing's failure and is not made again that launch. A name the system holds in no Unicode is passed over (node's lossy name named nothing either). Not measured: speed (gh's network and the walk bound the reads; a read replaces main's spawn and its JSON parse with one round trip).
  - CI coverage: `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes (the link listing on Linux and macOS only); `check` and `win-platform` run `src/main/routines/*.test.ts` against the real core (the `.cmd` stand-in gh on Windows); `mac-native` already lists `src/main/routines/*.test.ts`.
- **6a. The setup supervisor** (`crates/core/src/supervise.rs`, the tree's OS calls in `sys.rs`; was `SUPERVISOR_SOURCE` and `TREE_SWEEP_SCRIPT` of `src/main/setup/supervisor.ts`, run by Electron's own binary as Node through `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`): every supervised run (installs and tool updates, scheduled omp runs, ai-memory's `setup`, Autopilot's land check, a worktree hook, so 2i's `runWorktreeHook` too) starts `ompdesk-core --supervise` directly (`nativeBinPath`; a process mode as 4b's askpass helper, chosen in `main.rs` before the core joins its job; no running core, no request), the command in `OMPDESK_SETUP_SPEC` as before. `spawnSupervised` keeps its signature and returns a `ChildProcess`, so its seven callers are unchanged; `SetupRunner`, `findBun`, `quoteWindowsArg`, `isolatedLaunch` and Bun's isolated launcher stay TypeScript (4c's PATH lookups; the launcher's handle list is the memory rule's isolated `setup`, unchanged).
  - Kept: the exit status (the command's; 130 after a stop; 127 with node's message, `spawn <file> ENOENT` or `ENOENT: no such file or directory, open '<path>'`, when it could not start; 1 for a command a signal ended), the output on the same pipes, `inputFile` as stdin (else none), an isolated command's log (POSIX: its stdout and stderr in one file; both systems: printed after the command exited and removed), `shell` as node runs it (`/bin/sh -c`, `%ComSpec% /d /s /c "…"` verbatim), the spec variable left out of the command's environment. POSIX: the command leads its own session (`setsid`, node's `detached`), a stop sends SIGTERM to its group and SIGKILL 2 s later, the group is SIGKILLed once the command exited, SIGTERM or SIGINT to the supervisor stops as a stdin close does, and the command starts with an empty signal mask, as libuv starts it (the supervisor blocks the two for itself and std passes a thread's mask on: the first Linux build's command read `SigBlk 0x4002`, so a stop's SIGTERM never reached it and only the SIGKILL ended it; `sys::new_session` empties it, "asks a stopped installer to end with SIGTERM first" pins it).
  - Differences: Windows' tree is a job object (`sys::ProcessTree`, 4b's), not the PowerShell Toolhelp sweep with taskkill behind it: the command starts `CREATE_SUSPENDED`, joins its job, and only then runs (`sys::resume_process`, its thread found in a Toolhelp32 thread snapshot because std keeps the thread handle); a stop ends the job at once and is answered once the job is empty (at most 15 s), so a stop starts no `powershell.exe` or `taskkill.exe` and nothing born during it escapes. The job is kill-on-close unless the spec is `isolated` (Windows' isolated spec now says so): main's libuv puts the supervisor in its own kill-on-close job, so an app that crashes ends the supervisor before it reads its stdin's end, and the command's whole tree now ends with it; the Node supervisor ended only the command there (its own libuv job) and left its descendants running. Found on the first real run: with a job that outlived the supervisor, killing the parent left the installer and its grandchild running; with kill-on-close all three were gone 51 ms after the kill. So a non-isolated command's descendant that outlives it now also ends when the supervisor exits on Windows, as POSIX's group kill already did. An isolated command's job outlives the supervisor (ai-memory's service host stays; only a stop ends that tree). A bare program name is found by std (the command's PATH, then the system folders), not libuv (the current folder, then PATH); every caller passes an absolute path or a PATH name. The log's bytes pass as they are (node decoded UTF-8, an invalid byte as U+FFFD). A GUI command gets no `SW_HIDE` (std sets only `CREATE_NO_WINDOW`; every supervised command is a console program). A spec that cannot be read exits 127 with a line on stderr (node threw, exit 1). On Windows the supervisor no longer reacts to Ctrl+C (it has no console).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, this machine, a supervised `ping`, five runs each): the Electron-as-Node supervisor's working set 35.5–38.2 MB (private 22.6–25.3 MB) and a stop answered in 555–703 ms (2.7 s for the first, PowerShell cold); `ompdesk-core --supervise` 6.8–7.5 MB (private 1.2 MB), a stop answered in 3–5 ms. Linux (WSL, release build): VmRSS 4.0 MB. The release core with the mode is 6 449 664 bytes on Windows; its size before the mode was not measured. No new crate.
  - Verified on Windows: `setup/runner.test.ts` (16 cases; "kills what the installer starts while it is being stopped" replaces the taskkill-gap case, "ends the installer's whole tree with the supervisor, however the supervisor ends" is new, the two cases of a broken taskkill or PowerShell tested the deleted sweep and are gone), `setup/service.test.ts`, `schedules.test.ts`, `ai-memory/service.test.ts`, `worktree-seed.test.ts` (its Windows hook case; the POSIX hook cases run in CI) and `autopilot-land-check.test.ts`, against the staged release core; a real run where the parent holding the pipe is killed (supervisor, installer and grandchild gone in 51 ms) and a stop answering 130. POSIX under WSL Linux against a release build: a stop (130, the group gone), the parent SIGKILLed, SIGTERM to the supervisor, a command ignoring SIGTERM (SIGKILLed, 130 after 2.05 s), one trapping it (its trap ran, 130 at once), exit 3 with its output, a command killed by a signal (1), the log redirect (file removed), an input file, a shell command, a missing program and a missing input file (127). `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` for the host, `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` and `aarch64-apple-darwin`. No Rust unit test: the vitest suites run the real binary.
  - The repository lock's model (`formal/repo-lock-core`) covers `main.rs` and `sys.rs` whole: the mode returns before the core joins its job, and `kill_on_close` only moved its `CreateJobObjectW` into `new_job`; no modelled step changed. `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core`: 13 of 13 mutations detected, every `@code` tag resolves, DRIFT on `main.rs`, `sys.rs` and on `run.rs`, `server.rs` from 4a–4c (`run_kept`, registrations, ssh's `close_all`: none a modelled step), stamped after the commit.
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run the parity files against their real core; `mac-native` now runs `setup/runner.test.ts` (and already `worktree-seed.test.ts`), so the macOS group kill is exercised.
- **5c. The model setup research run** (`crates/core/src/models/`; was the logic of `src/main/model-setup-runner.ts`): the omp the assistant's "Find more evidence" runs (`omp --mode rpc --no-session --model <research model> --thinking auto --tools web_search,read --no-extensions --no-skills --no-rules --no-lsp --no-title`), the two frames it is sent (`set_event_filter`, `prompt`), the reading of its event stream (`stream.rs`, was `ResearchStream`: the latest tool call for the progress line, the last assistant reply, the `prompt_result` that ends it), the budget, the stop, and the omp's end once the run is over (stdin closed, SIGTERM 5 s later). `models.setupRun {run, bin, env, cwd, researchModel, prompt, timeoutMs}` answers once the omp started; the run then reports by events, `models.setupProgress {run, activity}` per tool call and `models.setupDone {run, reply}` or `{run, error, stderr?, cancelled?}` once, and `models.setupStop {run}` asks it to end (`stopped: false` when its outcome is already told). The run is not a held request, so a quit never waits on it: the core ends with its input and the omp with the core (`kill_on_drop`, the job object on Windows, the process group main kills on POSIX), besides stdin's end, which alone ends a stock omp.
  - Main hands how to run with the request, as for git and gh (`ModelSetupRunner.run`): the omp `benchmarkOmp` resolved, the whole environment (`ompPathEnv`, `PI_NOTIFICATIONS=off`, `OMP_TELEMETRY_OFF` over it), `os.tmpdir()` as the folder and `MODEL_SETUP_TIMEOUT_MS` (`src/shared/model-setup.ts`, the page's constant) as the budget. `spawnCommand`/`spawnArgs`/`needsShell` are not used for it: a `.cmd` omp (npm's shim, the tests' fake) is started by Rust's standard library as a batch file, as the `.cmd` stand-ins of 4d and 4e are.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: the thin client `ModelSetupRunner` with the signatures `machine-services.ts` and the tests call (`run`, `status`, `abort`, `running`; one run per runner, `model_setup_running`), which names each run and keeps the latest activity for `status()`; the request's check (`ModelSetupRequestSchema`), the prompt (`buildModelSetupPrompt`) and the plan read out of the reply (`parseModelSetupPlan`, `model_setup_no_plan` with the reply's first 4 000 units), all in `src/shared/model-setup.ts`, the one copy the page and a paired host share; the IPC and `host.modelSetup.*`.
  - No formal model of its own (the unit plan names none): one task owns a run and tells its end once; the one order that could lose a stop (a stop the core handles before the start it names, since requests run concurrently) cannot occur, because the client sends the stop only after the start was answered and the core lists the run before it answers. `model-setup-runner.test.ts` "cancels a run stopped before the core answered its start" holds it. `formal/repo-lock-core` covers `server.rs` and `run.rs` whole: this unit added the module's registration line and made `run.rs` `terminate` crate-visible. `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core`: 7 of 7 runs as expected, 13 of 13 mutations detected, `@code` tags resolve, DRIFT on `run.rs` and `server.rs` alone, stamped after the commit.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: rows captured from the TypeScript before it was deleted (`models/captured.json`): 93 streams of 243 lines with the activity and outcome after each line (the test file's two cases; every field with a wrong type, `null` or absent, as the zod schema refused or took it; ids, statuses and stop reasons; text, array and mixed content; details trimmed as JavaScript trims, at 200 and 201 UTF-16 units and through a surrogate pair; blank lines, lines that are no JSON, a BOM, a carriage return, two `type` keys), the argv for three model names and the stdin bytes for two requests (quotes, a backslash, control characters, U+2028, Korean, an emoji in the prompt). Two mutations of the port fail the table (the cut at 201 units; a wrong-typed optional field read as absent). `model-setup-runner.test.ts` runs its `ModelSetupRunner` cases through the process unchanged (plan, progress, a second run refused, stdin closed, the argv and stdin frames the omp saw, cancel, an invalid request, a reply without a plan) plus the case above; its `ResearchStream`, `modelSetupArgs` and `modelSetupInput` cases are the Rust table. Rust tests over a stand-in omp (a shell script, a `.cmd` on Windows): the reply with its progress and stdin's end, an early exit (`omp exited (3) before the research finished: boom`), the budget (`Model research timed out after … minutes`, the minutes of the budget main hands), a stop, a missing binary (`spawn <bin> ENOENT`).
  - Differences. Windows, a `.cmd` omp: the system folder's cmd.exe runs it with each argument escaped for cmd.exe (measured through the shim: `local/a%PATH%b`, `local/q"uote`, `local/amp&echo;x^y|z`, Korean with an emoji and a trailing backslash arrive as written), where node's shell spawn (`ComSpec`, `/d /s /c`) only wrapped each in quotes, so cmd.exe expanded a `%NAME%`. A run whose omp exited while something it started still holds its pipes is reported 2 s after the exit (the TypeScript waited for the pipes to close, without a bound). An omp that ignores both its stdin's end and the SIGTERM 5 s after its run ended is killed 2 s later (the TypeScript left it). stderr is kept by bytes (its first 64 KiB; was 65 536 UTF-16 units), a character cut there reads U+FFFD. Half a surrogate pair in a frame or the prompt reads as U+FFFD (a progress detail cut through a pair at 199 units ends in U+FFFD where JavaScript kept the lone half); a frame only JavaScript reads as JSON (nested past 128 levels, a number past a double's range) is skipped. `startedAt` is the moment the request is sent (was right after the spawn). An `abort()` issued before the core answered the start is sent once it has. A start that fails is a `CoreError` (`spawn_failed`) with node's message, which is still the result's `error`. A core that ends takes the run with it: the run fails with `ompdesk-core exited before the research finished` when the next core starts, reads cancelled when a stop finds no run, and main stops waiting 10 s past the budget (`ompdesk-core did not report the end of the research`).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, this machine): `cargo test -p ompdesk-core` 243 passed; `model-setup-runner.test.ts` 5 of 5 against the staged binary through the `.cmd` fake omp (3.8 s, 3.5 s of it the first case's core and bun start). Not measured: speed against the TypeScript (the model and the web bound a run), the binary's size change (`ompdesk-core.exe` is 6 750 208 bytes with 6a in it). No new crate: `Cargo.lock` and the notices are unchanged.
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run `model-setup-runner.test.ts` against the real core (the `.cmd` shim, cmd.exe and the job on Windows); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes; `mac-native` lists the file (`.github/actions/native-parity`). Developed on Windows: the POSIX paths (SIGTERM, the shell stand-ins, the process group) ran only in CI.
- **5b. Agent browsers** (`crates/core/src/browsers/`; was the Chromium logic of `src/main/agent-browsers.ts`): finding the Chromium browsers in their install places (`browsers.find`), whether a profile of each loaded its own copy of the omp Browser Relay extension and whether that copy's relay is connected (`browsers.status`), writing the copy bound to the browser's own port (`browsers.installRelay`: stock's `omp browser-relay install` into a staging folder, the one `DEFAULT_PORT` declaration of `background.js` and `options.js` rebound, the files copied over the copy one by one, the staging folder removed) and opening the browser at its extensions page (`browsers.openExtensions`). The relay probe is a hand-written loopback HTTP/1.1 `GET /json/version` (no crate): connected when the status line within 500 ms says 200.
  - Main hands over what it also spells for the page: the browsers in `auto` order with their names and relay ports (`src/shared/default-browser.ts`), the places (`systemBrowserPlaces`: home, `/Applications`, `%ProgramFiles%`, `%ProgramFiles(x86)%`, `%LOCALAPPDATA%`), and how omp runs (its binary and `ompRunEnv`, now the one builder `runOmp` uses too).
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: finding `ego-browser` (`resolveEgoBrowser`: `spawnPath` + `findOnPath`, which omp's own lookup shares, 4c); the answer held for the launch and the reprobe's `changed` (`agentBrowserStatus`, `reprobeAgentBrowserStatus`: a promise every spawn of `sidecar-factory.ts` shares, with ego's answer joined in main); `resolveAgentBrowser`'s pref rule (`src/shared`).
  - **The core's job allows breakaway** (owner's decision A, 12 Nov 2026). `ompdesk-core` lives in a kill-on-close job on Windows, so a browser it started that was not running would have become the person's browser inside that job and been killed when OMPdesk quit or the core restarted; Electron main, which started it before, is in no job. The core's own job (`sys.rs` `end_children_with_this_process`, `kill_on_close(true)`) now also sets `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_BREAKAWAY_OK`, and `browsers.openExtensions` is the one start that passes `CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB` (with `DETACHED_PROCESS | CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP`, node's `detached`); every other child still dies with the job. A `ProcessTree` (4b's per-ssh job, 6a's supervised runs) never allows breakaway, so nothing escapes a tree a stop must end. Behaviour change: a descendant of the core outside a tree (a git hook, gh) that asks for breakaway itself now leaves the job instead of failing to start. When a job around OMPdesk forbids breakaway the start fails (`spawn <exe> <code>`, as a failed start read before) and is never retried inside the job. Measured on Windows 11 (this machine, 12 Nov 2026, throwaway script against the release core): Edge not running, `browsers.openExtensions` started it (0 → 9 `msedge.exe`), a `browsers.installRelay` whose omp was `ping -n 120` ran beside it (1 `ping.exe`); the core was killed: Edge kept its 9 processes, the `ping` was gone. Not verified: the refusal under an outer job that forbids breakaway (no test makes one).
  - **The core's pipes stay its own** (owner's decision B). Rust's std starts every Windows process with handle inheritance on, and the core's stdin, stdout and stderr are main's inheritable pipes, so the broken-away browser inherited them: measured before the fix, a killed core gave main `exit` at once but never `close` while that Edge ran (10 s and on), and `CoreProcess` notices a dead core only on `close`, so pending requests ran to their timeout and the next request went to the dead core. `sys.rs` `stdio_not_inherited` clears `HANDLE_FLAG_INHERIT` on the three std handles, once, before the first browser start (`std::sync::Once` in `browsers/mod.rs`); the browser's own stdio is NUL. Every other child is unaffected: a piped or null child never used these handles, and std makes a duplicate inheritable for one spawn when a child asks for `Stdio::inherit`. Measured after (same machine, release core): Edge not running; the core opened it and then answered `git.repoRoot` through git with piped stdio; killed, main got `close` 5 ms later; through `CoreProcess`, a pending request ended `core_exited` 5 ms after the kill and the next request answered from a new core; Edge kept running (8 processes). Pinned by "starts the browser outside the core's job and off its pipes" in `agent-browsers.test.ts` (Windows only: node as the browser outlives a killed core, the pending request ends `core_exited`, the next answers from a new core).
  - macOS (from reading, not run here): `open -a` asks LaunchServices, so launchd starts the browser, never the core or `open`; the browser is outside the core's process group that main kills, and inherits nothing of the core. `open` itself runs through `run.rs` with its stdin null and its own stdout and stderr pipes; the core's fds 0 to 2 are replaced in the child and every other fd Rust opens is close-on-exec.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 30 `bindExtensionPort` rows (every keyword, `\b` against ASCII and non-ASCII neighbours, none and several declarations, digits past 2^53) and 41 profile layouts (`Local State` missing, not JSON, with a BOM, `info_cache` an object, an array, a string, null, empty; `Preferences` and `Secure Preferences`; a path followed by `-`, `_`, a digit, `/`, `.`, a non-ASCII letter; Windows paths raw and JSON-escaped, quotes, control characters, Korean and an emoji) captured from the TypeScript on Windows give the same answers (`browsers/captured.json`). `agent-browsers.test.ts` runs its 15 cases through the process: the probe seam (a JavaScript function) became loopback relays of the test with ports handed in (`detectChromiumBrowsers(places, ports)`), the omp seam a stand-in executable the core runs, and the `relayLoadedIn` cases go through `browsers.status`; `bindExtensionPort`'s two cases are rows of the table.
  - Differences: a relay that answers a redirect is not connected (`fetch` followed it); an answer whose status line takes over 500 ms is not connected, as before, but a body never matters. Places are joined as main sent them, not normalized (`/a//b` stays). omp's install is run as Rust's std runs a `.cmd` (4a), its stderr kept to 64 KiB and stdout to 4 MiB, its stdin closed where `runOmp` gave it an empty pipe; past 30 s it is asked to end, killed 2 s later and waited for before the request fails with the same `omp browser-relay timed out after 30000ms`. A failure is a `CoreError` with the same message (`omp_exit`, `omp_timeout`, `relay_changed`, `spawn_failed`, `open_failed`, `io` with node's file error text). The opened browser gets the environment the core started with, not main's of that moment. On macOS `open` is ended after 30 s (`execFile` had no limit) and a failure reads `Command failed: open -a <app> <url>\n<stderr>` as node's did. Each call has a deadline in main (30 s default, 37 s for the install and the open).
  - `repo-lock-core` covers `sys.rs` and `server.rs` whole: `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core` gives 7 of 7 runs as expected, 13 of 13 mutations detected, `@code` tags resolve, DRIFT on those two files only (rebased on 6a's restamp `7c01fa8e`); not stamped here. The model's Windows assumption (the job ends a dead core's gits) still holds for every git: none asks for breakaway.
  - Not measured: speed (file stats and small reads; the relay probe's 500 ms bounds a status read). CI coverage: `check` and `win-platform` run `agent-browsers.test.ts` against the real core (a `.cmd` stand-in omp on Windows, a shell script elsewhere); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes. `mac-native`'s list does not name it.
- **5d. The ai-memory client, and the core's HTTP stack** (`crates/core/src/aimemory/`, `crates/core/src/http.rs`; was `src/main/ai-memory/client.ts`): everything the app asks the local ai-memory server is an `aimemory.*` method of the core, 33 of them, one per question the TypeScript client answered (`token`, `healthy`, `status`, `projects`, `nameStatus`, `renameUnclaimedProject`, `search`, `pages`, `allPages`, `overview`, `sessions`, `observations`, `profile`, `lint`, `sweep`, `writePage`, `writeSessionPage`, `createPage`, `deletePage`, `headCheckpoint`, `restorePage`, `feedback`, `cancelHandoff`, `recent`, `recentEverywhere`, `handoffs`, `page`, `retirePage`, `pageCopy`, `writePageCopy`, `conclusions`, `writeConclusions`, and `serverUrl` for `serverUrlFromConfig`). The core reads the root token, does the HTTP (`/healthz`, the `/api/v1` reads, `/admin/*`, MCP `tools/call` on `/mcp`) and turns the server's JSON into the shapes of `src/shared/ai-memory.ts`. Main keeps `AiMemoryClient` with every signature (each method is one request), what it knows about the server, handed in with every request as `server` (`{url, dataDir}`, read from the service's state at the call), and `DEFAULT_SERVER_URL`, which `service.ts` reads synchronously (spelled in both; `client.test.ts` compares them through `serverUrlFromConfig(null)`). `serverUrlFromConfig` is a request now: its two callers in `service.ts` await it before their stall check, so a stopped step still writes no state. `plainSnippet` had no caller but `search`, so it moved with it.
  - One method per question, not one `memory.request` with a route: the mapping rules live with the request, so the memory units that follow (7b to 7e) call the same typed functions in process; a question of several reads (a search with its page lists, the merged lists, a delete with its checkpoint, a retirement, the profile) is one round trip; and main can ask for no route of its own making with the token on it. The names are `aimemory.*` because `memory.*` is the RAM sampler (3b).
  - **The HTTP stack, chosen once** (8a's server and the setup downloads build on it): `hyper` (`client`, `http1`: its HTTP/1 connection, no pooled client), `hyper-util` (`tokio`: the adapter from tokio's sockets) and `http-body-util`, against a hand-written client over tokio (written, tested with the same cases and measured, then deleted). Measured 12 Nov 2026, Windows x64, rustc 1.95, `bun run build:native`, `ompdesk-core.exe`: 6 893 568 bytes before (`c609c6c4`); 7 400 448 with the 33 operations and a transport that only fails; 7 429 632 with the hand-written client (+29 184 over that); 7 637 504 with hyper (+237 056), the size after this unit (+743 936, 10.8 %). `ompdesk.exe` is unchanged (410 112). hyper costs 207 872 bytes more than the hand-written client and adds 10 crates to `Cargo.lock` (79 to 89 packages): `atomic-waker`, `futures-core`, `http`, `http-body`, `http-body-util`, `httparse`, `hyper`, `hyper-util`, `try-lock`, `want`, each MIT or MIT/Apache-2.0, none with a C build; `LICENSES/ompdesk-native-dependencies.txt` lists 76 linked crates (66 before). Both candidates pass `cargo clippy --locked --all-targets -- -D warnings` for the host, `aarch64-apple-darwin` and `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` from Windows, and both gave the same requests and answers on the captured table below. hyper was taken because the web server needs a parser for input from the tailnet and the downloads need redirects and streamed bodies over TLS: with the hand-written client those units would add hyper anyway and the core would hold two HTTP implementations. Not measured: hyper-util's pooled client (`client-legacy`, not used), the size on macOS and Linux, speed (a loopback exchange; the server bounds it). TLS came after the unit (`fix(core)`, 12 Nov 2026), restoring what `fetch` did with an `https` server: `rustls` 0.23 with `ring`'s cryptography (`native/memory` already builds the same `ring` 0.17 on all three OSes, so its C/asm build was already part of this repository's builds; `aws-lc-rs`, rustls's default, is a larger C build with a cmake/nasm toolchain), `tokio-rustls` over the one connection `http.rs` dials (hyper-rustls would only add a connector for hyper-util's pooled client, which the core does not use), TLS 1.2 and 1.3, and `rustls-platform-verifier` 0.7: the certificate is checked by Windows' and macOS' own verifiers (the system store on Linux), so a machine's own authorities count as they did for Electron's Node, plus the certificates `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` names (`new_with_extra_roots`; an unreadable file is skipped with a line on stderr, as node warned). Bundled roots (`webpki-roots`) were not taken: they would refuse a company's or a self-hosted server's authority the machine trusts. Size, Windows x64, rustc 1.95, `bun run build:native`, `ompdesk-core.exe`: 8 135 168 bytes before (this tree before the fix), 9 180 672 after (+1 045 504, 12.9 %); `Cargo.lock` 89 → 161 packages (most are the verifier's per-OS crates and `rcgen`'s dev-only ones), `LICENSES/ompdesk-native-dependencies.txt` 76 → 92 linked crates. The alternatives (bundled roots; `aws-lc-rs`) were not measured: their builds in this session did not produce a binary. Cross clippy from Windows for `aarch64-apple-darwin` and `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` is not verified since: `ring` builds C for the target, which needs that target's C compiler; CI's `native` jobs run clippy and the tests natively on Linux, Windows and macOS arm64, which is the check of record.
  - Where the growth is, read from the linker map of a release build of the core alone (7 576 064 bytes, 4 848 251 of them code): symbols that name `aimemory` are 301 430 bytes of code, 97 080 of them the one dispatch future the 33 operations are inlined into, the rest their serde readers and writers (`member` and `out`, one instance per shape) and drop glue; hyper, hyper-util, http-body and http-body-util are 54 958, `http` 45 777, `httparse` 2 712. The rest of the file's growth is unwind tables and constants. No function is out of proportion: the size is the breadth of 33 field-by-field mappings (about 1 750 lines), several times what the earlier units that recorded a size added (100 to 140 KB each). The memory units that follow call these functions in process and add no second copy.
  - Privacy: the token is read by the core at each request (once per request of main, where the TypeScript read it before every HTTP exchange) and goes into the `authorization` header of the requests to the server main named, nowhere else; `/healthz` carries none. It is in no fault: the transport's failures are kinds (`http.rs` `Failure`) that hold nothing of a request (the one text, `Tls`, is rustls's account of a handshake, which ends before a header is written), and it never follows a redirect to another origin (scheme, host, port: dropped for good at the first such hop, as `fetch` drops `authorization`), and the words a server answered (`error`, an MCP error, a tool's error text) have the token replaced by `***` (8 characters or more, as 2e did for gh) before they become a message. The core logs no request, as before (2e). `aimemory.token` answers the token to main, which copies it to the clipboard as the web console's password, as it did. A token that is not visible ASCII (a line end, a control character) is never written into a request: that request reads `Memory is not running`. Proved by the Rust tests `a_fault_carries_nothing_of_the_token` (a server that says the token back in an error, a non-JSON answer, an MCP error and a tool error, a dropped connection, no server, `https`, wrong params: every fault's code and message) and `a_token_that_cannot_be_a_header_is_never_sent`, and by `client.test.ts` "reports nothing of the token when the server says it back, fails or is gone" through the process (every message and every line the core logged).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 132 scenarios captured from the TypeScript client against a scripted `fetch` (every method; sparse, `null` and wrongly typed answers; non-2xx with and without an `error` text; the MCP error forms; names that need encoding; 14 projects merged with one failing; the retirement's bounds in UTF-16 units; a copy whose metadata names the body's own members) give the same requests (method, path and query as the WHATWG URL parser sends them, `authorization`, `content-type`, `accept`, JSON body) and the same answers or messages (`aimemory/captured.json`, replayed against a server on 127.0.0.1 by `sends_and_answers_what_the_typescript_client_did`); 24 configs for `serverUrlFromConfig` (JavaScript's `\s` and line starts, an unclosed quote, a value across lines) and the `<mark>` cases. The order of projects that share a time is kept: the comparator never answered 0, so V8's steps decided it, and `sort_as_v8` follows them for fewer than 64 projects (88 lists of 0 to 63 projects with equal and missing times captured). `client.test.ts` runs its cases through the process against a stand-in server on 127.0.0.1 (`fake-server-test-rig.ts`, which takes the routes each case wrote for `fetch`); its `plainSnippet` case is a Rust test. `service.test.ts` now replaces `healthy` and `status` of the client (a subclass) where it scripted `fetch`, so its timing cases do not depend on a round trip; `sync.test.ts` and `backfill.test.ts` are unchanged.
  - Differences in the transport: redirects are followed as `fetch` followed them (up to 20, the 21st reads `Memory is not running`; 301/302 to a POST and 303 go on as a GET without body and `content-type`, 307/308 keep both; a 3xx without `location` is the answer); every request is its own connection (`connection: close`; `fetch` kept connections alive); a request carries `host`, `connection`, `authorization`, `content-type`, `content-length` and, for MCP, `accept`, without `fetch`'s own headers (`accept: */*`, `accept-language`, `sec-fetch-mode`, `user-agent`, `accept-encoding`), so no answer is compressed; an `https` server works as through `fetch`, and a TLS failure (a certificate the machine does not trust, one for another name, no TLS on the other side) reads `Memory's TLS connection failed: <rustls's words>` (code `memory_tls`) where `fetch` said `fetch failed`; a URL with credentials reads `Memory is not running` without a connection; `fetch`'s list of blocked ports is gone (a server on port 6000 answers); an answer past 64 MiB reads `Memory is not running` (no bound before). The times are the same (2 s for `/healthz`, 10 s a read, 60 s a write, each over the whole exchange) and are measured in the core; main waits 80 s for the core, then fails with `ompdesk-core did not answer aimemory.<name> in time` and cancels the request, which closes its connections.
  - Differences in answers and errors: a failure is a `CoreError` with the same message and a code (`memory_down`, `memory_tls`, `memory_refused` for a non-2xx, `memory_tool`, `memory_json`, `memory_no_checkpoint`, `memory_handoff_closed`, `memory_unknown_status`, `invalid_params`) where it was a plain `Error`. A 2xx answer that is no JSON fails in serde's words where V8 wrote `Unexpected token …`, and one cut short reads `Memory is not running` where `fetch` said `terminated`. `healthy`, `status` and `token` still never reject: a core that cannot answer reads false, null and null. When both reads of `profile` fail, the status read's error is the one reported (it was whichever failed first). A row of a list carries only its own fields, where `...project` also copied whatever else the caller's project object held (a project's page count onto each of its merged recent pages and handoffs). From 64 projects on, projects of one time stay in the server's order. `page` reads an answer of `null` as an empty page (a `TypeError` before). `writePageCopy` with `expected` left out writes `create_only` (neither member before; every caller passes it). Arguments main cannot put in JSON (`NaN`, an invalid `Date`) arrive as `null`: an offset reads 0, a retirement's time is `invalid_params`. `deletedAt` is the core's clock. JSON nested deeper than 128 levels is refused, half a surrogate pair reads as U+FFFD, and integer-like keys of `relations` keep the server's order (as 1 and 2e).
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` (`bunx vitest run src/main/ai-memory`) run `client.test.ts` against the real core; `mac-native`'s list names it; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes. `server.rs` gained its `register` line, which `repo-lock-core` covers whole.
- **7a. Scheduled runs** (`crates/core/src/schedules/`; was `ScheduleService` and `ompRunLauncher` of `src/main/schedules.ts`, and `nextRunAt` of `src/shared/schedules.ts`): the check every `SCHEDULE_TICK_MS` (a tokio interval, the system's clock read at each check), which schedule starts when (`rules.rs` `next_run_at`), the journal `schedules.json` (`JSON.stringify(…, null, 2)`'s text, written in one step by a thread per service with `disk/tree.rs`' new `write_atomic_if`) and its reading at launch, the run records with their limits, the run logs' removal and tail read, the routines' events (kept while a run goes, one more run after it, the loop guard), `once` and the agents' aged-out schedules, the webhook key (24 bytes of `sys::random` as base64url) and its constant-time check (`engine.rs` `same_key`, hand-written, no crate), and a run's omp under the supervisor (`launch.rs`: the core starts `ompdesk-core --supervise` itself, omp's stdout and stderr the log file, a session of its own on POSIX so main's kill of the core's group never takes a supervisor before it ended omp's tree). `engine.rs` is the state machine and touches nothing: every entry takes its moment, and what happens outside is a list its host (`mod.rs`) carries out.
  - Methods `schedules.open` (main names the service), `command`, `create`, `fire`, `webhook`, `triggerFailed`, `prune`, `log`, `close`, `closed` (held by main: its failure says the core died), `launch`, `launchStop`; `tick` only for a service opened `manual`. Events for a run's start and end: `schedules.start` (main starts it) answered by `schedules.started` and `schedules.ended`, `schedules.stop`, `schedules.launchEnded`; `schedules.state` every change; `schedules.reserve`/`schedules.refund` answered by `schedules.reserved`. `close_all` (two lines in `server.rs`, as control and ssh) closes what is open when the input ends, so the held `closed` never keeps the core alive and a main that died still leaves the runs stopped in the journal.
  - **The synchronous readers** (the unit plan left them undetermined; read first): `routines/triggers.ts` reads `state()` inside `onState`; no source reads what `fire` or `triggerFailed` answer; `web/server.ts` calls `webhook` in an async handler; `ipc.ts` calls `prune` for nothing; `index.ts` calls `dispose` at quit; `agent-control/host.ts` and `machine-services.ts` only await `command`, `create`, `readLog`. So main keeps a copy of the state written only from `schedules.state` (3a's pattern), which `state()` answers; a state event is sent before the answer of the request that caused it and before the run's start it records, so a reader never sees less than the caller was told.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: the client `ScheduleService` with its options as they were (`launch`, `wake`, `reserve`, `onState`, `tickMs`, `minuteMs`); `SCHEDULE_TICK_MS` (`routines/git.ts` reads it); `cleanDraft` (what `String(…)`, `Number(…)` and `=== true` make of whatever the page, an agent or a paired device sent, before the core checks it); what only main has, which the core asks for: a paired device's charge (`reserve`: `PeerQuota` lives on main's device records), a session's wake-up (`wake`: the control tools) and a run's omp, its environment and its folder's extensions (`ompRunLauncher` resolves `systemOmp`, the shell and proxy environment, ai-memory's root and the MCP hub at each start and hands them to `schedules.launch` with the supervisor's path and `spawnCommand`/`spawnArgs`/`needsShell` as before); the limits (`SCHEDULE_RUNS_KEPT`, `SCHEDULE_RUNS_PER_SCHEDULE`, `SCHEDULE_ONCE_DONE_KEPT`, `ROUTINE_CHAIN_LIMIT`, `AGENT_SCHEDULE_PREFIX`: one spelling in `src/shared`, handed over at open). `scheduleDraftProblem` stays in `src/shared` for the Settings dialog and has a copy in `rules.rs` (the core reads the journal and saves drafts without main); both are held to the same 170 captured rows (`src/shared/schedules.test.ts`).
  - **Order**: the core runs requests side by side, so the client sends one request after the other (`#send`): two creates, or an event and the end of the run it came during, are taken in the order main made them. Answers the core waits for (`reserved`) go at once.
  - **The clock in tests**: `ScheduleServiceOptions.now` (only a test hands it in) opens the service `manual`: the core starts no timer and reads no clock, the client checks every `tickMs` and says the time with each request, so vitest's fake `Date` and `setInterval` drive the real core. `schedules.test.ts` "counts a minute by minuteMs and checks every tickMs" runs on the core's own timer and clock, as does `e2e/routines.e2e.ts`.
  - Kept exactly, with tests (`schedules/captured.json`, captured from the TypeScript at `c609c6c4` before it was deleted): 26 scripted scenarios of 1 117 steps (every case of `schedules.test.ts`, and: a device's routine charged and refused, events kept and dropped by off, pause, delete and an edit to another trigger, 23 one-time schedules past the 20 kept, 230 runs against the 200 kept, a daily schedule over a week, a relaunch) replayed on the engine with the state after each of the 256 recorded steps and each journal equal as text, key order included; 10 journals as a launch finds them (not JSON, a list, a schedule without a name, invalid ones among valid ones with members no rule names, 205 runs); 1 128 `nextRunAt` rows in four zones without clock changes; 188 `Date.parse` rows; 11 `toISOString` rows; 170 `scheduleDraftProblem` rows with the nine the JavaScript threw over. `schedules.test.ts` (29 cases), `web/webhook.test.ts` (10), `routines/triggers.test.ts` (12) and `routines/git.test.ts` (7) run through the real core; their cases are unchanged but for `await` before `fire`, `webhook`, `triggerFailed`, `prune` and `dispose`, the `now` option in the harnesses, and `settled` waiting for a run to exist.
  - Differences. `fire`, `webhook`, `triggerFailed` and `prune` answer promises with the core's word, `SourceContext.fire` answers nothing (no source read it), `dispose` answers a promise that settles once the core answered that the journal is written (the close goes in its turn, behind what was asked before the quit) or after `QUIT_SCHEDULES_CLOSE_MS` (8 s); the quit holds the core's stop and the process's exit until then (`index.ts`, `installQuitGuard`'s `settled`), on every platform, so a change made as the app quits is in the journal as it was when `dispose` wrote synchronously (`schedules.test.ts` "at a quit"; measured on Windows with a process that exits right after the teardown: 2 of 20 saves answered before the quit and 20 of 20 still on their way were missing before, none of 40 after). A paired device's start is taken once main answered its charge (one round trip): among schedules due in one check it is recorded after the others, a check that falls in between neither calls it skipped nor asks again, and a schedule deleted meanwhile gets the request back. Runs that come due in one check start in the schedules' order (the TypeScript started them in whatever order their folders' `mkdir` finished). A run's files are made before the next request is taken, so a start that fails on them fails before an event of the same burst arrives (the TypeScript kept that event for one more run and counted it toward the loop guard). The journal holds the newest state only (a state superseded before it was written is skipped); a write that fails is a line on the core's stderr. A moment in a spelling other than ECMAScript's date-time format (and `t`, `z`, an offset without its colon, which V8 also took) is no moment: a one-time schedule saved with one is refused and one in a journal is left out, where V8's fallback parser guessed (`2026-1-5`); the agents' tool and the dialog write `toISOString`. A daily schedule's local time comes from the system's zone through `localtime_r`/`mktime` (POSIX) and kernel32's zone calls (Windows; never the C runtime, which reads Git Bash's `TZ=Asia/Seoul` as UTC), not ICU: a time inside the hour a clock change skips reads an hour later on Windows as JavaScript read it, and as `mktime` decides on POSIX. Half a surrogate pair (a detail cut at 500 units, a journal's escape) is U+FFFD. A journal nested deeper than 128 levels reads as empty; a number in it keeps its spelling (`1.0`). omp writes its log itself (the file is its stdout and stderr), so a log that fails mid-run is omp's write error, where main stopped piping and went on. A core that dies takes its timers and its omps with it: the service opens again on the next core from the journal, those runs read stopped, a woken session's turn goes on unfollowed, and a request made in between fails. A refusal of the service is a plain `Error` with the same words; a core that does not answer is a `CoreError` after 30 s. A save that names an unknown schedule and carries no draft fails on the draft first. `state()` answers the copy itself, not a fresh one.
  - Not verified: the POSIX paths (`localtime_r`/`mktime`, the supervisor's own session, a core's death there) ran only in CI; a zone with clock changes against V8 (no row has one); the quit's hold in the packaged app (the built app's relaunch spec passes; the loss itself was measured with a stand-in process). Not measured: speed (a check is a map lookup per schedule; a save is one round trip and one file write, as before) and the core's size before this unit (`ompdesk-core.exe` was 7 242 240 bytes with it, 7 979 520 once rebased on 5d's crates). No new crate: `Cargo.lock` and the notices are unchanged.
  - No formal model of its own (the unit plan names none). `formal/repo-lock-core` covers `server.rs` and `sys.rs` whole: this unit added the module's registration, `close_all`'s two calls and the zone calls at the end of `sys.rs`, none a modelled step. `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core`: its runs as expected, 13 of 13 mutations detected, `@code` tags resolve, DRIFT on those two files, stamped after the commit.
- **5a. The omp pid ledger** (`crates/core/src/omp/`; was the file, the identity and the leftover's stop of `src/main/omp-processes.ts`, 356 lines to 104, and `stopOmpPid` of `omp-stop.ts`): the omps this app runs are named in `<userData>/omp-pids.json` by the core. `omp.track {file, pid}` records a spawn (on Windows with the process's creation time, read then), `omp.exited {file, pid}` takes an exit out, `omp.reap {file, bridgeDir, graceMs, killWaitMs}` → `{stopped}` is the launch's stop of what an earlier instance left. The file is the whole state: each request reads it, changes it and writes it back (`disk/tree.rs` `write_atomic`), one at a time, so a core that started again goes on from it. `omp-processes.test.ts` runs through the process.
  - The first step's answer: 3d does not do the reap's work. Its sweep ends the processes of a tag whose session did not come back, once the saved tabs exist; a session that comes back keeps its tag, and so its stuck omp, and the reap has to be over before any omp starts (`holdUntil`). Nothing of `reapLeftovers` was deleted for it.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why. `OmpProcesses` is the client: `track(child)` tells the core the spawn and, at the child's exit, the exit, in order and awaited by nothing; `reapLeftovers()` is the one request. **The quit's stop of every omp stays whole in main (`stopAll`, no `omp.stopAll` in the core)**: the stop of one omp is `stopOmp` (`omp-stop.ts`, which moves with the sidecar manager), and it needs the child process main spawned, for the stdin whose end is how an omp is asked on Windows and for the stderr on which an omp asks not to be killed while it saves a login (`EXIT_HOLD`, up to 130 s). A core that stopped by pid beside it would kill such an omp at its own grace, send POSIX a second SIGTERM and end an ai-memory session before its stdin grace; a core that only waited would be a second, less reliable wait than the promises main already holds. `QUIT_OMP_STOP_MS`, `STOP_GRACE_MS` and `KILL_WAIT_MS` are main's and handed in.
