  - Deleted, not ported: the PowerShell lookup (`windowsStartTimes`), its retries (`IDENTIFY_RETRY_DELAYS_MS`, the identify loop), the `ps` lookup, `taskkill` for a leftover, `stopOmpPid`, `pidAlive` (the test has its own).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 86 rows captured from the TypeScript with its lookups faked (an entry with and without `started`, what the lookup showed of its pid, whether its stop left it gone → asked, reported, kept) and 23 file texts (damaged, other shapes, `started` of another type, `7e3`, a fraction) are Rust tests of `judge`, `settle`, `parse` and `render` (`omp/captured.json`). The creation time the core records equals the one PowerShell's `Get-Process` printed, so a file the TypeScript wrote still compares (6 of 6 processes, measured).
  - **Found by the run on the built app, and fixed here (a change of behaviour, not a port)**: on Windows the reap never stopped a dead app's omp. The recorded pid is the shim (`omp.cmd`'s cmd.exe, bun's `omp.exe`), which Windows ends with the app (node keeps its children in a job closed with it), while the real omp behind it runs on: after a kill the shim was gone and the omp alive in 16 of 16 runs, the old rule found the pid free, stopped nothing and dropped the entry in 9 of 9, and the leftover went only when the session sweep took it (in 1 of 9 nothing ended it within 60 s; cause not found). `omp.reap` now looks for what a gone shim left: a process whose parent id is the recorded pid, started no earlier than the shim, that no later holder of the pid started (`procs` `orphans`, 3d's rule that a process older than its parent is not its child), and whose command line holds the gui-bridge path. It gets `graceMs` to end by itself (its stdin ended with the app) and is then ended with its tree; `stopped` names it. After: an `omp-reap` line naming the real omp in 7 of 7 runs.
  - Differences: Windows reads the creation time in the request that records the spawn (1.6 ms median) where one batched PowerShell ran after it (570 ms median here, tried again for up to 15.5 s); an omp already gone then gets no entry, where it was listed without `started` until its exit. The lookup's log lines (`creation-time lookup failed`, `command-line lookup failed`, `gave-up`) are gone with the lookups; the one warning is `leftover omps not checked` (`cause` `timeout` or `error`) when the core does not answer the reap, which then stops nothing and holds the starts at most 15 s (`REAP_TIMEOUT_MS`). POSIX reads the command line as 3d does (`KERN_PROCARGS2`, `/proc/<pid>/cmdline`, the arguments joined by a space) where `ps -ww -o command=` ran: a process whose arguments cannot be read (another user's, a zombie) stays in the file as unresolved, where `ps` showed it and it left as another program's; and the command line is read again before each signal. A live Windows leftover is ended through handles opened on it and on what it started (no `taskkill`, so no WMI), and counts as gone once all of them exited, where the pid alone was asked. The file is 0600 (was the umask's default), is written within the request instead of on a deferred retry, an entry whose pid no process can have (0, negative, past 2^32) names no omp, a spawn replaces an older entry of its pid, and a reap drops the entries whose pid is gone even when none still runs (the TypeScript left them until the next spawn rewrote the file). `stopAll` also waits, inside the same bound, until the core took the exits out of the file. An empty `bridgeDir` is refused (it is in every command line). A leftover still saving a login when the app quit past its bound is ended by a launch within that time on Windows too now, as it already was on POSIX.
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, the machine loaded by other sessions' builds). `omp.track` 1.6 ms median of 20 (1.3 to 8.4); the PowerShell it replaces 570 ms median of 5 (447 to 1 590). `omp.reap` 0.3 ms median of 20 with an empty file, 51 ms with one live leftover and its child, two strangers, an unrecorded entry and a free pid. The built app with a fake omp that ignores its stdin's end: the leftover's tree was gone 1.4 to 4.0 s after the next launch began (7 runs; the sweep and the reap's grace ran together there, since the session did not come back, so the sweep ended the processes and both reported them: the reap's own kill after the grace is the vitest and Rust cases'). A quit with that stuck omp reached `app.exit` 5.4 to 5.6 s after it began in 4 of 4 runs of the final code (`QUIT_OMP_STOP_MS` is 12 s), the file reading `{"omps":[]}`; earlier builds of this unit took 5.5 to 6.6 s in 8 runs and once the whole 12 s (cause not found). The Windows core staged on this unit's base (before 5d) was 6 987 264 bytes with it; the base's own size was not measured.
  - Tests. vitest keeps its three cases on real processes (POSIX quit, POSIX launch, Windows launch) and gains four: the omp behind a shim that died (Windows), a quit with an omp that ends with its stdin and one that does not (Windows), the file at each spawn and exit, a reap the core refuses. Gone with the code they tested: the three identify-retry cases, the launch whose lookup failed, the `ps` failures and the five `windowsStartTimes` cases. Their rules that remain (no usable time, another time, another command line, a stop the leftover survived) are the captured rows. Rust: a leftover with its tree and no other process, the omp a dead shim left and no other orphan (Windows); a leftover that ends when asked, one that ignores SIGTERM and a stranger (Linux, macOS); `orphans` over a reused pid.
  - `repo-lock-core` covers `server.rs` and `sys.rs` whole: `verify-all.ts formal --only repo-lock-core` passes its runs and 13 of 13 mutations, every `@code` tag resolves, and the drift since 7a's stamp was this unit's alone: the `omp::register` line, `alive` and `process_started_ms`, none near the job object, a request's scope or a git's signals. Stamped at `a90e8369`. No model of its own: one lock orders the file's requests and the client tells an exit after its spawn.
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run `omp-processes.test.ts` against the real core, and so does `mac-native` (its list names it); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes. Developed on Windows, the Unix code run under WSL Linux (Rust tests, clippy); macOS's `KERN_PROCARGS2` read of a leftover runs first in CI. Not verified: bun's `omp.exe` shim (the cases use a `.cmd` one; that it starts the real omp as its child is inferred), and the built app on POSIX.
- **6b. The stock helper's host** (`crates/core/src/stock/`; was `StockHelper` of `src/main/stock-helper.ts`): the core starts and supervises the three Bun workers (`resources/gui-bridge/stock-helper/host.ts <worker>`, which stay TypeScript: they import omp's own modules) and owns everything around them: which worker a method runs in (`terminal.*`/`pty.*` terminals, `extensions.*` extensions, the rest stock), each request's timeout and `{cancel}` withdrawal, the stock worker that stays silent `RECYCLE_GRACE` (30 s) past a timeout (everything waiting fails with `omp stock helper restarted: "<method>" hung`, it is killed, the next request starts a fresh one), the extensions worker's idle exit (`EXTENSIONS_IDLE_EXIT`, 60 s after its last answer), and 1c71982f's rule (a stock or extensions worker started on another omp install, by its `package.json` time and size, is let go when nothing is in flight on it and started fresh; never the terminals worker). One method `stock.request {host, method, timeoutMs, tuning}` with the params as the JSON text main wrote, in the frame's `bin`, answered with the result's JSON text in `bin` (none for `undefined`); `stock.stop {host}` fails what waits, lets the workers go and answers once they exited (at most 5 s); the event `stock.launch {host, worker, launch}` asks main how to start a worker, answered by `stock.launched` with the binary, arguments, whole environment and stamp file, or with why none can start (`unavailable` for an omp that is not there). Each `(host, worker)` is one lane: a task that takes one message at a time and owns the lane's state; a late timer, reader line or exit names the worker it was armed for and changes nothing of a newer one. The core's end of input ends every lane it served (`close_all`).
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `stockHelperRequest`, `stopStockHelper` and `configureStockHelper` with their signatures (their callers do not change); how a worker starts (`ompWorkerLaunch`: `systemOmp`, `spawnPath`, `ompPathEnv`, `ompPackageRoot`, `withoutControlEnv`, which 4c keeps where they are), answered when the core asks, so omp is still resolved only when a worker starts; the refusal while machine setup has omp missing (`ompAvailability`, read synchronously before any request). `helperWorker` and the `HelperWorker` routing moved to `Worker::of`; the test seams `spawnWorker` and `ompStamp` became `launch` (how a worker starts, with its stamp file) and `worker`/`recycleGraceMs`/`idleExitMs` (sent as `tuning`).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: `stock-helper.test.ts` runs its cases through the real core (timeout of one request only, the late answer within the grace, the withdrawn mutation that never runs, the recycle and the lazy fresh start, never recycling the terminals or extensions worker, a per-request timeout, a start that never finishes, stop, the idle exit and the request that keeps it up, the omp change under an idle and a busy worker, never retiring the terminals worker); the routing table is a Rust test and a real-worker case; the request line and the worker-answer reading are Rust tests. New: "ends its workers with the core, however the core ends" SIGKILLs the app's core and sees the worker gone. `models-config.test.ts`, `local/settings.test.ts`, `bridge-input-history.test.ts` (real stock history through the core's worker), `omp-availability.test.ts`, `stock-helper-accounts.test.ts`, `stock-helper-worktrees.test.ts`, `stock-helper-extensions.test.ts` pass unchanged against the real core; `session-lists-report.test.ts` (opt-in) now counts the CPU of the core's `host.ts` children.
  - Differences: the workers are the core's children, so they end with the core: a core that dies or is restarted (its crash, three failed starts) takes every live shell of the Terminal panel with it, where before only the app's end did; a request waiting then fails with `ompdesk-core exited (…)` (`CoreError`) instead of `omp stock helper exited`. The helper's own failures are plain `Error`s with the same messages as before (`stock_*` codes are turned back), a refusal for a missing omp is an `OmpUnavailableError` as before, any other failure of the core stays a `CoreError`. The `hung` and `omp changed` warnings are the core's stderr lines, which main logs as they arrive (a moment after the failure, where `console.warn` came first). Main's own bound on an answer is the request's timeout plus 10 s; the core answers at the timeout. A request's params never pass through the core's parser, so numbers and key order reach the worker as main wrote them; a params text holding a raw line break (`JSON.stringify` writes none) is refused. A worker's error message with half a surrogate pair reads U+FFFD there (a result is passed on as written). A worker's stderr goes to the null device (it was read and dropped). `stopStockHelper()` with no core running starts none (there are no workers then).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, this Windows desktop, 16 cores, under the load of four other sessions; the old `StockHelper` as a copy and the new client in one process, called alternately on the same worker state): one terminal request on an idle PTY 0.37 to 0.52 ms before, 0.12 to 0.15 ms more after (3 000 pairs per run, five runs); a 64 KB slice of scrollback 0.15 to 0.19 ms more, so the ceiling of a drain at the web budget is 0.79 to 0.81 of before (92 to 118 MB/s before, 72 to 92 after); a 1 MB answer 0.25 to 0.42 ms more; keystroke to echo (write, then the long poll) 15.3 ms both (30 rounds, ratio 1.00); a live stream of 8 MB at the web budget, bound by the program and the load, ratio 0.99 (30 rounds); `sessions.list` of 27 sessions (217 KB of JSON) 17 to 20 ms both, new minus old −0.5 to 0.0 ms (200 pairs, three runs). Nothing a person sees moved; the cost is the extra pipe hop of every request, and the drain ceiling, 0.8 of before, stays an order of magnitude above a live terminal. What was tried and kept: params and results cross as raw bytes (never parsed in the core) and a worker's stdout is read on a thread of its own (tokio reads a child's pipe on Windows through its blocking pool). Tried and not kept: answering the caller from the reader thread without a turn of the lane, plus a writer thread for the worker's stdin: 0.79 to 0.80 of before, the same within the spread. **The rest of the gap lives in main↔core stdio**: on Windows tokio reads the core's stdin and writes its stdout through its blocking pool too (`main.rs`, `wire.rs`, covered whole by `repo-lock-core`), a hop on each side of every frame; that is a later unit's (every core request pays it), not this one's. macOS not measured.
  - Formal model `formal/stock-lane` (`.omp/skills/stock-lane-verify`): a lane of each kind with its late messages in any order, main's start answered late, failed or never, workers that hang or exit at any moment, omp replaced at any moment, stops; seven frozen properties (nothing in flight is cut, the terminals worker ends only at a stop, only the stock worker is recycled and only when stuck, no request stranded, no worker forgotten, a start asked before a stop starts nothing). `verify-all.ts formal --only stock-lane`: 8 of 8 runs as expected (89 630, 35 318 and 101 328 states at 2 requests, 3 workers, 3 starts; 4 459 158 at 3 requests, 2 workers, 2 starts on the stock lane), 11 of 11 mutations detected. Two design points came from it: start numbers are drawn from one process-wide sequence, so an answer to a start asked before a stop is never taken for a later start's; a stop forgets the start it asked for. `@code` tags are line ranges (`bun formal/stock-lane/retag.ts` after editing `stock/mod.rs`). `repo-lock-core` drifts on `server.rs` (one `register` line, two `close_all` lines; its four `@code` ranges there moved and were retagged).
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `stock-helper.test.ts`, `models-config.test.ts`, `bridge-input-history.test.ts` and `omp-availability.test.ts` against the macOS core; `check` and `win-platform` run the node project, which holds them; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes. `e2e/terminal.e2e.ts` (the real app's Terminal panel, skipped on Windows) ran here with its skip removed: through the old host and through the core alike it passes the echo, `cd`, a new and a closed terminal, and fails at the same step (`exit` in cmd.exe shows no "Restart shell" within 10 s), which is the reason the spec skips Windows, not this unit; macOS runs it in CI's `@smoke`.
- **P. Shell prototype: the decision record** (batch P, 12 Nov 2026, Windows 11 x64 on this repository's development machine; the prototype is `native/shell-prototype/` on branch `omp/agent/rust-batch-shell-prototype`, never on `main`). Each candidate loaded the real built page (`out/renderer`) from its own custom scheme, with `window.omp` made by the app's own `createOmpApi` over the dev harness transport (`stand-in/`), so the page is today's page with fixture state. The Electron reference is Electron 36.9.5 (the release's) serving the same page and stand-in with nothing of OMPdesk's main (`measure/electron-baseline/`). Candidates: (a) `cef` crate 156.4.0 (CEF 156.0.26, Chromium 156; Views, Alloy style), chosen over Tauri 3's CEF runtime for the three open Tauri defects above; (b) Tauri 2.12.5 with WebView2 157 (the Preview a second WebView2); (c) the same Tauri 2 window with the Preview in `preview-host` (CEF 156), parented as a child window (`cef-child`) or streamed by CDP screencast into a canvas with clicks sent back by `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` (`cef-stream`, the only form macOS allows).
  - Pass list, Windows (all three: pass; evidence in `results/items/<c>.json` and the agents' logs, gitignored on the branch):
    - Custom scheme with range requests and video seek: a 15 MB WebM seeked to 80/20/50 % landed each time (`selftest.video_seek ok`), 5 to 8 ranged requests answered 206 per run. CEF calls its handler's `skip` for any range not starting at 0 and fails the request when `skip` says unsupported, even after a 206: the handler answers the range itself and reports `skip` done.

(c)'s `cef-stream` failed its CDP self-tests once in two runs (`no page target on port 9303`), passed on the rerun.

(a) draws min/max/close in a native owned popup (posted clicks maximized, restored, minimized and closed); (b)/(c) inject HTML buttons that post to the shell; WebView2's own child window answers `HTCAPTION` and (b)/(c) forward it from the top-level window. The release offered v0.200.0 whose `ompdesk-windows-setup.exe` is a Rust handoff installer (`move/installer`) carrying the candidate. Every run: `check found` → `download verified` (SHA-512) → `scheduler safe` → `countdown` → 60 s → `install trigger auto` → `swap helper nsis`; the installer staged beside the old folder, moved it aside, swapped, wrote its uninstaller and the entry, started the new shell; the shell logged `migrate.token ok` (Electron's `safeStorage` on Windows is OSCrypt `v10`: AES-256-GCM under a key DPAPI-wraps in `Local State`; the Rust read's digest prefix equalled the stand-in's), `migrate.localStorage ok` with 8 to 10 keys and the page's `localStorage.imported`, `page.loaded`; the entry kept its GUID with `DisplayVersion 0.200.0`, `InstallLocation` and the exe name `OMPdesk.exe` were kept, and the new uninstaller removed the folder and both keys and kept userData. A failed move (`HANDOFF_FAIL_AT=swap`) put the Electron folder back, started it, and its log read `swap result outcome rolled back, previous 0.127.0, current 0.127.0`. So on Windows the released app needs no handoff release: the new shell can ship as its next update.
  - What the move taught: the page's origin changes (`file://` to `app://ompdesk` or `http://app.localhost`), so Local Storage (drafts, the outbox, the snapshot cache, unread marks) is carried by reading Electron's LevelDB from a copy and importing it once (`migrate/`); a failed handoff is offered again at once and counts down again every round, so the swap batch caps retries; the old folder could not be removed at once after 2 of 5 swaps (files still held), so the new shell sweeps `<install>.old-*` later; Electron is never written to, so a rollback finds it as it was.

The GPU process is the swing: in a split run Electron's held 210 MB idle and 127 MB with the Preview open, so how much of (a)'s 196 MB over Electron with the Preview is GPU attribution is **[not determined]**.

The released updater, unchanged: `check found 0.200.0 mode relaunch`, `download verified` (175 MB, 3.8 s; `stageBundleFromDmg`'s `codesign --verify --deep --strict`, bundle id and version passed), `scheduler safe`, `countdown`, `install auto`, `swap helper`; `swap.log` `installed`; `open -n` started the CEF shell with Electron's argv (its `--user-data-dir` read as the userData): `migrate.localStorage keys 5` (Korean text, a 200 000-character value), `page.report localStorage.imported`, `page.loaded`; `/Applications/OMPdesk.app` unchanged (version, CDHash). Failure: the apps folder made read-only before the countdown ended: `swap.log` `copy failed`, the Electron bundle untouched, and its next launch logged `swap result outcome rolled back, swapOutcome copy failed` and ran. No SecurityAgent line in either run (`log stream` on SecurityAgent and coreauthd).

Not run: macOS 27 (no hosted runner has it).
    - What macOS taught: a keychain reached through a test HOME makes Chromium's item carry `partition_id cdhash:<creator>` (seen on the MacBook, reproduced on both runners), so every other binary is asked for the keychain password: that was the dialog. The user's own login keychain gives no partition entry, so the designated requirement decides. The released app appends `--use-mock-keychain` under `OMP_GUI_HEADLESS`, so a headless test copy never encrypts with the real key. The real "ompdesk Safe Storage" item on the MacBook was created by ad-hoc builds: its ACL and partition list name three cdhashes and no certificate, so any build outside them (today's self-signed one and any new shell) is asked once, whatever the shell; carrying such secrets silently needs the handoff release to re-create the item under the designated requirement, or the new shell asks for each secret again with the reason shown.
    - Not verified on macOS: a real title-bar drag (needs physical input); the keychain read on macOS 27; notarization (none here, as for the release).
  - Building (a) on Windows needed: CEF's archive served locally (`cef-dll-sys`'s download cannot read CEF's 10 MB `index.json`), a short build path (the wrapper's object paths pass MAX_PATH from a worktree; `subst`), and an embedded application manifest (without it the GPU process crash-looped). CI for the swap batch carries these.
  - **Decision (13 Nov 2026, final): (a) CEF through the `cef` crate.** It is the only candidate that passes the pass list on both platforms: (b) fails CDP, Playwright and trusted input on macOS; (c) fails the desktop Playwright attach, the page's drag regions and the traffic lights on macOS. (c)'s lower idle memory on macOS does not outweigh a failed item. Two items carry into the swap batch S as its own checks: a real title-bar drag on both platforms, and the keychain read on macOS 27. **The keychain read on macOS 27 is not verified, accepted by the owner** (13 Nov 2026, "승인받지않고 그냥 진행하라고 빨리좀"): no GitHub-hosted runner has macOS 27 and tests never run on a person's Mac, so S proves the keychain on macOS 15 and 26 runners and on Windows, and the move release ships with macOS 27 an accepted, unverified risk; its release notes say so. **The ad-hoc keychain case (the owner's decision, 13 Nov 2026, "너가 생각한데로 진행해"):** no silent path exists. An item ad-hoc builds created trusts only their cdhashes (its ACL and partition list), so any other binary, the handoff release included, gets a SecurityAgent dialog, and changing its access needs the keychain password; today's Electron already cannot read it silently. So: (1) S lands only after a GitHub macOS runner shows the real users' path silent: an item a release-signed Electron build created is read by the new shell signed with the same certificate with no dialog; (2) the new shell reads the keychain only when userData holds ciphertext (`update-token.bin`, `mail-accounts/*.bin`; lazy read, `<exe> --keychain-key` on the first encrypt or decrypt), so a move without them touches no keychain (the owner's MacBook has none, read by `ls` on 13 Nov 2026); (3) where ciphertext sits beside an item the shell cannot read silently (development machines), it never touches the keychain: the update token is dropped (environment and `gh` tokens still work) and each mail account is marked for re-entry with an in-app notice naming it, never an OS dialog; (4) one item and one service name, no second one. Any dialog in a move is still a failure.
- **7b. The memory service's lifecycle** (`crates/core/src/aimemory/service/`; was `AiMemoryService` of `src/main/ai-memory/service.ts` and `binary.ts`, `unit.ts`, `root.ts`, `terminal.ts`, `suppress.ts`, `recall-state.ts`, `config.ts`, 1 706 lines to the 628 of the client): the wiring pass (`pass.rs`: stage, `setup --plan`, `init` and the embedding opt-out, the service take-over, the terminal migration, `setup --yes`, the extension root, the health wait), the 60 s poll and its heal, the bounded retries, purge, the terminal omp's wiring and its removal, `extensionArgs` with the suppression overlay, the binary's staging and its SHA-256 and signature checks (`binary.rs`), the launchd unit and the logon entry (`unit.rs`), the generated root and its swap (`root.rs`), the agent dir's wiring (`terminal.rs`), the parsers and texts (`text.rs`) and the recall file `<userData>/ai-memory/recall.json` (written by a thread of the core, newest first, 0600) are the core's. Health and stats use 5d's client in process (no new HTTP code). Methods `aimemory.service.open` (main names the service and hands its `AiMemoryServiceDeps` as data), `start` (held until the first pass ended), `queue` `{op: retry|purge|terminal}` (answered once queued, so main's order is the queue's) and `outcome` `{step}`, `bunInstalled`, `refresh`, `extensionArgs`, `answer`, `fire`, `close`, `closed` (held: its failure says the core died). Events `aimemory.service.state` (every change; main's copy for `state()`), `log` (main writes `gui-runtime.jsonl`: one writer), `stalled` (main writes the `chain-stall` line, `writeStall`), `ask` and `cancel`, and for a `manual` service `timer`/`clearTimer`. `close_all` (two lines in `server.rs`) closes what is open when the input ends.
  - **What only main has, asked for** (`aimemory.service.ask` `{ask, what}` answered by `aimemory.service.answer`): every `ai-memory` command (`run` `{bin, args, env, isolated}` → `{ok, cancelled, output}` or `{spawn}`, run by `SetupRunner` under the supervisor as before, `setup --yes` isolated; a stopped step's command gets `cancel` and main's `runner.cancel()`), `agentDir`, `terminalPref`, `persistTerminal`, `bunPresent`, `beforeStart`, `spawnEnv` (the override's `--version`), `disabledLists` (each config's `disabledExtensions`), and the test seams `codeRequirement` and `unit` (`pidFileControl`, the e2e's pid file, stays in main and answers it). A failure main reports keeps its stack in the `failed` line.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: the client `AiMemoryService` with every public method but `recallEnv()` and `AiMemoryServiceDeps` but `client` (plus `recallFile`); the reads Settings makes (`AiMemoryClient`, 5d); `AI_MEMORY_REQUIREMENT` and `MAC_STAGE_KEY` (one spelling, handed to the core with `AI_MEMORY_BINARIES`, the manifest's name, the version and `MEMORY_HUB_ENTRY`); the YAML read of the configs (main's `yaml` package: the core has no YAML parser, and a second one could read a person's config differently; the core applies stock's precedence over the lists); `SetupRunner`, `findBun` and `isolatedLaunch` (6a's decision). **`recallEnv()` is gone, not kept**: its one reader was `index.ts` writing the recall file on each state, which the core now writes itself.
  - **One step at a time, as `SerialChain` ran them**: a queue task per service runs the pass, a purge or a terminal change, each raced against `stepMaxMs` (30 min); a stopped step's future is dropped, so nothing it would still do happens (what `throwIfStepStalled` fenced), its command is cancelled, the caller gets a `StepStalledError` with the chain's words and the next step starts at once (the TypeScript chain held no tracked effect for ai-memory either, so no `ChainBusyError`). A check runs beside the queue and joins one already running.
  - **The clock in tests**: `AiMemoryServiceDeps.now` (only a test hands it in) opens the service `manual`: every request says the time, every timer of the service (the poll's interval, the step's bound, the retries, the health waits) is main's `setInterval`/`setTimeout`, fired back by `aimemory.service.fire`, and main's requests wait as long as a held one (a fake clock's jump would time them out). Production runs on tokio's timers and the system clock.
  - Kept exactly, with tests (`aimemory/service/captured.json`, captured from the TypeScript before it was deleted, 464 rows): 11 `parseProblems`, 18 `parsePlan`, 36 `tail`, 13 `withoutLocalEmbeddings`, 26 `withinPath` on both systems' `normalize`, 256 agent dirs (8 extension texts × 32 `mcp.json` texts: the wiring read and the file after the removal), 10 generated `.mcp.json` through the hub mount, 11 overlays (text and name), 10 `commandProgram`, 9 `runEntryCommand`, 13 `--version` outputs, 48 build records × platforms, 3 recall texts. The cases of `binary.test.ts`, `root.test.ts`, `terminal.test.ts`, `unit.test.ts` (launchd and logon, with the commands recorded by a stand-in `exec`) and `recall-state.test.ts` are Rust tests of `aimemory/service/tests.rs` (25 tests; their functions are the core's now, as 5c's `ResearchStream` cases became Rust tests); their build-record, requirement and `pidFileControl` cases moved to `service.test.ts`. `service.test.ts` runs its 66 cases (2 skipped off their OS) through the real core: its harness swapped `FakeServerClient` for a memory server on 127.0.0.1 (the config's `bind` its port), the parsers' cases for the table, one `rootComplete` for the state's wiring; "runs one check at a time" waits on a real timeout (its `setInterval` is fake) with one 300 ms wait for what must not happen, and "a stopped step that resumes later" waits for the Bun ask (the step's bound is set before it) before the jump. `ipc.test.ts` (21) is unchanged; `memory-capture-stock.test.ts` (5, installed omp) passes through the core.
  - Differences: a rewritten `mcp.json` (the removal) and `.mcp.json` (the hub mount) keep a number's spelling (`1.0`, `1e3`, `1e21`) and an integer-like key's place, where JavaScript wrote `1`, `1000`, `1e+21` and moved such keys first (18 of the 266 rows). A build record of `null` is not a build record (JavaScript threw a `TypeError` with the engine's words). A failure of the core's own (a file it cannot write) logs `stack: null`. `setup`'s scratch agent dir and new shared skills are put back also when its step is stopped (a `Drop`); a stopped generation leaves `omp-ext.next` for the next one to remove. `spawnEnv` is asked only for the override's `--version` (the TypeScript asked at every resolve); `--version`, `launchctl`, `reg`, `taskkill` and `codesign` run from the core (no shell, no console window, stdin null; a `.cmd` override through cmd.exe as std runs it), a codesign that fails silently reads `Command failed: /usr/bin/codesign …`. The core's pruning of older staged builds runs on a blocking thread. `extensionArgs` answers `[]` when the core cannot answer (a spawn never waits on a dead core); a failing `agentDir` still rejects. A core that dies takes its steps, checks and timers with it: the client opens the service on the next core and runs the launch pass again when memory had started (each command is idempotent). `dispose()` closes the core's service: a running step ends there and a caller waiting on a queued step reads `Memory's service closed`.
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, this machine, `bun run build:native`): `ompdesk-core.exe` 8 307 200 bytes before, 8 909 312 after (+602 112, 7.2 %); `ompdesk.exe` unchanged (410 112). **`sha2` is added once for the workspace** (RustCrypto, pure Rust): `Cargo.lock` gains `sha2`, `digest`, `block-buffer`, `crypto-common`, `generic-array`, `typenum`, `cpufeatures`, `cfg-if` and the build-time `version_check`; `LICENSES/ompdesk-native-dependencies.txt` lists 84 linked crates. Not measured: speed (`ai-memory`'s commands and the server bound a pass; a poll is one HTTP exchange as before plus two asks).
  - Not verified here: the POSIX paths (launchd's plist and `launchctl bootout`, a staged binary's mode 0755, the recall file's 0600) and the real `codesign` test ran only in CI's `native`; `e2e/packaged-ai-memory.e2e.ts` needs a packaged app and is left to the batch gate. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` ran for the host only.
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run `service.test.ts` against the real core; `mac-native`'s list names it (`.github/actions/native-parity`); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes (the codesign test macOS only, the logon entry's inspection Windows only).
  - No formal model of its own. `formal/repo-lock-core` covers `server.rs` whole: this unit added `close_all`'s two calls (none a modelled step), so it drifts there; `run.rs`, `sys.rs` and `main.rs` are untouched. Not run or stamped here (the batch's run does it).
- **6c. Setup downloads** (`crates/core/src/setup/`; was `src/main/setup/download.ts` and the network and parse parts of `src/main/setup/archives.ts`, 275 lines to 155 of clients): the verified download (`setup.download`: `<file>.part`, SHA-256 on the way, rename on a match, nothing left on a failure or a cancel) and the four release lookups, one request per question as 5d did (`setup.resolveArchive` for Node and gh, `setup.resolveAgentDesktop`, `setup.resolveTailscale`), on the HTTP stack of 5d (`http.rs` gained `open`, the answer's head with the body still to be read; TLS, redirects and the proxy-less connection are 5d's). The user agent is `ompdesk-setup`, as before.
  - Progress is the event `setup.downloadProgress` `{id, received, total}`: at 0, every 100 ms while bytes grew, and once more before the hash is checked (the TypeScript reported every chunk; `service.ts` already throttled what it emitted). The abort is the request's `{cancel}` frame: the client throws the signal's reason as `downloadVerified` did. The file is written by a thread of its own that the request joins on every path, so `.part` is closed before it is removed (Windows refuses to delete an open file) and a slow disk backs the body up through a queue of 8 chunks.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `downloadVerified` and the three `resolve*Release` functions as typed clients with their signatures (callers: `service.ts`, `scripts/build-agent-desktop.ts`, `scripts/setup-assets-health.ts`), `DownloadError` (the class `service.ts` tests with `instanceof`) and `AGENT_DESKTOP_MIN_VERSION` (a constant `detect.ts` and `agent-desktop.ts` read). `reaNodeSupported` (`src/shared/builtin-mcp.ts`) stays the one spelling main uses; the core spells REA's engines range again (`archives.rs`) and `archives.test.ts` holds the two to the same answers over ten versions.
  - **Test seam**: `fetchText`/`DownloadFetch` (fake functions) became one `mirror` (a URL main passes only when a test built the service with it): the core reads `https://host/path` from `<mirror>/host/path`, messages and releases still name the real address. The tests start a server on 127.0.0.1 (`src/main/setup/test-mirror.ts`); none reaches the network. `LookupOptions.githubToken` carries what `scripts/setup-assets-health.ts` sent as `authorization` to api.github.com (and to nothing else; `http.rs` drops it at a redirect to another origin); `accept` is what `build-agent-desktop.ts` sent for the API's asset URL.
  - Kept exactly, with tests (`setup/captured.json`, captured from the TypeScript before it was deleted): 36 indexes for `pickNodeLts` (every truthiness of `lts`, versions that are no plain version, a 23-digit major, leading zeros, non-ASCII digits), 28 listings for `parseShasums` (JavaScript's `\s`, `trim` and `\*?\S+` backtracking, CRLF and a lone CR, U+00A0, U+FEFF, U+0085), the file names, and 94 whole lookups (every case of `archives.test.ts` and `setup-assets-health.test.ts`, tags that are no version, hashes of 63, 65 and 70 hex digits, `\b` against `_` and non-ASCII letters, Tailscale indexes with a wrong type at each member, a missing version) replayed against the sequence of pages: the same URLs asked in the same order, the same release or the same message. The hand-written patterns (`is_version`, `parse_shasums`, `is_package_file`, `sha256_token`) read `\d`, `\s`, `\S`, `\b` and `$` as JavaScript does. A page reads as `fetch(...).text()` (a leading byte order mark dropped); the 30 s bound per page is the core's own timer.
  - Differences: a transport failure says what kind it was (`could not connect`, `the connection ended`, `too many redirects`, rustls's words for a certificate) where node said `getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND host` or `connect ECONNREFUSED 127.0.0.1:1`, and a body cut short is `<url>: the connection ended` where it was `terminated`; a page that is no JSON fails in serde's words where V8 wrote `Unexpected token …` (and JSON nested deeper than 128 levels is refused); a lookup that takes over 30 s fails `<url>: timed out after 30000 ms` where `AbortSignal.timeout` said `The operation was aborted due to timeout`; a failure of the core itself (it died) is a `CoreError`, not `DownloadError`, and `service.ts` reports it as a download failure as it did any other error; progress is throttled (above); every request is its own connection and carries no `accept-language`, `accept-encoding` or the other headers `fetch` added (5d); the `.part` that was a directory the test made is left (the TypeScript removed it recursively: the core removes only a file it created); `setup-assets-health.ts` lists with `ompdesk-setup` as its agent where it had its own name.
  - The scripts now need the core: `build-agent-desktop.ts` and `setup-assets-health.ts` call `ensureNative()` and stop the core when they end (it is a child with an open stdin pipe). Every job that runs `build:agent-desktop` already has the binary from the install action; the weekly health workflow builds it on the runner (no `bun install` there, and none needed).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, Windows 11, `cargo build --locked --release`, HEAD without and with this unit): `ompdesk-core.exe` 9 879 040 bytes before, 10 102 784 after (+223 744, 2.3 %); `ompdesk.exe` unchanged (410 112). No crate added: `Cargo.lock` and the notices are unchanged (`sha2` and the HTTP stack were there). Not measured: speed (a download is bound by the network and the disk).
  - Not verified here: macOS and Linux (the file thread, `rename` over an existing file on POSIX; the Rust tests and clippy ran for the host only), a real download from the real hosts (the weekly health check does that), the built app's setup screen.
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run `download.test.ts`, `archives.test.ts` and `service.test.ts` against the real core (the node project); `mac-native`'s list names the first two (`.github/actions/native-parity`); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes; `setup-assets-health.yml` runs the real downloads weekly through the core.

- **7c. Memory upkeep** (`crates/core/src/upkeep/`; was the logic of `src/main/ai-memory/{upkeep,distill,retitle,rules-file,distill-model,ambiguous-names,supersede}.ts`): the upkeep's tick (`upkeep.tick`: which conversation is read, the schedule state `upkeep.json` written as `JSON.stringify` wrote it, in one step with `disk/tree.rs` `write_atomic_if`), learning (the digest, the distill prompt `SYSTEM`, whose one copy is now `distill.rs` because its only reader moved, the proposals' checks, `proposalPath`/`proposalMarkdown`, supersede, the rules-file backstop and outline with the rules files read by the core), the launch repair (`upkeep.repair`: the ambiguous-name rename `names.rs`, the retitle of junk session pages and the child handoffs' cancel `retitle.rs`), `upkeep.retitle`, `upkeep.renameAmbiguous`, the cheap-model call (`upkeep.runSmol`, `model.rs`: `omp -p --mode json … --model pi/smol` in its empty folder, stdin the digest, 180 s, `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED`) and `upkeep.close`. Production asks only `omp` (how omp starts: `systemOmp()`, `ompRunEnv`), so omp is resolved per model call as before. A test's fakes are asked for by kind (`client` with the TypeScript method's name and arguments, `facts`, `journal`, `model`, `rules`), so the vitest files drive the real core with their own fake client, model, rules and clock (`now` with every request).

Every pure vitest case of `distill.test.ts`, `rules-file.test.ts`, `supersede.test.ts` and `retitle.test.ts` (`isChildHandoff`, `retitleMarkdown`) is a Rust test of the same name; those vitest files and cases are gone. `upkeep.test.ts` (19), `retitle.test.ts` (7), `ambiguous-names.test.ts` (3), `distill-model.test.ts` (2, against a stand-in omp) and the new `stock-helper-memory.test.ts` (5, the stock worker's `memory.*` on the installed omp) run against the real core: 36 of 36. New Rust engine tests over a scripted client: the state file's text, the learn line's bytes and the order of the asks, a failing model, a refusing facts read, a cancelled tick, a tick beside a running one, a session not read again until `close`.
  - Differences: the prompt facts are a stock-worker request, so a worker that fails (omp missing, a hung lane) fails the tick, or is a `failed` line per page or project of the retitle, where the bridge functions could not fail. `Date.parse` reads only ECMAScript's ISO forms (a date-time without an offset as UTC where JavaScript reads local time; any other spelling unparsable) for `runs`, a session's `startedAt` and a prompt's `createdAt`. `upkeep.json`: a `sessions` that is an array reads empty, a `done` that is a string counts only as plain decimal digits, `1e999` makes the file read empty, integer-like session ids keep their order where JavaScript moved them first, a float written by hand reads back as `1.0`. Main bounds a tick at 300 s, a repair or retitle at 600 s, a rename at 120 s. The rules files are read one after another (the same text).

Developed on Windows: the POSIX stand-in of `run_smol` ran only in CI. `e2e/memory-upkeep.e2e.ts` (the repair on the fake server) is left to the batch run.
- **7d. Memory care** (`crates/core/src/care/`; was the logic of `src/main/ai-memory/{care,care-units,backfill,curate,memory-judge}.ts` and of `freshness.ts`' caller side, `checkFreshness` and `namedItems`, which 2h left in main): `MemoryCare`'s schedule (`care.tick`: one unit per tick, the version reset, `RETRY_MS`, each unit's interval), its state `care.json` (read as `readState` read it, written in one step), the recording client (the kept pages answered `pinned`, the head checkpoint and the page's title read before a change, the stale/wrong refusal), `care.summary` and `care.undo`; the three units (`units.rs`: the backfill, the curation with its `unrelated` memo, the freshness check per project with `care-skipped`); the backfill (`backfill.rs`: journals walked, headers, the server's sessions and native conclusions paged, the cutoff, the title chain, the writes in batches of 200, the budgets); the curation (`curate.rs`: durable pages read 8 at a time, the weighted term cosine pairing, the code verdict, the decisions and retirements); both judges (`judge.rs`: Jev's one-shot `omp --mode rpc --no-session --no-extensions --no-skills --no-rules -e memory-judge-host.ts` in its empty folder with its in/out files, stdin held open, the 100 ms poll, 180 s, the folder removed whatever happens; the cheap model's one batched call through 7c's `run_smol`; the chain); the freshness check (`freshness.rs`, which calls 2h's `git/freshness.rs` in process with the git runner main hands in). Methods `care.tick`, `care.summary`, `care.undo`, `care.close`, `care.unitCall` and, for the tests' functions, `care.backfill`, `care.curate`, `care.freshness`, `care.judgeSmol`, `care.judgeJev`; the asks and log lines are 7c's (`upkeep.ask`, `upkeep.log`), with the kinds `unit` (a test's own unit, whose client calls come back as `care.unitCall` and go through that tick's recording client), `judge` and `journal`.

The pure vitest cases moved to Rust tests of the same subject: `memory-judge.test.ts`' `pageText` (2), `parseSmolVerdicts` (4), `smolMessage`, `jevVerdict` (2), `jevRequest` and `chainJudges` (3), `curate.test.ts`' `projectRules`, `freshness.test.ts`' `namedItems`; its case "ends the check when the core loses the verdict" is gone with the round trip it covered. `care.test.ts` (6), `backfill.test.ts` (13), `curate.test.ts` (17), `memory-judge.test.ts` (3; its four `jevJudge` cases use a POSIX shell stand-in and skip on Windows, as before), `freshness.test.ts` (9) and `bridge-memory-judge-stock.test.ts` (3, the installed omp with a loopback judge) run against the real core: 51 of 51. `cargo test -p ompdesk-core care`: 94 (the engine's cases over a scripted main, Jev's run with a `.cmd` stand-in).
  - A defect fixed on the way: an undo waited for a running tick, but a tick could start while the undo ran, and whichever wrote `care.json` last lost the other's changes (an undone page shown again, or a unit's step forgotten). The undo now holds the care service's gate for its whole run, so a tick asked meanwhile does nothing, as a tick asked during another tick does, and runs at the next one. Rust test `a_tick_asked_while_an_undo_runs_does_nothing_so_the_undo_is_not_written_over`.
  - Differences: `JSON.parse` against serde in the cheap model's answer: a lone surrogate escape, a number past `f64` (`1e999`) or nesting past 128 levels leaves every pair undecided where V8 read them (no other difference in 39 969 fuzzed answers); in Jev's out file such a number fails the whole file (the run did not decide) where the TypeScript dropped only that entry, a result's id counts only as a number or a string of digits, a `cost.total` that is no number counts 0 and a `model` that is no string is left out. `pageText` and `textKey` cut through a surrogate pair keep U+FFFD where JavaScript kept the half. `Math.log` is fdlibm's (`js_log`; `f64::ln` differed in the last digit for 15 320 of 180 300 arguments). `Date.parse` reads ECMAScript's ISO forms only (as 7c).
