The freshness check's gits run inside one core request: no per-verdict request deadline (each git keeps its 20 s), and no lost-verdict break. `curateMemory`'s test seam `rules` is asked per folder (`rules {cwd}`) where it was per project; the pure exports (`pairKey`, `candidatePairs`, `projectRules`) are gone from TypeScript. Jev's run waits up to 2 s for its omp to exit after the kill before its folder goes.
  - Measured: `ompdesk-core.exe` (`bun run build:native`, Windows x64) 8 936 448 bytes after 7c, 9 776 640 (+840 192, 9.4 %) after 7d. No new crate. Not measured: speed (the server, the judges and git bound each unit).
  - Formal: `repo-lock-core` drifts on `server.rs` (one `register` line); `git/mod.rs` only made `freshness` crate-visible. No model of its own: one gate per service serializes ticks and undos, and every unit step is one request.
  - CI coverage: `check` (Linux) and `win-platform` run the node project; `mac-native` runs `care.test.ts`, `backfill.test.ts`, `curate.test.ts`, `memory-judge.test.ts` (the `jevJudge` cases run there) and `freshness*.test.ts`; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes. Developed on Windows: the POSIX stand-ins ran only in CI; `care.tick` against a real server in the packaged app was not run.
- **9a. The command entry** (`crates/core/src/ipc/`; batch H1, the owner's choice B of 12 Nov 2026 among A, the doc's every-call-through-the-core; B, the core's own channels only; C, landing 9a with 9d): the core serves the page's `invoke`/`send` channels its subsystems own. `IpcRegistry.routeCore` (`web/ipc-registry.ts`) hands the core `IPC_POLICY` as data once per core (`ipc.start {policy}` → `{channels: [{channel, dir}]}`) and registers each channel the core names on `ipcMain` and in its web table, as `handle`/`on` do; a call of one goes to `ipc.call {caller: {key, window, connId}, channel, dir, args}` → `{value}`. Every other channel stays main's and never touches the core.
  - Landed before P's macOS half (the main session's call, 12 Nov 2026): 9a, 9b and 9c are the same core under every shell candidate, CEF passed every item of P's pass list on Windows, and the owner wants nothing held back; the macOS half of P runs on the MacBook and only S waits for it.
  - One source for the policy: the core's table (`OWNED`) names channels and handlers only; a channel's direction and web permission are `IPC_POLICY`'s (TypeScript, typed over the channel union), and a channel the core serves that the policy lists as no `invoke`/`send` refuses the start (`ipc_policy_missing`). A call in the wrong direction is `ipc_direction` (`<channel> is not a <dir> channel`), an unknown one `no_handler` with Electron's words.
  - **Built, not yet called**: `OWNED` is empty until the pool's first channel moves (9d), so in the app `ipc.start` answers no channel and nothing is routed. Only the plumbing channels `test:ipc.record`/`test:ipc.recorded` exist, served when `ipc.start` asks with `testChannels` (as `core.echo` is the wire's). 9d awaits `routeCore` before the first window, which this unit does not (an empty table has nothing to wait for).
  - The web permission check of a paired page's call stays in `ws-router.ts` and moves with the web router (8c): moved now it would be a second copy of the rule or a core hop on every web call.
  - A core that is down or resting fails only its own channels (`ipc-registry.test.ts` "fails only the core's channels while the core is down or resting": three failed starts, the rest, and a main channel answering after each). A core started again (another pid) is handed the policy again before any call reaches it; calls made meanwhile wait behind it in the order they came, and all fail with the hand-over's error when it fails.
  - Order: a page's calls reach their handlers in the order the page made them, as Electron runs them. Main writes each call's line at once; the server now calls every handler (its synchronous part) in the read loop, before the next line is read (`server.rs` `begin`; a panic there still fails only its request), and `ipc.call`'s synchronous part puts the call in its caller's lane (one per window page `w<webContents id>`, one per paired connection `c<connId>`). A `send` runs to its end before the lane takes the next call; an `invoke` is started in turn and answers on its own, as an async Electron handler does. Pinned by `ipc-registry.test.ts` "runs a page's sends to a core channel in the order the page made them" (the first send holds 50 ms in the core) and the Rust test `a_callers_sends_run_in_the_order_its_lines_came`.
  - Differences (for the channels that move): arguments and results cross as JSON (a `Uint8Array`, `undefined` in an array, a `Date` arrive as JSON spells them, where Electron's structured clone kept them), so a channel that carries bytes moves with a `bin` form of its own; a core-served call has no deadline (`2^31 - 1` ms), as an Electron handler has none.
  - `repo-lock-core` covers `server.rs` whole: the read loop now builds each request's future before reading the next line (`begin`/`finish`, was `answer`), the scope and the cancel unchanged.
- **9b, 9c. omp's pipes and the sidecar manager** (`crates/core/src/sidecar/`: `lane.rs` the tab's state machine, `frames.rs` was `rpc-bridge.ts`, `bridge.rs` was `bridge-client.ts`'s socket client, `stop.rs` was `omp-stop.ts`, `queue.rs` was `omp-start-queue.ts`, `messages.rs` was `run-messages.ts`, `launch.rs` `sessionOpen` and the restart policy, `process.rs` the child; batch H1). Each tab is a lane in the core, one task that owns its omp child, its stdio, its bridge socket and every rule of `SidecarManager` around them: spawn after the stopped omps exited and a start-queue place, the generation guard, `ready`, protocol v2, the event filter, the ask dialog, the host tools, the bridge connect, the first `get_state` with no deadline, the start gate, the slow start, the restart loop and its two boot recoveries, the session identity and the tab's session (`#sessionPath`/`#isNew`), the `agent_end` hold for entry ids and the run's messages, the side channel, kill, sleep, dispose with its stdin grace, the quit's stop of every omp (`sidecar.stopAll`) and the launch's hold of every start until the reap settled (`sidecar.holdStarts`). The pid ledger (5a) is written by the lane at each spawn and exit.
  - Wire: `sidecar.open {tab, seq, cwd, kind, fresh, eventFilter, bridge, ledgerFile, startSlowMs, sessionPath?, isNew?, tuning}` answers only when the tab is disposed and its omps exited, so its `core_exited` rejection is how main learns of a lost core; every later message of the tab carries main's `seq` and the lane applies them in that order. Commands: `start`, `restart`, `kill`, `sleep`, `dispose`, `cwd`, `gate`, `command`, `send`, `bridge`, `barrier`, `whenExited`. The lane asks main by event (`resolve`, `launch`, `exit`, `gateAsk`, `slow`, `sessionWait`) and main answers by request (`resolved`, `launched` with the bin, args, whole environment, cwd, shell and the host tools, `exitPlan`, `gated`, `slowLog`, `sessionFreed`). Reports: `status`, `spawned`, `exited`, `frame` (omp's frame as the event's `bin` tail, `Events::send_bin`: as omp wrote it, except a `message_update` the lane slimmed, `sidecar/stream.rs`, ledger "Slim lane stream"), `answer` (a stock command's response, in the same stream as the frames, so a response never overtakes a frame omp wrote before it), `session`, `tabSession`, `bridgeState`, `bridgeLost`, `stderr`, `failure`, `slowReport`, `disposed`.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `SidecarManager` keeps its constructor, members and events (`SidecarLike` unchanged) as the core's client and a **mirror** of the lane's state written only from the lane's events, plus the synchronous first effect of a command main sends (`start`/`restart` read `starting`, `kill`/`restart`/`sleep`/`dispose` drop `processAlive`, `pid` and `rpcClient`, `sleep` reads `asleep`, `restart(cwd)`/`adoptCwd` set `cwd`); a lane event older than the latest such command (its `cmd` below that seq) is not applied. Every closure of `SidecarOptions` (prefs, omp resolution, environments, builtin MCP, session ownership, browser, launch profiles, the boot model, the reports, the omp log, the priority, the session processes) is main state the lane asks for; `ompArgs`/`sessionEnv`/`SESSION_OWNED_ENV` assemble a spawn from it. `withoutFrameBookkeeping` (shares `withoutRequestBookkeeping` with the bridge's `transcript-page.ts`), `projectQueueUpdate`, `frameTransform`, the `EventBatcher` and the frame-to-event routing run in main on each delivered frame, in order. `parseModelRestoreFailure` (shared with the page) and `isNoModelBootFailure` classify a boot exit when the lane asks. The local handlers and their routing (`RpcClient`: a local handler first, else `sidecar.command`; `RpcTimeoutError` stays main's). `bridge-client.ts` stays as a test tool only: the `bridge-*-stock` tests and `scripts/measure-omp-startup.ts` start a bare omp without a lane.
  - The quit: the teardown starts `ompProcesses.stopAll()` right after `disposeAll()`, the core's stop waits for it and for the schedules' journal, and `settled` awaits the same promise, because the core's end takes every omp with it (an omp that asked to be left to save a login is held, as before). `sidecar.stopAll` joins a dispose's stop under way.
  - Differences: a spawned omp reads `processAlive` a round trip after the lane spawned it (`spawned`), and `lowerAgentPriority` runs then, a few ms later; every local command makes one `sidecar.barrier` round trip before it answers; a lost core takes the omps with it and the tab reads `restarting` (`ompdesk-core exited …`) and is opened again on its session (an asleep or disposed tab is not; a core main stopped is not reopened); a child's exit is told only after its stdout closed (at most 1 s), so a last answer written before it is not lost; stderr is logged by main from the `stderr` event, and a stopped omp's late stderr no longer lands in the next spawn's tail; the excerpt's cut and the exit-hold tail count characters (a cut surrogate reads U+FFFD); JSON nested deeper than 128 levels is refused; a non-string `set_session_name` name sends no `session_info_update`; a bridge answer for entry ids that is no array delivers the `agent_end` unchanged; signals go through the task that owns the child, so a reused pid is never signalled; a spawn failure reads node's `spawn <bin> <CODE>`; the Windows exit code is read unsigned; a boot model the closure refuses enters the restart loop where the tab stopped; `sidecar.command`'s deadline only stops counting a command as unanswered (its late response is delivered as a frame, as before); a `.cmd` omp runs as `ComSpec /d /s /c "<bin> <args>"` with the arguments main quoted, as node's shell spawn did.
  - Kept, with tests: `sidecar.test.ts`, `shell-env.test.ts`, `sidecar-os-shutdown.test.ts` (Windows: a node stand-in, since bun keeps only an exit code's low 8 bits), `sidecar-exit-hold.test.ts` (was `omp-stop.test.ts`'s save-hold case), `omp-processes.test.ts`, `session-launch-parity.test.ts`, `builtin-mcp-spawn.test.ts`, `remote/link-traffic-relaunch.test.ts` and `remote/link-events-stock.test.ts` (the real omp's captured frames replayed through a real lane) run through the core. Adapted where a case reached into what moved: a mocked `node:child_process`, fake timers over the stop's grace (now `tuning` and a shortened grace; each negative check says it waits), in-memory children. The cases of `rpc-bridge.test.ts`, `bridge-client.test.ts`'s socket rules, `omp-stop.test.ts`, `omp-start-queue`, `run-messages.test.ts`, `restart-policy` and `sessionOpen` are Rust tests (58 in `sidecar::`).
  - Formal: `formal/tab-session` and `formal/session-processes` `@code` tags point at `lane.rs`/`launch.rs`; their runs and restamps happen after the batch run (H1's gate).
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` lists the sidecar parity files (`.github/actions/native-parity`); `check` and `win-platform` run them in the node project; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes. Developed on Windows: the POSIX stop (process group, SIGTERM, SIGKILL, the `/bin/sh` stand-ins) is compile-checked here and runs first in CI. The lane and stop Rust tests that start processes run one at a time (run together on Windows, stand-ins went silent; cause not found).
- **R1-R3. The remote link** (`crates/core/src/remote/`; was `src/main/remote/crypto.ts`'s keys and key schedule, `identity.ts`'s file, `address.ts`, `wire.ts`'s framing, sealing, deflate context, ping and dead timer, `link-traffic.ts`, and the dial, probe and both handshakes of `client.ts` and `host-server.ts`): batch B2. The core keeps `remote-identity.json` (the private key never reaches main; `remote.identity` answers the device id and public key, which `index.ts` loads at launch beside the rest of the launch, since the device id is read synchronously everywhere), dials a host's candidate ports 250 ms apart (`remote.connect`), runs the client's handshake on the connected socket (`remote.handshake`, cancellable, the hello's capabilities handed in by main), probes (`remote.probe`, `remote.probeHost`), listens for a host (`remote.hostListen`, `remote.hostStop`: the first free candidate port on the address devices dial, then the same port on the others; closing never waits for the links it accepted) and runs each link: the 4-byte frames and the 16 MiB cap, AES-256-GCM under the strictly increasing 96-bit counter, deflate alone or against the link's 32 KiB context, ping every 10 s, dead after 30 s, `check`, and the host's traffic line once a minute. Main keeps what decides: the reconnect loop with its backoff, wake and stale-step rules (`RemoteClient`), the rebind and its log lines, the gate, the approval, the device records (`#bindDevice`) and the hello's answer (`RemoteHost`; the core asks `remote.ask` `gate`, `bind`, `open` and main answers `remote.answer`), and every request, answer and event on a link. Main holds a link by its number: `Link` (`wire.ts`) pushes `remote.send` (the message's JSON as the push's raw bytes) and the other commands, and hears `remote.message` (the peer's JSON as the event's bytes), `remote.checked`, `remote.closed`. The wire gained pushes: `{"push": method, params, bin}`, never answered, handled on the read loop before the next frame so a link's sends keep main's order (`server.rs` `Registry::add_push`, `CoreProcess.push`), and events may carry `bin`. A core that ends ends every link (`core.exited`, `CORE_EXITED`).
  - Decided with the main session (12 Nov 2026): R4 (`link-events.ts`) is not moved here and waits for the hubs (`docs/rust-remaining-units.md` §2.5: moved now it would make main serialize every raw omp event into the pipe and parse the rebuilt whole events back, up to ten times the slim stream, and copy the pool's slimmer). The trust stores (`trust-store.ts`, `hosts-store.ts`, `peers-store.ts`) and `secure-file.ts` stay TypeScript until R5/W1 move their readers (read in the same tick by `devices.ts` and `web/devices-store.ts`); `crypto.ts` keeps only `base32`, `isRawKey` and `randomDeviceId` for them.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: rows captured from the TypeScript before it was deleted (`remote/{crypto,codec,traffic}-captured.json`): Crockford base32 over 58 inputs, key pairs of 8 fixed secrets, 26 import rows, 308 `dh` rows with all 14 small-order peers refused, 8 transcript sets (quote, backslash, control characters, non-ASCII, U+2028), 30 HKDF rows, sealed frames at counters 0 to 2^96−1, 15 opener refusals, a 53-message context stream and a 19-message plain-deflate stream node sent (Rust inflates each to the same JSON, and node inflates Rust's: 93 020 bytes against node's 92 418 for the context stream, +0.65 %; 48 400 against 48 437 alone), 190 receiver cases (truncated, trailing data, 16 MiB exact and +1, a context sliding past 64 KiB), 19 traffic windows byte for byte and the deleted `link-traffic.test.ts`'s cases. `wire.test.ts` now runs the core's link against the released TypeScript link (`test-legacy-peer.ts`, `Link` and the handshakes as v0.127.0 ships them) over a real socket both ways: sealed messages, a context stream and a plain-deflate stream each way, pings, `dead`, `check`, `closed`, a graceful close, and the refusals (a failed tag, a counter that does not increase, a header past 16 MiB, a deflate bomb, a marker byte before `secure`, JSON that is no object or has no string `t`). `transport.test.ts` (40 cases), `devices.test.ts`, `crypto.test.ts` and the link-traffic suites run unchanged in what they assert, through the core; three of their seams changed with it: a port the system refuses is the host's `refusePorts` (was `bindPort`), a slow rebind is held in `bindHosts` (was the listen), and the traffic line's window is `trafficMs` (was a fake clock over the real sockets: the core's timers are its own). `e2e/remote-mixed.e2e.ts` pairs this build with an older release's app (`OMP_GUI_E2E_OLD_APP`, its built checkout) each way round and runs a prompt over the link.
  - Differences: a dial to a closed loopback port fails at once on Windows as node's did (`sys::no_syn_retries`, libuv's `SIO_TCP_INITIAL_RTO` setting; without it Windows retries the SYN for 2 s), on another address after the system's retries as before. A dial's failure message names node's code for refused, reset, timed out and unreachable (`connect ECONNREFUSED 127.0.0.1:1`) and the system's words otherwise. The traffic line is written by the core every minute of its own clock; a test window is `trafficMs`. A handshake message's strings are measured in UTF-16 units as zod did. A key whose base64url has non-canonical trailing bits imports as node imported it. The sealing counter panics at 2^96 where node threw. A host's link that main destroyed still writes its last traffic line. `remote.identity` replaces a hand-edited identity file with a lone-surrogate escape or nesting past 128 levels, which the TypeScript would have loaded. A core that dies drops every link at once (main reconnects as after any drop).
  - Measured (12 Nov 2026, this Windows PC, release builds, 9 rounds, `OMP_GUI_LINK_CPU=1 bunx vitest run src/main/remote/link-cpu-report.test.ts`, both ends in one process; the profile attributes each sample to the innermost frame of `src/main` or `src/shared` code, and the link's own is `wire.ts`, `link-traffic.ts` and `core/process.ts`): main's link work per frame 448.9 → 160.5 µs (450 tokens), 406.9 → 152.6 µs (900), 942.4 → 110.2 µs (1 800); main's whole busy time 137.6 → 170.6 ms, 189.7 → 176.3 ms, 1 036.4 → 352.8 ms (rounds vary by up to 5x on this loaded machine; the link's share fell in every round set). `ompdesk-core.exe` 8 307 200 → 8 946 688 bytes (win32-x64, with 8a's edge).
  - Crates (sizes are the release binary's growth above, licenses in `LICENSES/ompdesk-native-dependencies.txt`): `x25519-dalek` 3 (BSD-3-Clause), `hkdf` 0.13, `sha2` 0.11, `aes-gcm` 0.11, `base64` 0.23 (MIT OR Apache-2.0), `flate2` 1 with `zlib-rs` 0.6 (MIT OR Apache-2.0; Zlib), shared with 8a.
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `wire.test.ts`, `transport.test.ts`, `crypto.test.ts`, `devices.test.ts` against the macOS core; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes; the node project runs them on Linux and Windows. Batch runs: 43213428700 and 43216019870 red (lint; two hosts of one main binding in the core's order, now in the order asked; a rebind that listened again before its old listener closed, which Linux showed as the next port; the matrix guard's client meeting a reset after a 413), 43217367306 and 43247947993 green, each after one rerun of a Windows flake outside this batch (control socket's core-kill case, ssh forward).
  - Formal: `session-listing`'s link tags moved to the core's `drain` (`link.rs`), `FrameReader.push` (`core/process.ts`) and `Link.deliver` (`wire.ts`): the core opens a link's frames in order and hands each to main as one event through its one writer, and main delivers every frame of a chunk synchronously, so A1 (one ordered link) and F7 (frames behind a reply apply before the read continues) hold as modelled. `verify-all.ts formal --only session-listing --tier fast`: the runs and 20 of 20 mutations (2 declared equivalent) passed; its session-index tags were stale on `main` (that file shrank in 3c) and now point at the code as it is. `repo-lock-core` (`server.rs`, `sys.rs`, `process.ts` changed: pushes on the read loop start nothing, `no_syn_retries` is one ioctl, `push` writes one frame): 13 of 13 mutations detected. Both stamped after the commit.
- **8a. The web edge, built, not yet serving** (`crates/core/src/web/`): the Rust edge of `web/server.ts` and `auth.ts` (HTTP/1.1 on hyper, the static allowlist, the built `.br` and the brotli quality-9 fallback, CSP and every header, `checkRequest`'s order, the device cookie with only its secret's SHA-256 kept and compared in constant time, the 14-day sliding and 90-day absolute expiry, `/api/session`'s P-256 proof over the server nonce and its 30 s one-use ticket, the rate limits, the refusal page, the WebSocket upgrade with permessage-deflate), with no core method registered: main's TypeScript server still serves. It lands before 8b (decided by the main session under the owner's speed call, 12 Nov 2026), the ledger's first Rust that nothing calls yet; 8b cuts the server over.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: a matrix of 578 rows captured from the running TypeScript `WebServer` (pairwise over 14 dimensions: method, path, secret prefix absent/wrong/right/pending, `Host`, `Origin`, `Sec-Fetch-Site`, login absent/allowed/not allowed/blocked node, cookie none/valid/expired sliding/expired absolute/revoked, and the rows that show which wall wins when two fail; then rate-limit, session-proof, webhook, push, upload, blob and upgrade-refusal sequences): status, every header in order and the body's SHA-256, replayed against the Rust edge with no difference (`web/matrix.json`, 814 KB; `src/main/web/web-edge-matrix.test.ts` holds the stored rows to the TypeScript server, `OMPDESK_WEB_MATRIX=write` captures them again). P-256 proofs made by Playwright's Chromium and WebKit WebCrypto, 16 nonces each (`src/main/web/web-edge-p256.test.ts`, kept as Rust test vectors in `proof_browsers.rs`): all 32 accepted (15 with a high `s`, as node accepts them); a tampered nonce, a flipped bit at four places, another key, DER, 63, 65 and 0 bytes, an off-curve and a short key refused.
  - The WebSocket: `yawc` 0.5 frames and compresses; the edge answers the upgrade itself as `ws` does (yawc answers `client_max_window_bits=9` to a bare offer, `ws` a bare `permessage-deflate`). `ws` applies its 1024-byte threshold only when `server_no_context_takeover` was agreed, so with what Chromium offers every message is compressed (200 of 200 in the capture); the edge sets the threshold only then (200/200, 80/200 and 0/200 compressed frames, as `ws`). zlib-rs at level 3 compresses less than zlib at 3, so the edge uses 6: a 200-message stream of 219 765 bytes is 19 946 bytes against `ws`'s 19 700 (+1.25 %), 53 074 against 52 979 with no takeover, 21 441 against 21 185 at window bits 10.
  - Differences: hyper writes no `Keep-Alive: timeout=5` and lowercase header names on the 101, and may add `Date` to it; the 120 s request timeout and the 5 s keep-alive idle are not mirrored (the 15 s header timeout is); one-use tickets read the injected clock, where `OneTimeTokens` read `Date.now`; an unread request body up to 1 MiB is read before answering (otherwise Windows resets the socket before the client reads the answer); `+value` spellings of window bits (hex, exponents) are not read as `ws` read them; a webhook detail or device name cut inside a surrogate pair differs.
  - Crates: `p256` 0.14 (ECDSA verify), `brotli` 8 (BSD-3-Clause AND MIT), `subtle` 2 (BSD-3-Clause), `yawc` 0.5 (MPL-2.0, file-level; the one maintained crate found that negotiates permessage-deflate with context takeover; `tungstenite` 0.30 has no permessage-deflate, `soketto` 0.8 always asks for no context takeover), `sha1` 0.10 and `futures-util` 0.3 (already in the lock through yawc: the accept digest and sending frames), hyper's `server` feature.
  - Not verified: a node `ws` client against the Rust edge; Linux and macOS runs of the matrix (CI's `native` and `mac-native` run them).
- **8b. The web edge serving** (`crates/core/src/web/{host,relay}.rs` beside 8a's edge, the stores in `store.rs`, `uploads.rs`, `blobs.rs`, `archive.rs`; was `src/main/web/server.ts`, `auth.ts`, `refusal-page.ts`, the edge's half of `rate-limit.ts`, and the logic of `devices-store.ts`, `blocks-store.ts`, `serve-secret.ts`, `uploads.ts`, `blobs.ts`, `asset-archive.ts`): batch W1. The core listens on `127.0.0.1` (`web.start {host, userData, port, staticRoot}` → `{port}`, a taken port the fault `EADDRINUSE` in node's words; `web.stop {host}`) and answers every request with 8a's edge over the core's own stores of that `userData`. `server.ts` is `WebServer`'s client with the same `start`, `stop`, `configure` and `port`. Main keeps what the edge asks per request or per socket (`relay.rs`: the event `web.ask {call, host, kind, …}` answered by `web.answer`): the policy (`policy`: published address, serve secrets, allowed logins; read for every request, so `configure` stays synchronous and Mobile web's service and Settings stay its owners), the desktop's approval (`confirmNew`, `confirm`: `pairing.ts`, which computer sharing's gate shares), `whois` (main's located CLI), the schedule service's webhook (`hook`), and until 8c the router (`count`, `retire`, `attach`): a page socket stays in the core, its text frames reach `WsRouter` by the core's connection number (`web.ws.frame` with the text as `bin`, `web.ws.close`, `web.ws.gone`) and the router writes back with ordered pushes (`web.ws.send` + `bin`, `web.ws.ping`, `web.ws.close`, `web.ws.terminate`). Events `web.deviceConnected`, `web.unpairedAuth`.
  - The stores: `web-devices.json`, `web-blocked.json`, `web-serve-secret.json` (0600, atomic, `{v: 1, …}`) are one `FileDevices`/`FileBlocks` per `userData` (`store::stores`), shared by the edge and main's requests; changes are applied under the store's lock and written by an ordered writer thread, sent to main as `web.devices.changed`/`web.devices.revoked`/`web.blocks.changed` with the whole list (`version`, `ack` of main's `seq`); `web.devices.flush` answers once earlier writes are on disk, and `serve` flushes at its input's end. `WebDevices`, `WebBlocks`, `ServeSecretStore` (`web-stores-client.ts`) are synchronous copies filled by `web.devices.open` and kept by those events; a change made here applies at once and goes as an ordered push. `WebHost.ready()` resolves once the copies are in, and `index.ts` waits for it before anything reads them (the sharing gate's block check, the paired devices' layouts, which are not restored from a copy that never filled). Uploads, blobs and the build archive: `web.uploads.*`, `web.blobs.*`, `web.assets.*`; main mints a blob's token and registers it by an ordered push before the page can see the URL, and a media blob's bytes come from main's `MediaStreams` (`web.media.open` → pushes `web.media.head`/`chunk`/`end`, a 1 MiB window by `web.media.ack`, `web.media.cancel`, `web.media.drop`).
  - The packaged renderer lies outside the asar (`asarUnpack: out/renderer/**` in both builder configs, pinned by `packaging-config.test.ts`): the core reads the page it serves as plain files, and `WebHost` hands it the `app.asar.unpacked` path. The desktop window still loads it through the asar path.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `WsRouter` and the web-only calls (8c, after 9a); `pairing.ts` (computer sharing's gate asks the same desktop); the policy object and its readers (Mobile web's service, Settings); `WebBlocks.check` as a synchronous rule over the copy, for `RemoteDevices.allowsPeer`, which `host-services.ts` reads synchronously: a second copy of `FileBlocks::check` pinned by the same 55 captured visitors, deleted with the sharing gate's move (R5, batch W2); `BlobStore`'s token minting and `UploadStore.owns` (synchronous callers in `ws-router.ts`, `host-actions.ts`).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: 8a's matrix (578 rows captured from the last TypeScript server, `matrix.json`) still replays against the edge with no difference (`web::matrix_replay`); `web-edge-matrix.test.ts`, whose subject was the TypeScript server, is gone with it, and `auth.test.ts`, `refusal-page.test.ts` and the edge's limiter cases of `rate-limit.test.ts` are the Rust tests of `auth.rs`, `refusal.rs`, `limits.rs`. `server.test.ts` (the whole server on a port behind the prefix: static files, walls, pairing with and without approval, blocks, the key proof, a WebSocket call, uploads and blobs, revocation closing 4401, push settings, the stop's 1001), `webhook.test.ts` and `web-push.integration.test.ts` run through the core; `devices-store`, `blocks-store`, `serve-secret`, `web-stores-client`, `uploads`, `blobs`, `asset-archive` tests run through the process. The browsers' P-256 proofs (`testdata/browser-p256.json`) are accepted and refused alike by the Rust verifier (`proof_browsers.rs`) and by node's, which `web-edge-p256.test.ts` now holds as its own oracle.
  - Differences: a change main makes reaches the edge over the core's pipe and one the edge makes reaches main's copy as an event, while a page's request takes the network, so the next request may still see the state before a change made in the same millisecond (the tests wait for `flush()`; `settled()` in `server.test.ts`); a write is asynchronous, so a core that dies loses changes not yet written (the quit flushes); a core that ends closes every page socket and `WebServer` listens again on the same port, where the TypeScript server lived as long as main; blobs and the one-use tickets do not survive a core restart; a queue past 8 MiB closes the socket 4408 in the edge (`deps.rs` `Outbox`, counted until the socket wrote it) where the router read `ws`'s `bufferedAmount`; the edge asks main for the policy on every request (one pipe round trip); `create`/`authenticate` are no TypeScript calls; a record without `nodeId` is written with `"nodeId": null`; a stored key that is no P-256 key is an unusable key; the differences 8a names (hyper's headers on the 101, no 120 s request timeout, the zlib-rs level). Uploads and blobs: `release`, `mediaUrl`, `count` and `sweep` are asynchronous; a name cut inside a surrogate pair reads U+FFFD; a media stream that fails after its head ends cleanly.
  - Not measured: speed (the edge's per-request ask adds one pipe round trip, about 0.1 ms as 2b measured; WebSocket frames take one more hop until 8c).
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `server.test.ts`, `webhook.test.ts`, the store and byte suites and the push suites against the real core; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes; the e2e pairing and phone specs (`mobile-web`, `mobile-attach`, `phone-*`, `pwa-install`) run the built app's core edge.
- **8d. Web Push** (`crates/core/src/web/push.rs`; was `src/main/web/push.ts`'s transport, `vapid-store.ts` and the `web-push` package): `push.vapid {userData}` → `{publicKey, subject, created}` makes or reads `<userData>/web-push.json` (v 1, public and private key, 0600, atomic) and keeps the pair in the core (`loaded` is the edge's `/api/push` key); `push.send {userData, subscription, payload, ttl, urgency, topic?, timeoutMs}` → `{statusCode}` signs the VAPID token (ES256; `aud` the endpoint's origin, `exp` 12 hours, `sub` `https://github.com/SeuPut0705/ompdesk`), encrypts the payload (RFC 8291 over RFC 8188 `aes128gcm`, 4096-byte records, no padding) and POSTs it once with `TTL`, `Urgency`, `Topic`, `Content-Encoding`, `Content-Type` and `Authorization: vapid t=…, k=…`. The private key never leaves the core.
  - What main keeps: `PushSender` (the 3800-byte payload limit, the 10 s ceiling, the 404/410 → forget rule, the log lines) and `web-push-notifier.ts` with its debounce and `NotificationBatcher`, because the batcher also serves the desktop's notifications and `SidecarPool.observeTabs` moves with the hubs (H2): a Rust copy would be a second copy of one rule. `isPushEndpoint` is the edge's alone (`server.rs` `is_push_endpoint`, its table a Rust test).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: RFC 8291 Appendix A's body byte for byte; a round trip that decrypts with the subscriber's key at nine sizes around the record boundary; the token verifies with the public key (Rust and `push.test.ts` with node's verify), and the pushed body decrypts with `http_ece`, an independent implementation (a devDependency); `web-push.json` files as `webpush.generateVAPIDKeys` and `JSON.stringify` wrote them load untouched; the damaged-file cases of `vapid-store.test.ts` make a new pair and report `created`.
  - Differences: the timeout covers the whole request (the package's was a socket idle timeout); a redirect is an answer and is not followed, as `https.request` took it; the core POSTs to `http` only for a loopback host (the tests' push service; a page cannot subscribe such an address); faults are coded (`push_connect`, `push_tls`, `push_broken`, `push_timeout`, …) and carry no endpoint, which is a capability URL; the package's `gcmAPIKey`, `proxy`, `agent`, `aesgcm` and `Crypto-Key` paths are gone (none was used); a request is not kept alive; a `WebHost` that never starts no longer drops subscriptions after a new key (`WebHost.start` does it).
  - Crates: none new; `p256` gained its `ecdh` feature (MIT OR Apache-2.0). `web-push` and its 12 npm components leave `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.txt`.
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `push.test.ts`, `vapid-store.test.ts`, `web-push-notifier.test.ts` and `web-push.integration.test.ts` through the real core; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes.
- **8e. Mobile web's state machine** (`crates/core/src/tailscale/{service,version}.rs`; was the logic of `src/main/tailscale/service.ts`, `compareVersions` of `src/shared/version.ts` for it, `loginUrlAllowed` of `cli.ts`): the phases, the poll tick's reading of `status --json`/`serve status --json`, enable/disable, install, start-app, sign-in with its 10-minute window, OS approval after 20 s, publish → confirm → unpublish around 4a's functions (called in the core), the block retry (60 s), Funnel stopping the page's server, allowed users and confirm-new-devices are core methods (`mobileWeb.step`, `act`, `start`, `whois`, `adopt`, `dispose`), with events `mobileWeb.state {service, state, pollMs, seq}`, `mobileWeb.ask` (answered by `mobileWeb.answer`) and `mobileWeb.refresh`. The page's server is behind the trait `WebServerPort`, answered by main (`AsksMain`): `WebHost.start` also opens the push key and the stores' copies and keeps the build, which are main's.
  - What main keeps, and why: the `SerialChain` and its 10-minute step bound (shared machinery); the poll's timer (the core answers `pollMs`; `service.test.ts` moves time with vitest's fake timers); the prefs, the setup item, the located CLI with main's environment, the web server, the browser and the OS settings, which the core asks for; the synchronous `allowedUsers()`, `confirmNewDevices()` and `install`; the zod check of the page's request.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: `service.test.ts` passes unchanged through the real core (60 cases, with `cli.test.ts`); `service-restart.test.ts` (new) covers a core that ends; `compareVersions` 21 rows and `loginUrlAllowed` 10 rows from the TypeScript; five Rust tests run the machine over a shell stand-in (publish and take down, a stopped step, 20 s OS approval, 10-minute sign-in, the allow list).
  - Differences: the 10-minute bound counts in main; a stopped step is cancelled in the core, its questions dropped and a late answer ignored (Rust test `a_stopped_step_asks_nothing_more_and_writes_nothing`); the CLI's environment is read once per located CLI; a time a request names (`now`) is that step's whole clock; the server's `configure` is a question that is waited for; a core that ended loses the state and main reads the switch from the prefs again (`coreLost`); a launch with Mobile web off sends one request (`mobileWeb.start`).
  - Crates: none new. Binary 7 331 136 → 7 546 400 bytes (+2.9 %, release, macOS arm64).
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `service.test.ts`, `cli.test.ts`, `service-restart.test.ts`; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes (the stand-in machine tests POSIX-only).
- **8c. The page router** (`crates/core/src/web/router.rs`; was `src/main/web/ws-router.ts`'s `WsConnection` and the transport half of `WsRouter`, and `rate-limit.ts`'s `TokenBucket` and `ConcurrencyLimiter`): batch W2, on W1's 8b edge. The core holds every page connection the edge upgrades (`Router`/`Connection` of `deps.rs`): up to four a device, the 200/s-burst-400 message bucket, the 1 MiB frame cap, `FrameSchema` (`inv`, `snd`, `ack`, `presence`, `ping`, `pong`), the `hello`, the pushes numbered and batched for 75 ms with a tab's neighbouring batches joined and their slim `message_update` deltas merged, the streams dropped while the page is hidden and the hello again on its return, the 20 s heartbeat that ends a socket after three quiet ticks (a late tick counts for nothing), resume (`resume.<connId>`, ended connections kept 10 min, 64 at most) and retire, and each call's checks: the device revoked (4401), `IPC_POLICY`'s direction and permission, the boundary, `pairing-admin` never, the web-only table, `mobileWeb.*` prefs, four heavy calls a device. `web.start` hands the router `IPC_POLICY`, the web-only table's direction, permission and heavy flag, the channel names it treats apart (`EVENTS_BATCH`, the stream channels, the heavy ones, `PREFS_SET`), the protocol version, the features and the build, all as data from main (`WsRouter.config`), so no channel name or protocol number is spelled in Rust. A host's router lives from its first start to the core's end, through `web.stop`.
  - The wire to main, each handled in order on one stream: `web.call {host, call, answer, device, connId, dir, ch}` with the arguments' JSON as `bin` (a call the checks let through); `web.reply {host, call, ok, e}` with the value's JSON as `bin` (written after the batch is flushed, so after every push main sent before it); `web.deliver {host, device, ch}` with the data's JSON as `bin`, or with no `ch` when main's copy says the device has no connection (one per absence: it counts as a push, so a page that comes back is not resumed, and a connection that came meanwhile resyncs); `web.conns {host, device, conns: [{connId, visible}], change, connId}` (main's copy for `isConnected` and `isDeviceVisible`, `onConnectionsChanged` on `attach`/`end`, `onConnectionEnded` on `end`); `web.resync {host, conn, device}` → main runs `onResync` (the pool's whole stream snapshots) → `web.resynced {host, conn}` → the hello `resumed: false` goes, and pushes reach that connection only from then; `web.closeDevice`. The edge's own asks (`relay.rs`: the policy, pairing, whois, hooks) stay as 8b left them; its router half (`RelayRouter`, `web.ws.*`, the count/retire/attach asks) is gone.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: main's side of `ws-router.ts` runs each call (`registry.invoke`/`send` with the device's `WebClientWindow`, `windows.callerFor(device, connId)`), because the model-use quota reserves and refunds synchronously on the device records `PeerQuota` and the device lists read in the same tick (§1.3 a, c), the pool's `activeTab` names the driven tab, a picture's upload is landed with `src/shared/web-protocol.ts`'s `holdsBlock`/`replaceBlocks`/`isWebUploadedImage` (the page's rules, d) and stock's `image.toModel`, and the answer is shaped through `BlobStore`'s synchronous token minting; it also keeps the tokens a turn spends (`#recordTokens`, `PeerQuota.record`) and makes a push's JSON (below). `file-edit-reports.ts` stays (§1.3 table: read synchronously by `ipc.ts`, `index.ts` and agent-control; its report still reaches it within the `web.call` event, before the `web.conns` that ends the connection). `host-actions.ts` stays: of its eleven channels, `open`, `reveal` and `openExternal` need Electron's `shell`, the two models.yml ones are `models-config.ts` (§1.3 d), `save` and `blobOpen` read the pool's tab folder in the same tick (`deps.tabs.cwd`, c) and mint blob tokens through the synchronous client, and `listDirs` alone would split one table across two processes for no measured gain; the core checks its direction, permission and heavy flag. `web-host.ts` is W1's (start and stop); the router's part of it is the wiring.
  - Measured (13 Nov 2026, this MacBook, M5 Pro under the other sessions' load, `OMP_GUI_FANOUT_CPU=<dir> OMP_GUI_FANOUT_WINDOWS=1 OMP_GUI_FANOUT_COMPUTERS=0 OMP_GUI_FANOUT_ROUNDS=11 OMP_GUI_FANOUT_PAGES=<n> bunx vitest run src/main/remote/fanout-cpu-report.test.ts`, 5 956 events, base 42900f45 with its page sockets handing every frame to the core as 8b's relay did (`web.ws.send`), alternated with this change, two runs each, medians of 11): main's CPU 17.1 / 38.4 / 46.0 / 66.0 ms at 0 / 1 / 2 / 4 pages before, 16.9 / 34.7 / 33.1 / 40.4 after (−9 %, −28 %, −39 %); each page past the first added about 9 ms before and about 2 after. Before, each connection's frame text was encoded again for the pipe (`utf8Write` 11 ms of 4 pages); after, a stream's JSON is made once for every device (`#dataBytes`) and its bytes go to the core once per device. Main hands the core 10.31 MB a page (687 pushes) where it handed 9.95 MB (517 batched frames). The probe that decided the build (9 rounds, a quieter machine; a stand-in that only handed each push to the core) gave 27.9 / 26.1 / 26.0 ms at 1 / 2 / 4 pages against the relay's 31.2 / 39.0 / 50.1 ms.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: `coalesceEvents`' output for 300 random queues captured from the TypeScript before it was deleted (`router-captured.json`; joined and merged batches, extra keys, escapes, `1e21`, `1e-7`, `-0`, emoji, U+2028), every frame text byte for byte (`web::router` tests; a pushed data text is spliced unread and a joined one built from the pieces as written); `TokenBucket`'s burst, refill and cap, the frame schema's refusals and the `mobileWeb.*` guard as Rust tests. `ws-router.test.ts` (55 cases) drives the real core's router through its test entry (`web.router.test.open`, `web.router.test`: no edge, the test's clock and timers, each socket's frames read back as events before the call answers; `ws-router-test-rig.ts`, which `e2e/fixture/preview-stream-main.ts` uses with real timers); its cases are the TypeScript's, awaited, plus one for the `mobileWeb.*` guard; "writes a streamed push's data once" now counts one text per push of a status where each connection wrote its own. `server.test.ts` (58), `host-actions.test.ts` (14) and `file-edit-reports.test.ts` (10) pass unchanged. `fanout-cpu-report.test.ts` counts what main hands the core for each page, as it counts a computer's link.
  - Differences: a non-stream push's JSON is made when main pushes it (once a device), where each connection wrote its own at its batch's end, so a handler that changes an object after pushing it no longer changes what goes out; a push `JSON.stringify` cannot write is dropped in main without using a `seq` (the router skipped one); a first hello, and the hello after streams were dropped while hidden, go once main prepared the resync, one round trip later, and a push main made before that is dropped for that connection (the page loads its state after the hello); the resume entries live in the core, so a core that ended forgets them and a page that comes back after it is not resumed (the sockets ended with it anyway); a call id or `ack` past 2^64, or a fractional one, is a malformed frame (zod took any non-negative integer); two halves of a surrogate pair split across joined deltas stay two escapes (JSON-equal, not byte-equal), and a delta that is not a string is never joined (dead: deltas are strings); a key of a joined object is written again by serde (the keys are the code's ASCII names); a connection's heavy slot is freed when main's answer arrives (the handler had settled just before); a quota refusal of a model command whose pictures came by upload still refuses before landing them (main meters first, as before). The router's test entry ships in the binary, as `core.echo` and `test:ipc.*` do, and serves only a host a test opened.
  - Crates: none. `ompdesk-core` 11 169 440 → 11 301 872 bytes (+1.2 %, release, macOS arm64, 13 Nov 2026).
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` adds `ws-router.test.ts` to the web files it runs (`server.test.ts` was there); `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes; the e2e web specs (`mobile-web`, `mobile-attach`, `phone-*`, `web-no-reload`, `preview-stream`) are the gate's.
- **R8. Bytes** (`crates/core/src/remotebytes/`; was the I/O of `src/main/remote/transcript-cache.ts`, `image-blob-cache.ts`, `sign-in-relay.ts` and `preview-relay-tunnels.ts`): batch W2. The core keeps a device's copies of its hosts' transcripts (`remotebytes.readJournal`, `remotebytes.page`, `remotebytes.keepPage`: `<userData>/remote-transcripts`, 128 MB) and their pictures (`remotebytes.resolveBlobs`: `<userData>/remote-image-blobs`, 256 MiB, the SHA-256 address check, one read per blob for concurrent reads), both bounded by one `Folder` (the measured size, the trim to three quarters, least recently read first; one copy where the TypeScript had two), with one read of a key at a time; it listens for a paired host's browser sign-in on this computer's loopback (`remotebytes.signInStart`, push `remotebytes.signInClose`, event `remotebytes.signInEnded`: the Host header, path, `state`, method, one use, the 409/404/403/502 answers and the failure page, the timeout); and on a host it holds the WebSocket to a tab's browser relay (`remotebytes.relayOpen`, pushes `remotebytes.relaySend`/`remotebytes.relayClose`, event `remotebytes.relay`). Main keeps every request on the link (R1-R3), so what only the host has is asked of main: the event `remotebytes.ask` (`journal` {fromByte, knownTail}, `blob` {ref}, `signIn` {path, query}) names the caller main registered (`asker`, `src/main/remote/bytes-asks.ts`), main answers `remotebytes.answer` or says the caller failed, and `askingCall` rethrows the caller's own error (`ask_failed`). `TranscriptCache`, `ImageBlobCache`, `startSignInRelay` and `PreviewRelayTunnels` keep their signatures as the typed clients.
  - Stayed, by §1.3 of `docs/rust-remaining-units.md`: `image-reads.ts` (no I/O of its own, b: an in-memory LRU of data URLs around main's link request, `ipc.ts:2506` `new RemoteImageReads()`, `ipc.ts:2514` `imageReads.read`; in the core every picture would cross the pipe twice); `host-upload.ts` (b: it only cuts a file into parts for main's link request, `ipc.ts:2634`; the parts' 2-minute idle drop and per-device cap are the host's, `host-services.ts` `UPLOAD_PART_IDLE_MS`, R6); `link-relay-socket.ts` (b: its I/O is main's link request; c: `preview/host.ts:816` reads `isOpen`, `devices.ts:1087` calls `linkLost()` and `devices.ts:1194` `receive()` in the same tick); of `preview-relay-tunnels.ts` the map of tab, endpoint, socket and owning connection (c: `host-services.ts:1607` `has(key)` and `host-services.ts:1437` `dropConnection(conn)`; the guard that only a relay the tab's own bridge answered is dialed, `relayExtensionUrl`, stays with it); of `sign-in-relay.ts` the reading of the authorization URL (`loopbackCallback`, shared with `local/settings.ts` and `host-services.ts`); of `image-blob-cache.ts` the walk that finds the references (`imageBlobSites`, shared with the host's `withImageRefs`; in the core whole transcripts would cross the pipe).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: rows captured from the TypeScript before it was deleted (`remotebytes/captured.json`): a journal written in four steps (read whole, grown, unchanged, grown) with what each step asked the host and the folder's bytes after it, messages with non-ASCII, U+2028, control characters and numbers JSON spells unlike serde (`1e+21`, `123456789012345680000`); the folder the TypeScript left (journal and tail page) read back to the same messages, offset and page; its picture folder read back to the same base64; node's lenient base64 over 28 inputs (both alphabets, skipped characters, the first `=` the end, short last groups); 20 Host headers and 22 request targets read as `new URL` did (`url` crate); the failure page over 3 sets of words. Numbers, tails and messages are kept as the JSON text main sent and never re-encoded, so a file is byte for byte the TypeScript's. `transcript-cache.test.ts` (6), `image-blob-cache.test.ts` (4), `sign-in-relay.test.ts` (11), `remote-sidecar.test.ts` (39) and `link-traffic-images.test.ts` run unchanged through the core, as do the tunnel, upload and sign-in cases of `host-services.test.ts` and `devices.test.ts`. `OMP_GUI_LINK_TRAFFIC=1` `link-traffic-images.test.ts`: 9 974 KB for the current device before and after (running 4 334 KB in 27 requests, completed 3 028 KB, store reset 1 KB, relaunch 1 306 KB, picture changed 1 306 KB; 0.86.1 31 356 KB both times).
  - Differences: a folder's measured size, its key turns and its shared blob reads are the core's per folder, not per `TranscriptCache`/`ImageBlobCache` (main makes one of each per folder). A journal, page or blob read from disk has its time set before the answer, where the TypeScript set it beside. A core call that waits on the host is bounded at 120 s (the host request inside has its own). A sign-in relay closed before the core answered its start is closed once the start is answered, and reports no state; a relay's `close()` reaches the core a moment later, so a callback in that moment hears the stand-in's 409 (it forwards nothing once closed) where node's closed listener refused the connection; one the core could not start reports its port as not listening, so the panel shows the paste; hyper's answers carry `Content-Length` and `Date` as it spells them. The relay socket is yawc's client, which offers no permessage-deflate (undici offered it); a binary frame from the relay goes on as lossy UTF-8 text (node's WebSocket made it `[object Blob]`); a frame past 16 MiB fails the read and ends the tunnel with `close` (was: the link refused the push, which ended it); a send between the relay's end and main hearing of it is dropped, not refused.
  - Crates: none new. `ompdesk-core` 7 397 328 → 7 745 408 bytes (darwin-arm64); inferred, not measured: the growth is hyper's server and yawc's client handshake, which nothing called before (8a's edge serves nothing yet).
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` runs `transcript-cache.test.ts`, `image-blob-cache.test.ts`, `sign-in-relay.test.ts` and `link-traffic-images.test.ts` against the macOS core; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes.
- **7e. Memory between computers** (`crates/core/src/memsync/`; was `src/main/ai-memory/sync.ts`, 1 043 lines to 246): batch W2. The core runs a device's rounds with each host (`device.rs`: pages, then conclusions, each pulled then pushed; batches of 25 pages or 1 MB, 40 batches a round, 200 conclusions a batch; the agreement pairs `base`, the `remote` hashes, `pending`, the conflict rule and its log line, the identity pass), keeps `<userData>/ai-memory/sync.json` (`state.rs`, written with `disk/tree.rs` `write_atomic_if`, 0600), answers a device's `host.memory.*` from this computer's server (`host.rs`), and holds what both sides compute (`wire.rs`: `page_key`, `page_hash`, `cut_bytes`, a conclusion's cut and checks, every bound of the link's schema, the durable paths). It reads and writes this computer's server through 5d's client in process (`store.rs`, one `Client` per request, so the token is read per request as before), including `read_conclusions`' reset rule. Methods: `memsync.servePull`, `servePush`, `serveConclusionPull`, `serveConclusionPush` (`{server, self, peer, params}`, `server` null while memory is off), `memsync.readConclusions`, `memsync.start`, `sync`, `hostOnline`, `syncAll`, `dispose` (`{sync, stateFile, intervalMs?, started, host?}`: a sync is made on its first call, so a core that started again picks it up at the next link-up, which every host goes through since the links die with the core, or at memory-ready), `memsync.answer`; events `memsync.ask`, `memsync.log`.
  - What stays main's, asked with `memsync.ask`: `request` (one request over a host's link through `RemoteDevices.request`; **the link seam**: `Link::request` in `memsync/mod.rs` becomes an in-core call when R5-R7 move the links), `state` (the server or null, the device id, the connected hosts, the clock; `now` is main's so the identity-pass case keeps its fake clock) and `redact` (`redactDiagnosticsText`: `resources/gui-bridge/redactor.ts` is the one redactor, which memory capture embeds and diagnostics read, so a Rust copy would be a second definition; one ask per batch). `src/main/ai-memory/sync.ts` (246) is the typed client: the four host functions and `readConclusions` are one core call each, `MemorySync` answers the asks for its sync and refuses rounds after `dispose`. `host-services.ts` keeps its gate and the zod check of `host.memory.*` (main's link code), so `isDurablePath` and the wire's bounds are held in `app-protocol.ts` and in `wire.rs`, the Rust pinned to the TypeScript by the captured rows.
  - `recall-terms.ts` stays: it fails §1.3 (d). Its terms come from `addConclusionTerms` (`resources/gui-bridge/modes/memory-recall-core.ts`), the bridge's own tokenizer, which the bridge's coverage gate reads `recall-terms.json` with; a Rust copy would be a second definition. It now reads both streams through `memsync.readConclusions`, so the reset rule has one owner.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: rows captured from the TypeScript at `dc549cf3` before it was deleted (`memsync/captured.json`): 47 page hashes (NFC and NFD, emoji and astral characters, controls, U+2028/2029, BOM, quotes and backslashes, tags and relations whose UTF-16 order differs from their code-point order (U+FF5E against 😀), empty relations, kind absent or empty, expiry absent, empty or set, a 10 KB body), 13 cuts (a 4-byte character across the cap), 19 durable paths, 44 pages on the wire byte for byte with their key order (every bound at its edge and one past, 256 KiB bodies in 2- and 4-byte characters, a repository with and without its source), 28 conclusions as main's redactor left them (a token, JavaScript whitespace in ids and bodies, the 500-character title cut on emoji). `state.rs`: a `sync.json` the TypeScript wrote reads and writes back byte for byte, one from before conclusions gets both cursors at 0, files zod refused start over. Parity against the real core: `sync.test.ts` 36, `recall-terms.test.ts` 3, the memory cases of `host-services.test.ts` 2; their fake stores answer behind `ai-memory/memory-store-test-rig.ts` (a 127.0.0.1 server with the routes the core's client asks), and the one pure case of `sync.test.ts` (`pageHash`) is the Rust `hashes_what_a_page_holds_not_where_it_came_from_or_when`.
  - Across versions (run once, 12 Nov 2026, not kept: it imports the deleted TypeScript): `dc549cf3`'s TypeScript `servePull`/`servePush`/`serveConclusion*` against the core's device side, the TypeScript `MemorySync` against the core's host side, both over two fake servers (one lowercasing entities, as the write path does) holding NFC/NFD, emoji, a retired page, a 300 KB page that stays home, both sides' edits of one page, identities named differently on each side and conclusions with a token: each pair settled in one round (4 pulled, 2 pushed, the one seeded conflict, 2 + 1 conclusions), then three rounds moved nothing (no request carried a page, no write, no conflict, no `failed`), both servers held the same memory and no conclusion kept the token. A `sync.json` the TypeScript wrote was read by the core, three quiet rounds wrote it back byte for byte (2 161 bytes), and the reverse (2 761 bytes).
  - Differences: a reply that fails the link's schema logs `failed` with `invalid memory page` (and the like) where zod listed its issues; a lone surrogate (only a hostile peer could send one; the servers' JSON never holds one) reads as U+FFFD, so its hash differs; a round writes its host's state back when it ends, where the TypeScript saved the shared object, so a crash during one host's round no longer keeps another round's half-moved cursors; titles and bodies are redacted in one ask per batch instead of per row.
  - No new crate (`sha2` came with R1-R3). `ompdesk-core` 7 331 136 → 7 662 256 bytes (+331 120, darwin-arm64, release, rustc 1.98.1).

- **R4. The link's event encoding, built, not yet called** (`crates/core/src/remote/events/`; the logic of `src/main/remote/link-events.ts`, `message-update-slim.ts`, `subagent-frames.ts`'s `slimSubagentFrame` and `frame-bookkeeping.ts`'s `withoutFrameBookkeeping`): batch W2. Every encoder and decoder of `link-events.ts` as plain Rust over `serde_json::Value` (`preserve_order`): the windows' slimmer (`slim::Slimmer`: `new`, `slim(&'a [Value]) -> Cow<'a, [Value]>`, borrowed when nothing changed, `reset`; `slim_shared` with a `SharedBatch` kept beside the batch gives receivers that stand at one message one `Arc` result, as `createMessageUpdateSlimmer`'s `WeakMap` did), the usage dropper and restorer, `FieldDelta` and `ItemsDelta` with `shift` and `~fields`, the status, read (`ReadEncoder` with its epoch, `ReadDecoder` with its last four) and progress encoders and decoders, the `~sent` references with read groups and `fieldPatch`, `~tool`, `~output` (UTF-16 offsets, `CONTINUATION_TRIES`), `~args`, the 256-entry memories and their eviction order (`delta::Kept`), `encodedReadKey`, the device's rebuild of the text, thinking and tool call blocks, and the sender (`encoder::Sender`: the 32 ms batch, frames before an events batch, a new encoder after a failed send, `dispose`; it owns no clock: `subagent_frame` answers the timer generation to arm and `timer(generation)` runs it, so a flush in between makes a stale timer a no-op). `js.rs` gives what JavaScript gave the TypeScript for free: `JSON.stringify`'s text (key order, number spelling, escapes), `===`, `isDeepStrictEqual`, equality of two `JSON.stringify`s, `String()`, truthiness and a spread's key order (array-index keys first).
  - Nothing calls it yet (as 8a). Main holds the raw omp events until the pool's events reach the core (H2), so `link-events.ts` and its callers stay: `host-services.ts` (`#emit`, `#sender`, the read encoders) and `remote-sidecar.ts` (its decoders). The cutover: the core's tab lane (`sidecar::subscribe(tab)`, H2) feeds one `encoder::Sender` per tab and connection, whose tab events go to the link as `js::stringify` text (serde's writer spells some numbers otherwise, `1e21` for JavaScript's `1e+21`); `createLinkEventSender`, `createLinkEventEncoder`, `createReadEncoder` and `LocalHostServices`'s sender are deleted then. The device's side (`Decoder`, `ReadDecoder`, `encoded_read_key`) takes over with R7. `remote.events.open`/`call`/`close` are the parity suite's plumbing; nothing else calls them.
  - The one slimmer, and the copy that stays until the shell swap: after the cutover the windows' stream still goes through `createMessageUpdateSlimmer` in `sidecar-pool.ts` (with `subagent-frames.ts`'s batcher, and `withoutFrameBookkeeping` where the sidecar takes each frame), because the event batcher that feeds the windows moves only with the shell swap (decided with H2, 12 Nov 2026); from then `slim::Slimmer` is the one copy.
  - Kept exactly, with tests: every call the TypeScript tests make on the encoding, captured as rows (the input, the options, the output's JSON text, a sender's tab events and what each send answered; `captured/*.json.gz`, 1.5 MB, written by `src/main/remote/link-events-capture.ts` under `OMPDESK_LINK_EVENTS_CAPTURE=write` through a `vi.mock` that otherwise passes the modules through): `link-events.test.ts` (534 streams, 18 126 calls), `message-update-slim.test.ts` (43), `subagent-frames.test.ts` and `frame-bookkeeping.test.ts` (10), the installed omp's run of `link-events-stock.test.ts` (57), and the traffic rig of `link-traffic-report.test.ts` (61 streams, 1 314 calls, 158 MB of JSON: the 450, 900 and 1 800-token replies, a subagent's stream, tool output and list reads through a real `LocalHostServices` and `RemoteSidecar`, for older and current devices). The Rust tests (`remote::events::tests`, 6) replay them all, encoders and decoders, with no difference in output text or tab events; `src/main/remote/link-events-core.test.ts` (12 cases) replays them through the real binary and holds the rows to the TypeScript as it is now, so a change to the TypeScript encoding fails there until the rows are written again and the Rust follows.
  - Differences: an encoded read without `status` reads `null` where it read `undefined` (no host sends one); shapes the TypeScript threw a `TypeError` on (a `message_update` without `assistantMessageEvent`, a frame without `payload`, an `agent_end` whose `messages` is no array) pass as they came; `partial.content[contentIndex]` reads arrays only, where an object with index keys was read too; a lone surrogate cannot reach a Rust string (`json.rs` reads it as U+FFFD), so an output continuation over such a text may differ; the usage memories and a tool end's output are keyed by `String()` of the subagent or call id, where a `Map` keyed the value, so a number id and its string share one entry; the slimmer's shared batches are a `SharedBatch` the caller keeps beside the batch, not a `WeakMap` keyed by the array.
  - Measured (13 Nov 2026, this MacBook, shared with two other projects' jobs; the rig's streamed replies of 450, 900 and 1 800 tokens, 462, 912 and 1 812 events, 200 rounds of the same sender calls; `cargo test --release … remote::events::tests::cost -- --ignored`, `OMP_GUI_LINK_EVENTS_COST=1 OMPDESK_EVENTS_COST_ROUNDS=200 bunx vitest run src/main/remote/link-events-core.test.ts`): the sender's work per event, Rust 1.46, 1.56, 1.53 µs against the TypeScript's 0.25, 0.21, 0.21 µs in node; with each tab event's JSON text, Rust 1.66, 1.67, 1.72 µs against 0.32, 0.33, 0.36 µs. The Rust is about five times slower per event: a sample of the encoding thread (`sample`, 4 s) puts 55 % of it in malloc and free and 13 % in memory copies, since each slimmed update is built as new `serde_json` objects (an `IndexMap` and a `String` per key) where V8 copies objects of one hidden class from a bump allocator. At 1 800 events a reply that is 3 ms of the core's time for the work that leaves main's thread (0.6 ms of it there). Not measured: slimming the owned lane events in place at the cutover instead of rebuilding them (inferred to remove most of those allocations).
  - Binary: `ompdesk-core` 7 331 136 → 7 562 896 bytes (darwin-arm64, release; +231 760, the captured rows are test-only). Crates: none new.
  - CI coverage: `native` runs the replay tests on all three OSes; `mac-native` runs `link-events-core.test.ts` (in `.github/actions/native-parity/action.yml`), and the node project runs it on Linux and Windows against their core.
- **R5. Devices** (`crates/core/src/remote/devices/`, `remote/{store,tailnet}.rs`, `crates/core/src/{keepawake,clock}.rs`; was the decisions and state of `src/main/remote/devices.ts`, `RemoteClient`'s reconnect loop in `client.ts`, `RemoteHost`'s rebind, gate and `#bindDevice` in `host-server.ts`, `PeerStore`/`HostStore` of `peers-store.ts`/`hosts-store.ts`, `KeepAwakePolicy`'s rule in `keep-awake.ts` and the caches of `tailscale/tailnet.ts`): batch W2. One devices instance per main (`remote.devices.open`; `sharing.ts` is its client) owns `remote-peers.json` and `remote-hosts.json` (byte for byte as `JSON.stringify(…, null, "\t")` wrote them, a key unset keeping its place, 0600, one copy per file), Tailscale's side (the 15 s poll, the 5 s scan while a view watches, the probes, `isTailnetAddress`, status cached 3 s and whois 60 s up to 256), the host's listener and its rebind with the `listening`/`not-listening`/`rebind-slow` lines, the gate (address, whois, then main's `policy` ask for Mobile web's allowed accounts and blocks), TOFU per node with `key_mismatch` and the `admit` ask, the record's name and last-seen on a link's open and close, each known host's reconnecting client (`devices/client.rs`: the 1 s → 30 s backoff, `wake`, the 1 s stale `peer` step, the 60 s refused window, the `attempt`/`up`/`down` lines as `remote.client.log`), Connect's probe and first contact, the code-paired hosts' migration (main keeps the prefs it reports, `remote.devices.migrated`) and the 30-day forgetting of code-paired devices, and the host's keep-awake (its inputs: sharing, General's switch pushed by main, its own device links, the running turns main pushes). Main mirrors `remote.devices.state` for its synchronous readers (`RemoteDevices.state()`, `allowsPeer`, `reviveHostTab`, the quota), and holds every link the core brings up by its number: `RemoteClient` mirrors `remote.client.state` and carries requests and events, `RemoteHost` answers the `open` ask (the hello, the slim capabilities) and serves the link.
  - What stays TypeScript, and why: `peer-quota.ts` (`reserve` is synchronous in `ws-router.ts` `#weight` and `LocalHostServices`, §1.3 a and c; its remote store is `PeerMirror` in `devices.ts`: the core's records with main's own `limits`/`usage` writes over them until the core says it applied them, `peerPatch` with a sequence, so N reservations at the limit in one tick still let exactly one through); `readopt.ts` (a flow over a link request, `tabs.kept`, and the pool's synchronous `sessionOwner`/`startTab`/`acquire`; no I/O of its own); the Electron blocker of keep-awake (`KeepAwakePolicy` is the core policy's client: it calls `blocker.start`/`stop` on `keepAwake.changed`, and `dispose` releases at once without waiting on the core); `trust-store.ts` and `secure-file.ts` (W1's web stores and `host-services.ts` still read through them; `crypto.ts` keeps `base32` and `randomDeviceId` for `web/devices-store.ts`); everything a link carries (R6, R7).
  - Kept exactly, with tests: rows captured from the TypeScript before it was deleted: `remote/store-captured.json` (276 scenarios, 1 713 steps: each answer and the file's bytes and mode, malformed files and entries, key order on reload, numbers as JavaScript spells them, the 8-address cap, `rememberAddress`), `keepawake-captured.json` (247 sequences of updates and clock steps with each blocker call and `onChange`), `tailnet-captured.json` (71 addresses through `isTailnetAddress` and `isIP`); Rust tests replay them. The parity suites run against the real core with their cases unchanged: `transport.test.ts` (40), `devices.test.ts`, `devices-rename.test.ts`, `crypto.test.ts`, `store.test.ts`, `keep-awake.test.ts`, `tailnet.test.ts`. What moved behind the core's interface: the transport rig's host is a devices instance whose Tailscale is the rig's (`gate.next` decides what whois names and what Mobile web's list and blocks say; `not_tailnet` stops counting loopback); the rig's address that cannot be bound is a tailnet one (`fd7a:115c:a1e0::ffff:ffff`), since the core listens only on tailnet addresses; the stores are read and seeded through `test-stores.ts` (async); fake timers move main's and the core's clock together (`src/main/core/test-clock.ts`, `clock.manual`/`advance`/`real`: a manual clock runs `clock::after` callbacks inside the advance, so what a timer tells main arrives before the advance answers).
  - Differences: `RemoteClient.connect`/`wake` on an offline link tell `connecting` at once, as the loop did when it ran in main, before the core's own `connecting` arrives. `removeHost` answers once the core removed the record (it was synchronous). The host's `open` line's `ms` runs from the core taking the connection in to the hello's ask (it ran to main's answer). The CLI's environment is main's at the first lookup (each run read it again), and computer sharing's CLI runs are not held to a Mobile web step (they never ran inside one). A store file holding `1e999` or a lone surrogate escape reads as empty (serde cannot read it as JSON). The devices tests' Tailscale stays the rig's answers, asked of main (`tailnet` ask); production uses the core's `tailnet.rs` instance.
  - Crates: none. `ompdesk-core` 7 331 136 → 7 794 896 bytes (darwin-arm64 release, 13 Nov 2026).
  - CI coverage: `mac-native` adds `devices-rename.test.ts`, `keep-awake.test.ts` and `store.test.ts` to the remote files it ran; `native` runs the Rust tests on all three OSes.
- **R7. The device side: stays until the pool is in the core (H2)** (`src/main/remote/remote-sidecar.ts`, `host-session-list.ts`, `hosted-view.ts`, `hosted-autopilot.ts`, `quota-commands.ts`, `app-protocol.ts`; batch W2, decided with the batch owner on 13 Nov 2026 from the evidence below): nothing moved, no Rust added. Each file fails `docs/rust-remaining-units.md` §1.3 today:
  - `remote-sidecar.ts` (c): the pool reads the tab's state in the tick its input arrives (`sidecar-pool.ts:1020-1043` reads `statusFromLink` inside the `status` listener; `:642`, `:737`, `:801`, `:992`, `:2084`, `:2689` read `status`/`processAlive`), and its inputs are main's (`devices.ts:926` `linkStateChanged`, `:1040` `handleEvent`, `:582` `relink`). Its parity suite asserts the same: 62 `handleEvent` and 31 `linkStateChanged` calls in `remote-sidecar.test.ts`, 24 of them followed by an `expect` in the same tick (and `sidecar-pool.test.ts` "keeps a paired host's turn and ask through a re-attach"), which a state machine in the core turns into round trips. And the stream decode would grow main's work, measured: a throwaway probe on this branch's base (the real rig of `link-traffic-test-rig.ts`, the device's `tab.event events` frames of one streamed turn, median of 20, this MacBook, 13 Nov 2026) compared main's JSON parse plus `createLinkEventDecoder` today with a JSON parse of the rebuilt batches the core would send through the pipe: 450 tokens 65 frames, 82 KB on the wire against 925 KB rebuilt (11x), main 0.28 → 0.77 ms; 900 tokens 129 frames, 163 KB against 3.3 MB (20x), 0.55 → 1.74 ms; 1 800 tokens 258 frames, 323 KB against 12.4 MB (38x), 0.94 → 4.99 ms. A rebuilt `message_update` carries the whole message, so the pipe's bytes and main's parse grow with the square of the reply (§2.5's reason for R4), and the pool needs those whole events (its per-window slimmer sends a window that attaches mid-message the whole one).
  - `host-session-list.ts` (d): its rules are `src/shared/session-list-delta.ts` (`applySessionListDelta`, `compareSessionRows`, `olderSessionListVersion`, which the page runs in `renderer/stores/session-list.ts:182,222`) and `session-list-share.ts` (`SessionListShare`, also this computer's own list, `session-index.ts:79`, and the host's device list, `host-services.ts:614`), so a core copy would be a second definition; its one I/O is a link request main makes (b); `host-session-list.test.ts` holds the copy's object identity across reads; `formal/session-listing` models its microtask order (F7, `@code` tags on `hostChanged`, `stale`, `#read`).
  - `hosted-view.ts`, `hosted-autopilot.ts`: host-side views over this computer's own sidecars, no I/O of their own (b): `HostedTabView` is held by `host-services.ts:1004,1838` and `sidecar-pool.ts:329`, `hostedAutopilot` by `ipc.ts:1218` over the pool's synchronous `sessionOwner`/`sidecarOf`/`windowOfTab`. They move with R6 and the pool (9d, 9e).
  - `quota-commands.ts` (a, c): pure, called synchronously before a forward by `web/ws-router.ts:873` (`#weight`) and `host-services.ts:2296` (the reservation).
  - `app-protocol.ts`: zod schemas main's link code parses with on both sides; with nothing above moved, no core code parses them, so there is no Rust to hold to zod. The bounds memory sync shares already are (`memsync/wire.rs`, 7e).
  - The next attempt: once the pool's events are in the core (H2), R4's `Decoder` and `ReadDecoder` feed the core's pool in process, so the rebuilt events never cross a pipe; `RemoteSidecar`'s state machine moves with the pool that reads it, and its suite runs against the core's pool there.
  - Baseline for that attempt, on this branch's base (13 Nov 2026, this MacBook, `OMP_GUI_LINK_CPU=1 OMP_GUI_LINK_CPU_ROUNDS=9 bunx vitest run src/main/remote/link-cpu-report.test.ts`, both ends in one process): main's link work 68.9, 66.0, 59.7 µs a frame and main busy 69.2, 92.1, 179.3 ms at 450, 900 and 1 800 tokens (67, 130, 255 frames).
- **R6. Host services: stays until the pool and turn resume are hub instances (9d-9f)** (`src/main/remote/host-services.ts`, `LocalHostServices`; batch W2, decided with the batch owner on 13 Nov 2026 from the evidence below): nothing moved, no Rust added. R6a (the link's encoder and sender cut over onto lane events, R4's Rust called, `createLinkEventSender` deleted) and R6b (the rest) both wait. Per part, by `docs/rust-remaining-units.md` §1.3:
  - Tab registry and lifecycle (`#tabs`, `#open`/`#adopt`/`#dedupe`/`#createTab`/`#closeTab`, grace timers, views) (a, c): read synchronously by `index.ts:1156` (`runningPeers`), `:1191`, `:2115`, `:2476`, `:2494`, `:2497` (`resumeFacts`), `:1200` (`memoryTabs`), `:1277`, `:1687` (`workspaceDirs`), `:2128` (`startTab`), `:2130` (`tabForSession`), `:2499` (`compacting`), `:2502` (`runningBridges`), `ipc.ts:1174` (`sidecarOf`), `ipc.ts:1302` (`checkoutMembers`), `remote/devices.ts:322`, `:391` (`runningPeers`), `:661` (`reviveTab`); the pool hands a view its `HostedTabView` in the same call (`viewForDevice`, `sidecar-pool.ts:329`).
